- **KEEL-209** Ship the plugin: a marketplace entry and a setup command that runs one tested script `p2` · phase10, plugin

  Three commands and a restart. The restart is real and gets said out loud rather than buried: Claude Code connects MCP servers when it starts, and at install time nothing is listening yet because the binary arrives with setup.
  
  `.claude-plugin/marketplace.json` at the repository root pointing at `./plugin`, the way the Firebase plugin does it. The plugin stays in this repository rather than getting its own, so one commit changes both the skills and the behaviour they describe — the drift that TQ-26 was about, removed by construction.
  
  **`/keel:setup` runs exactly one script.** A slash command is a prompt, so anything written as "then run this, then run that" gets executed non-deterministically and asks permission at every step. The command's whole job is to run `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}/scripts/setup.sh`; the determinism lives in the script, where it can be tested.
  
  The script downloads and verifies the binaries, creates the store, resolves a port collision if there is one and writes the result into both the service unit and the plugin config, installs the service, starts the daemon, and asks once about embeddings and once about automatic updates.
  
  The MCP entry keeps its HTTP transport pointing at the daemon — that is what preserves the single writer, since a stdio server would be one process per session fighting over the same store.
  
  `curl … | sh` stays as the path for anyone not using Claude Code.

  - Two things settled with KB on 2026-08-14, both of which change how this task should be finished.  **The marketplace entry publishes nothing to Anthropic.** KB asked directly, and the answer is no: `.claude-plugin/marketplace.json` makes this repository a marketplace someone adds by name with `/plugin marketplace add kiritbasu/keel`. There is no central listing, no submission step and no index. The repository being public is the entire exposure, and it reaches only somebody who already has the URL. So building this task's deliverable does not make Keel discoverable to anyone.  **Nothing ships until it has been tested.** In KB's words:  > i don't want it out there till i actually test everything  The repository going public is not a release. Adding the marketplace entry is not a release. The first tag is, and §12 tier 1 has never been run against a real installer by anyone. So tier 1 comes before the tag, not after — which puts KEEL-210 ahead of finishing this. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-15</sub>


### todo (20)

- **KEEL-217** A task can be created directly in a terminal status, skipping the rule that guards closing one `p2` · mcp, storage

  Hit by doing it: KEEL-216 was created with `fields: {"status": "done"}` and landed as `status: done`, `close_reason: null`, `close_message: null`, `evidence: []`, `closed_at: null`.
  
  The standing contract says this is impossible: "A task cannot reach a terminal status without a reason, a message and — for `done` — evidence: the check is in the storage layer, so the CLI and MCP cannot disagree and moving the status by hand does not get round it."
  
  It is true of the path it guards. `update` computes `terminal` and calls `task.validate_close()` on the transition, and there is a comment explaining why it is on the transition and not on every write — a hundred and seven tasks predate the rule and checking always would freeze them. That reasoning is right. It just leaves the create path open, where there is no previous status to transition from.
  
  The consequence is a row that reads as finished and says nothing about why, which is precisely what the rule exists to prevent. It also has a null `closed_at`, so it is invisible to anything asking what was closed and when — including, probably, the changelog and any "what shipped this week" query.
  
  Two candidate fixes:
  
  1. **Refuse a create into a terminal status.** A task nobody ever opened did not happen, and back-filling history is what `keel import` is for. Simplest, and it makes the invariant structural.
  2. **Hold it to the same rule** — require reason, message and evidence when a create names a terminal status, and stamp `closed_at`. More permissive, and it keeps the door open for adopting a finished backlog.
  
  Recommending 1, with 2 as the answer if adoption turns out to need it. Either way `closed_at` must be stamped wherever a row reaches a terminal status, because a closed task with no closing time is a hole in the one log that is meant to be complete.
  
  Worth checking whether the same gap exists for the other statuses that have side effects, and whether `keel_create` should be refusing a `claimed_by` too.

- **KEEL-214** Twelve places the code could be wrong and no test would notice `p2` · testing, hardening

  The raw list, from `mutants.out/missed.txt`:
  
  ```
  id.rs:51:42   replace < with ==     in ensure_above
  id.rs:51:42   replace < with <=     in ensure_above
  id.rs:55:96   replace + with *      in ensure_above
  id.rs:79:5    minted_at             -> None
  id.rs:261:9   into_string           -> String::new()
  id.rs:261:9   into_string           -> "xyzzy".into()
  id.rs:273:9   as_ref                -> ""
  id.rs:273:9   as_ref                -> "xyzzy"
  next.rs:80:9  NextUp::is_empty      -> true
  next.rs:80:31 replace && with ||    in NextUp::is_empty
  next.rs:80:65 replace && with ||    in NextUp::is_empty
  next.rs:191:9 delete match arm Some(Entity::Project(p)) in rank
  ```
  
  They are not equally interesting, and triaging them is most of the work.
  
  **`next.rs` is the sharp end.** This is the ready/blocked ranking — what `keel_ready` answers when a session asks what to pick up. `is_empty` returning `true` unconditionally, or either `&&` flipping to `||`, means the digest says there is nothing to work on when there is. That is this project's defining failure shape: a plausible answer, no error, and nobody notices. The deleted match arm in `rank` is the same family.
  
  **`ensure_above` matters more than it looks.** It is the monotonic-id guard, and `<` becoming `<=` or `==` is exactly the off-by-one that lets two ids collide or lets the clock-step check pass when it should not. There is already a note in the store about the newest stored id being compared against the machine's clock; this is that comparison.
  
  **Four are probably noise and should be closed as such rather than silently skipped.** `into_string` and `as_ref` returning `""` or `"xyzzy"` are the standard cargo-mutants stubs for accessors. If nothing asserts on a formatted id, that may be fine — but say so, because "we looked and it does not matter" and "we never looked" are the same colour on a report.
  
  **Also worth knowing: 24 mutants timed out**, all in `next.rs` and `ready`. A timeout is not a pass. It means the suite hung with that mutation rather than failing, so those 24 are unknowns wearing a different label — `next.rs:443 replace != with ==` and `next.rs:452 replace > with >=` among them. The headline "59 caught of 113" is softer than it sounds.
  
  The full evidence is in `mutants.out/` locally, which is gitignored. To regenerate after this is fixed, without the weekly job:
  
  ```
  cargo mutants --no-shuffle -j 2 --file crates/keel-core/src/next.rs --file crates/keel-core/src/id.rs
  ```
  
  It took two hours and thirteen minutes for four files, so scope it to what changed.

- **KEEL-212** Decide what happens to Intel Mac builds before the runners disappear in August 2027 `p3` · phase10, release

  Filed now so it is a decision with a date rather than a surprise. Nothing to do yet.
  
  The dependency is not the compiler, it is the signature: a macOS binary cross-compiled from Linux carries no ad-hoc signature and is killed at exec on Apple Silicon. Intel Macs are less strict, so the honest options when the runners go are an explicit signing step in the cross-compile, or dropping `x86_64-apple-darwin` and saying so.
  
  Whichever it is, decide before the runners vanish rather than during the release that discovers it.

- **KEEL-211** Put the embedding model somewhere sensible and ask before downloading it `p3` · phase10, search

  Set the cache path explicitly to `~/.keel/models` rather than accepting fastembed's default.
  
  Opt-in behind a visible prompt in setup, never a silent pull. Keyword search works without any of it, which is what makes opt-in honest rather than a downgrade — the offer is "better search later" and not "search is broken until you agree".
  
  The quantized variant is 66 MB against 133 MB and is worth preferring.
  
  Nothing about this adds a build step: the ONNX Runtime is fetched prebuilt and statically linked at our build time, and `ort-sys` already pulls a 77 MB archive on every build regardless.
  
  Deferred because keyword search already works and nobody is blocked on it.

- **KEEL-207** Serve the read surface from the daemon instead of a dev server `p2` · phase10, ui

  **Blocked, and deliberately so.** The open question about whether a browser-served write or intake endpoint needs hard constraint 7 amended has to be answered before this starts, not discovered halfway through. A read-only unauthenticated API on loopback is defensible; the moment an intake endpoint exists it is not.
  
  The React app already exists, already talks to `/api`, and already builds to `apps/desktop/dist`. Compile that build into `keel-daemon` with `rust-embed` and serve it from the axum server already there. `keel ui` opens it.
  
  This is what replaces the Tauri shell rather than delaying it. A browser-downloaded `.dmg` is quarantined and then needs Developer ID signing and notarization — $99 a year forever, with a signing pipeline to maintain and no free workaround since Sequoia removed the Control-click bypass. Tauri also wants Node, Xcode tools, WebView2 and webkit2gtk and does not meaningfully cross-compile. Serving from a local daemon is what Jupyter, Syncthing, Grafana, Meilisearch, Qdrant and pgAdmin all do.
  
  `keel ui` opens the site with a per-session token, since anything on loopback is reachable by every other process on the machine.
  
  One consequence for the release job: embedding the site puts Node in the build, which the generated workflow will not do by itself.

- **KEEL-206** Move the hooks into the binary, leaving one shim that can report the binary is missing `p2` · phase10, plugin

  `keel hook session-start` and `keel hook stop`.
  
  Four reasons, and the fourth is the one that matters most here. python3 is absent on a clean macOS until the Xcode command line tools arrive. `install.sh` warns about a missing `jq`, which neither script uses, so the only dependency check that exists is checking the wrong thing. Bash means these could never run on Windows. And **nothing anywhere executes them**: KEEL-192 was found by reading and fixed by reading, and the fix is guarded by nothing. Everything else in this phase is about surfaces that describe themselves and are tested; the hooks are the one surface that does neither.
  
  **One shim has to stay**, and pretending otherwise would leave a hole. The install flow wants the session to say "the binary is missing, run `/keel:setup`" — and a hook that *is* the binary cannot report its own absence. So `hooks/hooks.json` keeps pointing at a plugin-root script whose whole body is:
  
  ```sh
  [ -x "$KEEL_BIN" ] && exec "$KEEL_BIN" hook session-start
  printf '%s' '{"hookSpecificOutput":{…"Keel is installed but not set up — run /keel:setup."}}'
  ```
  
  Ten lines that never change and hold no logic worth testing, with everything that can change on the other side of the `exec`.
  
  Carry KEEL-192's fix across: the Stop hook resolves `cwd` to a project before deciding whether to speak, so it stays quiet in repositories Keel knows nothing about. That behaviour currently has no test, and this is where it gets one.

- **KEEL-204** Tell people the update check phones home, and let them turn it off `p2` · phase10, updates, privacy

  Disclosed rather than discovered. The difference matters more for this product than for most: the pitch is that your project's history lives on your machine, and an undisclosed outbound request undermines that claim whether or not it carries anything.
  
  `/keel:setup` asks once, in plain words, and no is a real answer. `KEEL_AUTO_UPDATE=0` turns it off afterwards. `keel doctor` reports whether it is on and when it last ran, so the answer to "is this thing calling out?" is a command rather than a reading of the source.
  
  Nothing about the check sends anything from the store. Worth stating in the prompt, because the honest answer to the worry is narrower and better than a reassurance.

- **KEEL-203** Update the binary without asking, but only when it cannot touch the store's shape `p2` · phase10, updates

  The split is on schema version, not on how the update arrives. Same schema and no breaking entry: apply it. Different schema: stop, name what changes, point at the command that backs up first and waits.
  
  **It never runs in the hook.** The session-start hook goes before the user's first word and must never block a session; that constraint does not bend for this. The daemon checks the manifest daily, verifies checksum and build attestation, and stages the new binary beside the current one. At its next startup it finds the staged binary, renames it over itself and re-execs — so nothing ever swaps a binary that is running, and the restart comes free from launchd `KeepAlive` or systemd `Restart=always`.
  
  Verification is not optional and never degrades. A failed checksum or attestation means staying put and saying so, never falling back to unverified.
  
  **Rollback.** Keep the previous binary as `keel.previous`; `keel update --rollback` puts it back. This is the difference between one bad release being an inconvenience and being the end of somebody's trust. A migrated store cannot be un-migrated, which is exactly why schema changes never auto-apply — rollback covers the case automatic updates create and does not pretend to cover the other one.
  
  `keel update` forces a check now.

- **KEEL-200** Keep a store from every schema version, and prove each one still migrates forward `p2` · phase10, contracts, data-safety

  Four stores today, one per schema version rather than one per release, because most releases do not touch the tables. A fixture store is 700 KB, so the whole corpus is under 3 MB.
  
  This is the only artifact in the phase that cannot be reconstructed later. A store can only be written by the code that wrote it, so every version that goes by without one captured is a version that can never be tested against again. That makes starting now worth more than starting well.
  
  **Seed from a scrubbed copy of the real store, not from `keel fixture`.** The synthetic corpus has the shapes we thought to generate; the real one is 7.2 MB and has archived rows, retracted notes, superseded decisions, blobs, a DuckDB-era history and five months of accidents. A migration that breaks on a null the fixture never leaves null passes green and then eats data in the field.
  
  Before trusting any of it, diff the fixture's table-shape coverage against the real store's and treat every gap as a case the fixture cannot speak for. Scrubbing needs deciding too — what has to come out before a store can sit in a public repository.
  
  The test asserts row counts per table and content hashes per document, and runs `fsck` afterwards. Assert equality; do not eyeball it.

- **KEEL-187** Drop documents.embedding, and take the dead trigger clause with it `p2` · storage, cleanup

  B-55 moved vectors to `document_chunks` and said `documents.embedding` stays "until a later migration takes it". This is that migration, and there is a second thing to do in the same one.
  
  **The column.** Nothing writes it — the only remaining assignment is in a test fixture — and nothing reads it. `search_semantic` reads passages. It is dead weight in every row of the largest table.
  
  **The trigger clause.** `prose_archive_triggers` still ends with:
  
  ```sql
  UPDATE documents SET embedding = NULL, embedding_model = NULL
    WHERE entity_id = new.id;
  ```
  
  That runs on every archive and blanks a column that is already blank. Harmless today, and it is the reason to do these together: a trigger referring to a dropped column fails when it *runs*, not when anything compiles. The symptom would be archiving suddenly erroring, some time after the migration that caused it, with nothing connecting the two. Triggers are the worst place to leave dead code because nobody reads them.
  
  Flagged as a required fix in review and then reconsidered, honestly: on its own it needs a migration to drop and recreate five triggers, which is the same migration this task needs anyway. Two migrations for one cleanup is worse than one, and the clause cannot bite until the column goes.
  
  Worth being careful about:
  
  - SQLite cannot drop a column that an index or trigger references, and older versions cannot `DROP COLUMN` at all. Check what the bundled amalgamation supports before assuming; the fallback is the twelve-step table rebuild, which is a different size of job.
  - The recreated triggers must keep the two things that are *not* dead — removing the row from `fts_source`, and deleting its passages. Losing either resurrects KEEL-175.
  - `a_restored_document_keeps_its_embedding` and anything else asserting on that column will need to move or go.
  - Verify on a copy of the live store, and check archiving still works afterwards rather than only that the migration applied.

- **KEEL-186** A running daemon blocks writes to stores it is not serving `p2` · cli, daemon

  Hit while setting up a scratch store to verify something unrelated:
  
  ```
  keel --home /tmp/scratch fixture
  Error: a daemon is running at http://127.0.0.1:7654, and it owns the single write path.
  ```
  
  It does not own that one. It has never opened it. The store in `/tmp/scratch` did not exist until a moment earlier.
  
  `refuse_if_daemon_is_running` asks one question — is anything listening on the daemon URL — and that question is not about the store being written to. It was the only question available before B-60, when the whole mechanism was a TCP probe. It is not the only one available now: the advisory lock is per-store and answers exactly the right thing, because a daemon serving `~/.keel` does not hold `/tmp/scratch/keel.sqlite.lock`.
  
  So the probe and the lock now disagree about scope, and the probe is the over-broad one. The fix is probably to let the lock be the authority for "is this store busy" and keep the probe for what it is actually good at — telling someone that a daemon exists and they probably meant to go through it.
  
  Worth being careful about one case: when the daemon *does* hold the store, the probe's error is much better than the lock's, because it names the daemon and says to ask it instead. Losing that message would be a downgrade. The likely shape is: probe first for the message, then let the lock decide.
  
  Cost of leaving it: every scratch store, every test fixture and every second project has to pass `--force` or unset `KEEL_DAEMON_URL`, which teaches people to use `--force` reflexively. That is the habit the flag most needs not to become.

- **KEEL-184** Generated files and stored bodies differ by a trailing newline, so re-importing one is not a no-op `p2` · cli, storage

  Found by `keel import --dry-run` the first time it was pointed at real files, which is a decent argument for the flag.
  
  ```
  revise     product/SPEC.md  →  Keel — Technical Specification
  unchanged  product/PRD.md   →  Keel — Product Requirements Document
  unchanged  product/JOURNAL.md → Keel — Build journal
  ```
  
  `keel generate keel --check` says SPEC.md is current, so the store-to-file direction is fine. The difference is one byte in the other direction:
  
  ```
  store bytes : 68690    tail: 'rDB/falkordb-rs-next-gen)'
  disk  bytes : 68691    tail: 'DB/falkordb-rs-next-gen)\n'
  ```
  
  The stored body has no trailing newline and the generated file does. `body_hash` covers title and body, so that one byte is a different hash and the import would write a revision that changes nothing anyone can read.
  
  It is per-document rather than systemic: PRD and JOURNAL round-trip clean, so their stored bodies already end with a newline. SPEC's does not, presumably from however it was first written.
  
  This contradicts what `import`'s own module doc promises — "re-importing an unchanged file does nothing at all — so it is safe to re-run during the migration". For a generated file that is not reliably true, and the failure is invisible: an extra revision looks like an edit somebody made.
  
  Two ways to fix it and they are not equivalent. Normalising on the way *in* — ensuring a stored body ends with exactly one newline — fixes it for every document and quietly rewrites one existing body. Writing the body verbatim on the way *out* leaves the file without a trailing newline, which most tools and every `diff` will complain about. The first is probably right, but it changes stored content, so it wants deciding rather than assuming.
  
  Worth checking whether the same gap exists in `keel_write_doc` over MCP, since that is the path that writes most bodies now.

  - The scope is bigger than the one document that surfaced it. **105 of the live current documents have a body with no trailing newline**, so the round trip is one-byte-lossy for most of the store, not for SPEC alone.  SPEC.md itself is now clean, incidentally rather than deliberately: annotating §13 for TQ-37 meant importing the file, and the imported body carried the trailing newline the generated file has. Re-importing it now reports `unchanged`. That is one document out of 105 and it does not close this.  It also sharpens which fix is right. Normalising on the way in would rewrite 105 stored bodies — a revision each, all of them differing by one invisible byte, which is a lot of history for nothing anyone can read. Worth considering a third option the task did not list: normalise on *comparison* rather than on storage, so `body_hash` is computed over a body with trailing whitespace trimmed. That makes the round trip a no-op without rewriting anything, at the cost of two documents differing only in trailing whitespace being treated as the same document — which, for markdown, they are.  Still worth checking `keel_write_doc`: if it stores exactly what a model sends, and models rarely end a body with a newline, that is where the 105 came from. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>
