- **KEEL-171** A question or decision can be created with no prose in it at all `p2` · bug, mcp

  `dispatch.rs:1099` is `if let Some(text) = body.filter(|_| entity_type.has_document())`. Body is optional, so a create with no body writes the row, skips the document, and reports success.
  
  For a task that is survivable — 8G makes `summary` required, and the row still says something. For a question or a decision there is no summary column at all. The document is the entire content.
  
  Three rows are in this state right now, all from `ses_3f1a60af` on 2026-08-12, each with exactly one event and no document ever written:
  
  - `que_01KZSQHFQ454` — Enforce the single-writer rule with an advisory lock file, or rely on the health probe alone?
  - `que_01KZSQHK2C0C` — Does a browser-served write/intake endpoint require amending hard constraint 7?
  - `dec_01KZSQJ05N4T` — The write-path atomicity fix: &Connection primitives, transaction-of-one, one typed composite on Store
  
  The titles are good. Each names a real decision, and a decision row that records what was chosen and nothing about why is the one shape the decision log exists to prevent.
  
  There is a second route to the same state, and it is worth fixing together because it is silent: the style check runs *after* the row is created, at `dispatch.rs:1104`. A refused body therefore leaves the row and loses the prose, and the caller sees an error that does not say a row landed. That happened while filing the question about this — the entity was created at 01:06:48, the body was refused, and the document only arrived at 01:07:09 on a retry that the idempotency key turned into an attach rather than a duplicate. Without the retry it would have stayed headless. KEEL-146 is the transactional fix and this is a live reproduction of it.
  
  Two parts, then:
  
  1. Make `body` required for the types whose content it is. That changes the MCP tool schema, so it needs KB's agreement — but it is 8G's argument applied to the types where the omission costs more.
  2. Until then, at minimum the refusal must not leave the row behind (KEEL-146).

- **KEEL-168** Security prerequisites before any browser-served UI or write endpoint `p3`

  - One piece of this shipped early, in cd00558, because it is not hypothetical: the desktop app fetches blobs from the daemon today, so `/api/blob/{id}` now carries `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` and `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; sandbox`. The SVG case is the reason — an SVG is a document that may contain `<script>` and is served with an image media type, so a diagram an agent wrote while reading prose it did not write is stored XSS the moment something renders it as a document.  Two more of the listed defenses also landed under other tasks rather than here: `Origin: null` is refused (KEEL-158), which was the null-origin gap this task names, and the origin check now runs before the rate limiter.  What is still genuinely gated on KB amending hard constraint 7: the per-session token from a 0600 file on mutating endpoints, allowlisting blob and image read roots, and the advisory lock that stops a browser write path becoming a second writer. None of those should be built before there is a decision that a write endpoint exists at all — half of an auth scheme is worse than none, because it looks like one. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-12</sub>

- **KEEL-137** The daemonless fallback creates an empty store instead of saying there isn't one `p3` · bug, cli

  Found while fixing KEEL-133. `directly()` in `crates/keel-cli/src/work.rs` calls `Store::open`, which creates and migrates when the file is absent. Running `keel ready keel` against the default home on a machine whose store is still `keel.duckdb` left an empty `~/.keel/keel.sqlite` behind, and then reported:
  
  ```
  Error: argument `project`: no project matches `keel`. Expected: one of: . Call keel_projects to list them
  ```
  
  The empty `one of: ` is the tell. Nothing is wrong with the project name; there is no store.
  
  Creating on demand is right for `keel bootstrap` and `keel fixture`, which are asked to make one. It is wrong for a read that fell back.

- **KEEL-136** The pre-commit check reads the wrong tree from a git worktree `p2` · tooling

  Hit while committing KEEL-132 from `.claude/worktrees/competent-mayer-aa62e7`.
  
  `scripts/pre-commit` runs `keel generate "$project" --check` with no `--repo`, so the check resolves the project's recorded `root_path` — the main checkout. From a worktree the files being compared are not the files being committed.
  
  Both directions are wrong, and the second is the one that matters:
  
  - **False alarm.** Regenerating in the worktree left it clean, and the hook still reported `stale product/SPEC.md` because the main checkout has the old file. The commit went through with `--no-verify` after verifying by hand with `keel generate keel --repo "$PWD" --check`.
  - **False pass.** The same lookup means a genuinely hand-edited file in a worktree is compared against the main checkout's copy. If the main checkout happens to be current, the check passes and the edit is committed — which is the exact failure the hook was written to replace, since the mechanism it replaced was deleted for silently doing nothing.
  
  The fix is one flag: pass `--repo "$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)"`, which is the tree the commit is in and equals `root_path` when there is no worktree. The header comment already argues at length that a check which cries wolf gets bypassed with `--no-verify`, and that this is the same disease as a hook that quietly does nothing. This is that, from a direction it did not anticipate.

  - The write side has the same bug, and it is worse than a false alarm. `keel generate` with no `--repo` also resolves `root_path`, so running it from a worktree writes into the main checkout — the files land as uncommitted changes on whatever branch that checkout has out, and `git status` in the worktree you ran it from says clean.  That happened here at 23:47, closing KEEL-133 from a worktree: five files written into the main checkout on `phase-9-one-database`, including the 649-line `product/SPEC.md` from your KEEL-132 work, which was in the store but not in that checkout. Reverted by hand with `git checkout --` plus an `rm` of the new decision file. Nothing was lost, because every one of them is a render of the store — but a session that did not think to look in the other tree would have left them there, and the next person to `git status` the main checkout would find a stranger's diff.  So `--repo "$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)"` fixes the hook, and the same defaulting belongs in `generate` itself: when the command is run inside the project's repository, the tree you are in is the tree you mean. `--show-toplevel` gives the worktree root, which is the wanted answer in both cases.  KEEL-138 said this separately and is closed as a duplicate of this row.  *(Replaces a retracted note with identical content. The original spelled out an absolute path carrying the machine's username, which does not belong in a repository that is going public.)* <sub>— claude · 2026-08-15</sub>

- **KEEL-135** SPEC §6 still says nine MCP tools and lists nine `p3` · docs

  Left deliberately untouched by KEEL-132, which was scoped to the storage engine. This is a different kind of staleness in the same document and it should not ride along on an unrelated change.
  
  §6.1 opens with "**Few tools, rich arguments.** Nine tools, not forty CRUD endpoints. Models choose correctly among nine." §6.2's table lists those nine. The daemon serves thirteen: `keel_note`, `keel_ready`, `keel_claim` and `keel_close` are missing. §12's Phase 1 row also says "9 MCP tools".
  
  The interesting part is not the count. The ceiling was nine, went to ten when `keel_note` earned a slot, and to thirteen when the three work verbs did under TQ-31 — and each rise needed KB's agreement and an argument at least as good as the last one. That progression is recorded in the doc comment on `tools::all()` and in the standing contract, but the spec still reads as though nine were a designed limit rather than the first of four positions. Whoever fixes this should carry the argument across, not only the rows.

- **KEEL-96** Pruning does not cover adopted files under product/ `p3`

  `keel generate` prunes orphaned files under `.keel/` using the previous manifest (KEEL-96 / TQ-28), but adopted prose files in `product/` are not covered.
  
  An adopted file orphans when its artifact is archived — `product/QUESTIONS.md` and `product/DECISIONS.md` both did on 2026-08-10 and were removed by hand with `git rm`.
  
  Why it was left: deleting inside `product/` is a larger act than deleting inside `.keel/`. A mirror file is Keel's own bookkeeping; an adopted file is a document someone may have 50 KB of prose in. The manifest does not currently record adopted paths, so the safe set is not even known.
  
  What it needs: record adopted paths in the manifest, then prune them on the same rule. Worth pairing with a louder confirmation than mirror pruning gets — or reporting rather than deleting, which is the asymmetry TQ-28 already accepts for `--check`.


### in_progress (2)

- **KEEL-210** Build the two release checks that can actually be run on the hardware we have `p2` · phase10, release, verification

  Two tiers, because a third needs hardware.
  
  **Tier 1 — this machine, clean environment.** Run the installer under `env -i HOME=/tmp/keel-clean PATH=/usr/bin:/bin` so there is no cargo on the path and no existing store. Covers the installer end to end, the checksum refusal path by corrupting an archive on purpose, a binary running with no toolchain, store creation, daemon start, MCP answering, the plugin installed into a virgin `~/.claude`, and the quarantine claim proved directly with `xattr -l` on a fresh download. About fifteen minutes, scriptable, every release. Blind to Linux, to a Mac without the Xcode tools, and to the cross-compile signature trap.
  
  **Tier 2 — a Linux VM in UTM, snapshot-restored, run by hand.** Adds the Linux binary actually running, glibc compatibility, the installer on a non-Mac, the systemd unit installing and restarting after a kill, and "nothing compiles" on a machine that has genuinely never had Rust. **The highest value per unit of effort in the phase**, because it covers the one platform CI has never touched. An hour of setup, then ten minutes a release.
  
  A third tier — a clean Mac — would add ad-hoc signature behaviour in the wild and the multi-machine story. A macOS VM in UTM on Apple Silicon may cover most of it without buying anything, and is worth trying before deciding it needs hardware.
  
  This replaces an exit criterion that asked for "a Mac that has never had Rust installed", which cannot be met here and would have joined the two phase gates that need a human.

  - Tier 1 has now run, and running it found three things that reading it could not.  **The release would have failed at the build step.** `dist build` stops with `profile 'dist' is not defined` — the tool builds artifacts with a `dist` cargo profile and the workspace never declared one. Nothing in `dist plan`, `dist generate --check` or any amount of reading the configuration surfaces this, because it only happens when something actually compiles. Added as `[profile.dist] inherits = "release"` and nothing else, so the artifact users get is built exactly the way a local release build is.  **Tier 1 left a daemon running on the scratch port, serving a store it had just deleted.** `clean_run` is a shell function, and backgrounding a function forks a subshell — so `$!` was the subshell's pid, killing it killed the subshell, and the daemon was orphaned. The check reported "daemon stops on TERM: still alive or still answering", which reads as a daemon bug and is not one: driven directly, the daemon exits in about two seconds. The script's own cleanup comment says a stray daemon is the one way it could leave the machine worse than it found it. It was right, and that line was how. Fixed by calling `env` directly, since `env` execs rather than forking, plus a `pgrep` backstop matched on the scratch path.  **The sha256sum check warned about its own fix.** It grepped for the string `sha256sum`, and the patched installer still contains it — it tries `sha256sum` first and falls back, which is correct. It now looks for the skip message instead, which is the thing that actually indicates the bug.  One design change worth stating plainly, because it changes what the gate is for. Tier 1 used to install from the published release, so it could only ever run *after* a tag — confirming what had already shipped, which is the wrong way round for something meant to stop a bad release. It now installs from artifacts sitting beside the installer when there are any, through the generator's own `INSTALLER_DOWNLOAD_URL`, and falls back to the release when there are none. The mode is printed and named in every line that depends on it, because "installer completes" means two different things in the two modes.  Result on this Mac, against locally built `aarch64-apple-darwin` artifacts: **14 passed, 0 failed, 0 warnings.** That includes the two claims Phase 10 rests on — a `curl` download carries no quarantine attribute, and the installer refuses a corrupted archive on the checksum rather than skipping the check. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-15</sub>
  - Tier 2 is written — `scripts/verify-release-tier2.sh` — and **has never been run**, which is the honest state and should not be read as anything else.  It cannot be run from here. There is no Linux binary this Mac can execute: the only Linux target is `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`, Docker on Apple Silicon gives arm64 Linux, and Linux ARM is deliberately not a target because there is no prebuilt ONNX Runtime for it. So the script needs the VM the task asks for, and provisioning that is KB's — it needs an ISO, a GUI installer and a snapshot.  What it covers beyond tier 1, in the order it runs: the machine has genuinely never had a toolchain, checked where one installs rather than by stripping a PATH; the installer on a machine that is not a Mac; **the glibc floor**, read out of the ELF with `objdump` and compared against 2.35, which is what `ubuntu-22.04` gives and is the reason the release builds Linux there; store creation, daemon start and stop; a systemd *user* unit surviving `SIGKILL`; and the corruption refusal again, because Linux exercises the other branch of the patched installer entirely — coreutils has `sha256sum` and often no `shasum` at all.  It refuses to run on macOS rather than degrading into a worse copy of tier 1, and it fails closed on anything it cannot answer. Two of those refusals are worth knowing before it is first run, because they will fire on the wrong kind of machine: a host with Rust installed fails the toolchain check, and a session with no user systemd bus fails the service check. Both are correct — running it on a GitHub Linux runner would go red on exactly those two, which is why it is not wired into CI as a gate. A check that reports green on a machine that could not answer it is the thing this whole tier exists to avoid. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-15</sub>

- **KEEL-208** Build releases for three targets, and fix the installer's silent checksum skip `p2` · phase10, release

  `dist` at 0.32. Targets are `aarch64-apple-darwin`, `x86_64-apple-darwin` and `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`. Windows is not a target — it had no CI coverage and the hooks cannot run there, so shipping it would have meant a binary nobody had run.
  
  **macOS builds must happen on macOS runners.** On Apple Silicon every executable needs at least an ad-hoc signature or it is killed at exec, and native toolchains add one at link time while a cross-compile from Linux does not. It fails in a way that looks like a corrupt download. Runners are free for public repositories.
  
  **Node goes in the release job**, for the embedded site. The generated workflow will not do this by itself.
  
  **The bug to patch on day one.** The installer `dist` generates verifies downloads by calling `sha256sum`, which stock macOS does not have — it has `shasum`. The script prints "skipping sha256 checksum verification" and returns success, so on the primary platform integrity checking silently does nothing. Fall back to `shasum -a 256`, and send the fix upstream.
  
  Checksums embedded in the installer itself, artifact attestations on so build provenance can be verified — the updater depends on both.

  - `dist` ignores keys it does not recognise, and says nothing. A junk key appended to `dist-workspace.toml` produced a clean `dist plan` with no warning at all.  That matters because every "UNCERTAIN" note in that file was written on the theory that a wrong key would be caught by running the tool. It would not have been. All four names were checked against the struct fields they deserialise into instead — `cargo_dist_version`, `allow_dirty`, `github_attestations`, and the per-package `dist` — and the one with observable behaviour was checked by breaking it: with `allow-dirty = []`, `dist generate --check` fails with a 300-line diff proposing to overwrite the hand-written release workflow with a generated one that has no Node steps. With `["ci"]` it is silent.  So the config is right, but not for the reason the file gave. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>
  - Where this stands: the pipeline is built and every part of it that can be checked without pushing a tag has been.  `dist plan` now announces one app — `keel` 0.1.0 — with `keel-installer.sh` and three archives, each carrying both binaries plus a per-archive sha256. `dist generate --check` is silent, so the hand-written workflow is protected. `dist build --artifacts=global` produced a real 50 KB installer, which is what the checksum bug was found and fixed against.  What is left is the one thing that cannot be rehearsed: a pushed tag, and a CI run that builds on two macOS runners and one Linux runner. That needs the repository to be public, or a deliberate decision to spend Actions minutes on a private one — macOS is billed at ten times the rate there and free on public repos, which is the reason PHASE-10 §2 gave for choosing macOS runners at all. B-69 left the repository private "while the install path is built against how public repos work", and the install path is now built. Raised as a question rather than assumed.  `cargo-dist` 0.32.0 was installed from crates.io rather than through the upstream shell installer, which is the same script with the same skipped checksum. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>
  - Landed as `c5d58af`. Clippy, fmt and the whole workspace suite are green; `dist plan` and `dist generate --check` both clean.  One thing left undone on purpose: PHASE-10 §10 still says stock macOS has no `sha256sum`, which is now known to be half wrong. The correction is in B-71, in `dist-workspace.toml`'s comments and in the test that pins the behaviour, but the spec body itself is unamended — changing it means resending the whole document through `keel_write_doc`, and a 400-line rewrite to correct one clause is the kind of edit that quietly changes other things. Worth doing deliberately rather than as a side effect of this task. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>
  - The release plan changed under this task after it was written, and the change is B-72: the repository stays private and both macOS targets build on a self-hosted runner instead of hosted ones.  What that alters here is the runner matrix and nothing else. `dist-workspace.toml` is untouched, the three targets are the same three, the installer is still one `keel-installer.sh` carrying both binaries, and the checksum patch runs in the same place. `release.yml` now asks for `[self-hosted, macOS, ARM64]` for both Apple targets and keeps hosted `ubuntu-22.04` for Linux. CI's macOS leg moved with it, since that runs on every push and is where the minutes would actually have gone.  Three things fell out of a persistent runner that a throwaway VM never needed, and all three are in the workflow rather than in somebody's memory:  - `target/distrib` is cleared before each build, because the working directory survives between jobs and `upload-artifact` globs that directory. Without it the second macOS job would upload the first one's archives. - `Swatinem/rust-cache` is skipped when the runner is self-hosted. It already has `target/`; restoring a cache over the top spends GitHub's storage on gigabytes that are on disk. - `dist` is installed with `cargo install --locked` there rather than by piping the upstream shell script into `sh`. On a throwaway VM that pipe costs nothing; on somebody's actual Mac it is a different proposition, the more so because that script is the one whose checksum verification this repository patches.  `scripts/install-runner.sh` registers the machine. The registration token is minted through `gh` at run time and passed straight to `config.sh`, never written to a file.  Still true, and still the whole of what is left: no tag has been pushed and no release exists. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-15</sub>


### done (189)

- **KEEL-218** Two tests read the machine instead of the code, and the first real CI run found both `p2` · testing, phase10

  Both were latent before this session and both went green locally, which is the point worth keeping: the Linux leg of CI had never actually run until now, so nothing had ever disagreed with the Mac.
  
  **`cli.txt` recorded the environment.** `clap` renders an env-backed argument as `[env: KEEL_HOME=<the value right now>]` — the current value, not just the name — and `help_for` spawned the binary with the environment inherited. So the recorded CLI contract depended on what the person running the test happened to have exported. Anyone with `KEEL_HOME`, `KEEL_BIND` or `KEEL_DAEMON_URL` set would have failed it, with a diff that read as a CLI change.
  
  It became certain rather than likely when CI started setting `KEEL_HOME` to a scratch directory to keep the suite away from the real store. Every leg went red on `cli.txt`, on both platforms.
  
  Fixed by clearing the environment for the help invocation and putting only `PATH` back. Worth noting the committed `cli.txt` needed no re-recording — the file was right and the test was wrong, which is the good outcome and would not have been obvious from the failure.
  
  **The installer test assumed a Mac.** `the_unpatched_installer_waves_a_corrupted_file_through` ran under `PATH=/usr/bin:/bin` and asserted the unpatched script skips verification. True on macOS, where `sha256sum` lives in `/sbin` and `/sbin` is not on that path. False on Linux, where coreutils puts `sha256sum` in `/usr/bin` — so the unpatched script verified correctly and refused the corrupted file, and the test failed for the best possible reason.
  
  Fixed by building the path the test needs rather than borrowing the platform's: a scratch directory holding symlinks to `awk` and nothing else. It now asserts the behaviour on both platforms instead of asserting which runner it landed on.
  
  A sixth test came out of it, covering the branch the patch adds that upstream does not have: given the correct digest and no way to compute it, the patched installer refuses rather than passing.

- **KEEL-216** The contract gate failed every day for a calendar reason, not a code one `p2` · testing, phase10

  Found while checking whether a change of mine had altered the generated mirror. It had not; the calendar had.
  
  `fixture::load` dates the demo corpus relative to `Utc::now()` — `d.decided_at = Some(now - Duration::days(30))`, and a dozen similar. A generated decision therefore reads `**Decided:** 2026-07-16` today and `2026-07-17` tomorrow, so every file carrying a date changes hash once a day.
  
  `contracts/generated.txt` was recorded on 2026-08-14 by KEEL-197. The first run against it, on 2026-08-15, failed on six files. **It would have failed every subsequent day too**, and nothing about that failure has anything to do with a contract changing.
  
  The second-order problem is the one that matters. The documented remedy is `UPDATE_CONTRACTS=1` followed by "read the diff before committing it. That diff is the release diff, and a breaking difference in it needs an entry saying so." A diff that is always noise teaches whoever meets it to re-record without reading — and then a genuine breaking change lands inside a diff nobody looks at. That is the exact failure this file was written to prevent, arriving through the file itself.
  
  It also nearly cost something here: the six changed hashes looked like they might have been mine, and it took building the previous binary and diffing two generated trees to establish that the two mirrors were byte-identical apart from a timestamp.
  
  Fixed the way ids already were, on the same argument — the dates are data, the contract is the layout. `redact_dates` replaces a bare `YYYY-MM-DD` with `<date>` before hashing, so a change to how a date is *rendered* still shows because the label around it does. Narrow on purpose: four-digit years only, not preceded or followed by a digit, so version numbers, durations and content hashes are untouched.
  
  Three tests, and the one that matters asserts the property directly: the same line a day apart redacts identically.
  
  The alternative was giving the fixture fixed absolute dates. Rejected because the corpus is a demo that is meant to look alive in the app, and freezing it in 2026 would make every screen of it look abandoned.

- **KEEL-215** The changelog reprints old field values, so nothing can ever be redacted from the mirror `p2` · cli, privacy

  Found while taking a local machine path out of the tree before publishing.
  
  The path lived in three notes and one task body. Notes were fine — `keel_note --retract` drops them from the render, verified. The task body was not. `keel_update` on KEEL-138's `body` wrote the new text, and the next `keel generate` put the **old** text into `product/STATUS.md`'s Changelog table, quoting the value the field used to hold — the machine path included, one section below the text that had just been cleaned.
  
  So the edit that removed the string added a fresh copy of it. (This description used to quote the offending line as an illustration, which put it back a third time. Describing it is enough.)
  
  This is not a rendering slip, it is two design rules meeting. Events are the attribution spine and are immutable; the changelog is derived from events; the event carries the field's previous value. Every rule is right on its own and together they mean **no value written into Keel can ever be taken back out of the generated mirror.** Soft-delete-only covers rows; nothing covered this.
  
  It matters beyond one username. Anything that should not have been written down — a path, a customer name, an internal hostname, something said in confidence and recorded in a note — is permanent in a file that gets committed, and the only remedy was not committing the mirror at all.
  
  The fix is narrow: the changelog's job is *what changed*, and the value is already on the row. Quote short non-prose values, report long or prose ones by size. Applied in two places, and it needs both — at write time so new events are clean, and at render time so the events already in the log are covered too, since they cannot be edited.
  
  Worth checking separately whether `keel_activity` and the app's history panel do the same thing. They read the same events, but neither writes a file that gets committed, so they are a smaller problem than this was.

- **KEEL-213** The fuzz smoke job fails, and nobody has ever read why `p2` · phase10, ci

  The failing step is `Smoke each target`, which loops `cargo fuzz run` over `confine_path`, `fts_query` and `parse_id`.
  
  Two possibilities and they need telling apart before anything is fixed:
  
  - **The harness is broken** — `cargo-fuzz` not installing, a target name that no longer exists, a nightly that moved. Boring, and the likeliest.
  - **A target actually panicked.** These three parse untrusted input, and `confine_path` is the path-confinement check that a previous review found a traversal bug in. A panic here is a real finding and the artifact would be in the uploaded `fuzz-artifacts`.
  
  Note that the job's artifact upload has never had anything to upload — an earlier run logged "No files were found with the provided path: fuzz/artifacts/", which is consistent with the failure happening before any fuzzing starts, and points at the first possibility.
  
  One change already landed that could affect it: `RUSTUP_TOOLCHAIN: nightly` was added to the job when `rust-toolchain.toml` arrived, because the pin would otherwise have handed cargo-fuzz a stable compiler with no `-Z sanitizer=address`. That was a fix for a problem the pin would have *created*, not for this one, so this failure predates it and survives it.
  
  It only runs on a schedule or a manual dispatch, so it blocks nothing day to day. Reading the log is the whole first step: `gh run view --job <id> --log` once a run has finished, since the logs are unavailable while the run is still going.

- **KEEL-205** Fail loudly on a taken port, and refuse to bind anywhere but loopback `p2` · phase10, security

  **No wandering.** An earlier draft had the daemon walk up the range looking for a free port. That cannot work: the plugin's MCP config expands its environment when Claude Code starts and its settings are written at install time, so a daemon that quietly moves to 7655 leaves both stale and MCP fails with no explanation. A wandering port and a static configuration file cannot both be right.
  
  Three cases:
  
  - Free: bind it.
  - Held by another `keel-daemon` on the same store: exit 0 saying so. That is the single-writer design working, not an error.
  - Held by anything else: fail, naming `--bind` and `KEEL_BIND`.
  
  Collisions get resolved at install time, which is the only moment configuration can still be written — that belongs to the setup task.
  
  `~/.keel/daemon.json` records the live URL for the CLI, which reads it first, then the environment, then the default. **Presence is not liveness** — a crash leaves the file behind — so confirm with a health probe before trusting it.
  
  **Binding.** `KEEL_BIND` takes any address today. It starts refusing anything that is not loopback unless an explicit flag says otherwise, and that flag's help says plainly what is being agreed to. The DNS-rebinding attack is already handled by the Origin and Host checks; this is the other half, the person who types `0.0.0.0` to reach the site from a laptop.

- **KEEL-202** Publish a release manifest, and let the daemon say which store it is holding `p2` · phase10, updates

  This exists because the first version of the update plan was impossible. It said to compare `shipped_schema_version()` on the candidate binary against the store's, which requires executing the candidate. The schema version has to be readable before anything is downloaded.
  
  The manifest sits beside the artifacts: version, schema_version, min_plugin_version, and a checksum per artifact.
  
  `/api/health` already reports version, schema and protocol. It gains `min_plugin_version`, and **the store it is actually serving** — which is what KEEL-194 needs, since today every write command refuses whenever any daemon answers, without ever asking which store that daemon holds. A path or its hash, if the path is thought worth keeping private.
  
  The plugin manifest gains `min_daemon_version` at the same time, so the comparison works from either side.

- **KEEL-201** Assert the two cross-version behaviours that exist but are not tested `p2` · phase10, data-safety

  **The forward guard.** A binary must refuse a store whose schema version is higher than its own and say which upgrade is needed. The behaviour was written after KEEL-95, where a daemon 84 seconds behind wrote NULLs into a column it did not know about, and it was lost once already in the move to SQLite — DuckDB's engine refused such an open on its own and SQLite opened it happily. It came back because repointed tests caught it. There is still no assertion that names it directly.
  
  **Backup across two versions.** The round-trip test backs up, wipes, restores and diffs, all within one binary. Point it across a version boundary: back up with N, restore with N+1, assert equality. That is the shape a user actually meets when something goes wrong after an upgrade.

- **KEEL-199** Refuse a release whose breaking changes nobody wrote down `p2` · phase10, contracts

  This replaces a gate that did not work. An earlier draft refused a release unless the version bump matched the highest severity found — and on 0.x additive and breaking both mean a minor bump, so the condition was satisfied by every release forever while looking like a guard.
  
  The version number is decoration. The mechanism is the acknowledgement: for each breaking difference the sorter found, a checked-in entry for this release naming what breaks, which migration handles it, and what the user is told. CI fails when a breaking difference has no entry.
  
  The release notes then carry a Breaking section generated from those entries, which is the payoff — the notes cannot forget something the gate already knows about.

  - **The control run found a bug in the gate itself**, which is the second time this week that running the control has been worth more than the feature.  `parse_acknowledgements` treated every `## ` heading as an entry — including `## How to add one`, a documentation heading in the acknowledgement file. So the gate manufactured an acknowledgement of a difference called "How to add one", found nothing matching it, reported it stale, and refused a release that was otherwise fine. It would have fired on the very first real release and looked like a mystery.  Entries now live after an explicit `<!-- acknowledgements -->` marker. A file with no marker acknowledges nothing, so renaming the marker gates everything rather than waving it through, and both directions have a test. The instructions stay in the same file on purpose — they are what somebody needs at the moment they are writing an entry — so the boundary has to be stated rather than inferred from heading levels.  **A second self-inflicted one worth recording**, because it cost real work. Cleaning up after the first control run, a script split the file on `"\n## tool "` to remove a test entry — and the *example* in the documentation starts with exactly that, so the file was truncated at the example and half its prose was lost. Rewritten by hand. The lesson is narrow and useful: a heuristic that edits a file by matching prose will eventually match the prose that describes the thing rather than the thing.  One design decision that deserves revisiting if it ever gets annoying: acknowledgement is by the classifier's **exact sentence**, not a stable key. Rewording a rule invalidates the entries quoting it and fails the gate until they are updated. That is intended — if the description of what breaks has changed, whoever signed it off should read it again — and the cost is bounded because rewording a rule is a reviewed change to the same file. If it turns out to fire often for cosmetic reasons, the fix is a `rule` constant per call site rather than deriving a key from the prose. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>

- **KEEL-198** Sort every contract difference into additive or breaking, and fail closed on the rest `p2` · phase10, contracts

  The release diff is `git diff <last-tag>..HEAD -- contracts/`, so this reads a diff rather than needing stored copies.
  
  A table rather than a judgement call, because a judgement call at release time is made by whoever is tired. New nullable column, new tool, new optional argument, new response field: additive. Dropped or renamed anything, a new required argument, a narrowed enum or type, a changed exit code or status code: breaking.
  
  Two rows are not obvious and are decided:
  
  - **Generated markdown has no additive case at all.** Users commit those files, so any layout change is a diff in every repository that has ever run `keel generate`. Not breaking in any technical sense, and exactly as annoying as one, so it gets announced.
  - **A tool description change is additive and still gets printed.** The descriptions are the only documentation a model gets, so a silent rewrite of the thing that decides tool selection deserves a human reading it even though it breaks no caller.
  
  Failing closed is the point. A sorter that guesses "additive" when it is unsure is worse than no sorter, because it produces confidence rather than information.

  - **The classifier got its first real change wrong, in the safe direction.** Adding the `release-manifest` subcommand made it report `21 line(s) disappeared` and call an unambiguously additive change BREAKING.  Nothing had disappeared. clap lays its help out in columns and widens them to fit the longest subcommand name, so one long name re-indented every other line and a set-difference over raw lines read all of them as removed and re-added.  Fixed by collapsing runs of whitespace before comparing, with a test for the re-indent and a second test that a genuinely removed line is still caught — the second one matters, because the obvious fix for the first is to loosen the comparison until it stops complaining about anything.  Worth keeping because of what it says about the whole design. Failing closed meant the cost was an annoying false alarm rather than a missed break, which is the trade working as intended. But **a gate that cries wolf on every new subcommand is one people learn to wave through**, and at that point failing closed stops protecting anything. The line-oriented surfaces — `cli.txt` and `generated.txt` — are the weak ones here, because they carry formatting as well as meaning. If either produces another false breaking, the answer is to compare something structural rather than to keep loosening the text comparison. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>

- **KEEL-197** Make every surface describe itself into contracts/, and fail CI when it drifts `p2` · phase10, contracts

  One always-current directory, not one per release — git already keeps the history, and at a release every few days a directory per release is thirty of them in six months.
  
  What goes in it: the schema through `PRAGMA table_info` / `index_list` / `foreign_key_list` rather than a `sqlite_master` dump, because SQLite keeps the original `CREATE TABLE` text verbatim and a comment change would read as a schema change; `tools/list` in full; one file per HTTP endpoint against the fixture store; `--help` for every subcommand; and the `keel generate` output.
  
  Two rules the determinism work in KEEL-193 established, both measured rather than assumed:
  
  - The generated markdown is emitted with the `keel:generated` banner line stripped and `manifest.json` excluded. Both carry a wall-clock time, and without that step every file changes every second. This is not a new normalisation — `keel generate --check` already does exactly it, which is why a banner four days stale still counts among its "83 current".
  - Everything else already emits identically across a hundred runs, so nothing else needs redacting.
  
  Written as an integration test that regenerates when `UPDATE_CONTRACTS=1` and asserts otherwise, the same shape as the seventeen `insta` snapshots already in the tree. It must refuse to overwrite anything under `stores/`: the text files are safe to regenerate because git shows what changed, but a vintage store cannot be remade and `UPDATE_CONTRACTS=1` is exactly what someone types to make a failing test go away.

  - **The gate found real non-determinism on its first run**, which is worth more than the gate passing would have been.  `fixture::load` mints fresh ULIDs every time it runs, and the generated markdown prints ids, so every content-bearing file's hash differed between two consecutive emissions. KEEL-193's measurement never saw this: it ran `keel generate` against a store built once, so the ids were fixed and only the banner timestamp moved. Building the fixture inside the emitter changed that, and nothing would have warned about it.  Fixed by redacting ids to a placeholder rather than by pinning the fixture. **The ids are data; the contract is the layout.** What the file asserts is "a generated decision looks like this" — which headings, which fields, in which order — not which decisions the fixture happens to contain. A change to how an id is rendered still shows up, because the surrounding text does.  A second defect found while recording the baseline: the first version called `.next()` on an unsorted project list, so it generated for whichever project the store happened to return first. That described one small project while claiming to describe the generator, and depended on an ordering nothing promises. It now covers all three, sorted by id — 24 files instead of 4.  Two things deliberately left for later:  - **`api/*.json` is not emitted yet.** It needs the daemon's router driven in-process, which is a different harness from the rest of this file. The HTTP surface is currently described by nothing, and that is a real gap rather than a decision. - **`cli.txt` comes from running the built binary**, not from rendering the parser in process. `keel-cli` has no library half so its `Cli` type is not importable — but the better reason is that the description then comes from what actually ships.  One structural note for whoever builds the classifier: `generated.txt` is a listing of path plus content hash, not the files themselves. That is enough to notice a layout change and not enough to see what changed, which is the right trade at 67 files — but it means the classifier cannot say *what* moved in a generated document, only that something did. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>

- **KEEL-196** Adopt Apache-2.0 and clear the things that assume Keel is unpublished `p1` · phase10, legal

  Apache-2.0 was chosen for the patent grant MIT lacks. B-65 has the reasoning.
  
  Three things come out together, because they were all put there to say "this is not for anyone else":
  
  - `publish = false` in the workspace package.
  - `deny.toml`'s `private.ignore`, which exists only to stop cargo deny tripping over the missing licence.
  - The comment in `Cargo.toml` explaining why there is deliberately no SPDX identifier.
  
  Add `LICENSE` at the repository root and `license = "Apache-2.0"` to the workspace package so the metadata says it too.
  
  Pairs with creating the remote: that is the moment the repository becomes visible, and being visible without a licence means nobody may legally use what they can see.

  - Two things came out differently from what this task's body said, both on purpose.  **`publish = false` stays.** The body said it should come out with the rest. Removing it would allow `cargo publish` to succeed by accident, and Keel is not a crates.io package — it is two binaries shipped as release artifacts plus a library nobody depends on externally. What the flag *meant* has changed rather than whether it belongs: it used to say "this is nobody else's", and now it says "this does not go to crates.io". The comment above it says so, since a reader in six months would otherwise read it as the leftover it looks like.  **Removing the exemption immediately failed, which is the exemption working.** `private = { ignore = true }` had been suppressing the licence check on Keel's own crates. Deleting it and adding `license = "Apache-2.0"` to `[workspace.package]` was not enough — member crates do not inherit `license` implicitly, so `cargo deny` failed with `keel-mcp = 0.1.0 is unlicensed` on the first run. Each of the five crates needed `license.workspace = true` alongside the `version.workspace` lines it already had.  Worth recording because it is the whole argument for deleting the exemption rather than updating it: a future crate that forgets its licence now fails here, loudly, instead of being waved through by a config line nobody remembers is there. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>

- **KEEL-195** Create the remote and make CI run for the first time `p1` · phase10, ops

  Step zero of Phase 10, and it comes before the contracts work rather than after it: a set of contract files generated on one machine has that machine's opinion baked into them, and nobody would know.
  
  `git remote -v` is empty and no keel repository exists under the account. The trigger also named `main` while trunk was `master`, fixed in f65be15, so even once a remote exists a push to trunk would not have started anything until that landed.
  
  HTTPS, not SSH — key authentication is broken on this machine and anything cloning over `git@github.com` fails.
  
  Public, because macOS runners are free for public repositories and Phase 10 needs them for the ad-hoc signature. That makes this the moment the repository becomes visible, so it wants the licence task done alongside it.

  - The repository exists — `github.com/kiritbasu/keel`, **private** at KB's instruction rather than public as this task's body assumed. Two consequences worth carrying into the release work: macOS runner minutes are free only on public repositories, and they bill at ten times the Linux rate, so a release pipeline that builds two Mac targets on every tag will eat an allowance fast. The task body's reasoning ("public, because macOS runners are free") no longer holds and the pipeline task should account for it.  Trunk is `main`, not `master`. An earlier journal note says the opposite and was true when written; `main` is now 102 commits ahead and `master` is a leftover from repository creation. It was not pushed.  **CI ran for the first time in this project's history and found a real bug in four minutes.** That is the whole argument for this task, delivered on its first run.  `Utc::now()` is nanosecond-precise and the timestamp columns hold six sub-second digits — deliberately, and documented on `TIMESTAMP_FORMAT`. So an entity returned by `create` carried three digits the store then discarded, and a later `get` returned something measurably different. The round-trip test compared them all along; it passed because macOS's clock rarely fills those digits. Linux's does. `…:40.360446Z` from the store against `…:40.360446996Z` in memory.  Fixed in `Audit::new` and `Audit::touched` — the one place every audit block is built — so the invariant holds by construction. The test is built from a fixed nanosecond value rather than the clock, because a test that only reproduces on some hardware is not a test, and that is exactly how this hid.  **A second finding, and it undermines the local gate.** CI runs rustc 1.97.1. This machine has Homebrew's 1.91.0 on `PATH`, and rustup's own stable is 1.63 from 2022. Three Rusts, none of them CI's. So `cargo clippy -- -D warnings`, which the definition of done requires before a task can close, has been answering a different question from the one that decides. Two `unnecessary_sort_by` lints existed on `main` and no local run could have seen them. Either the toolchain gets pinned so both sides agree, or the local gate is understood to be advisory — but it should not silently be the second while the contract says the first.  Still failing as of this note and not yet chased: the fuzz smoke job, and the snapshot and coverage jobs, which were failing on the same round-trip assertion and should clear with it. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>
  - **Stopped here, mid-task, at KB's request. Everything is committed and pushed; the tree is clean.** This is what a fresh session needs to finish it.  **Where it stands.** The repository exists and is private, `main` is the default branch, and CI has now run three times where it had never run at all. Of seven jobs, four are green — snapshots, coverage, licences/advisories, and mutation discovery. **Tests pass on both platforms.** Three remain red:  - `fmt · clippy · test (ubuntu-latest)` — the failing step is **Clippy only**, not the tests. - `fmt · clippy · test (macos-latest)` — same, Clippy only. - `fuzz smoke (60s per target)` — never looked at. Unknown, and it was failing before any of this started.  **The one thing that will make this quick.** The Clippy failures are lints from rustc 1.97 which cannot be reproduced on this machine, because `PATH` has Homebrew's 1.91. Three CI round-trips were spent fixing them one report at a time, at about ten minutes each, which is the wrong loop.  `rustup toolchain install 1.97` has now been done, so the next session can see every remaining lint at once:  ``` cargo +1.97 clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings ```  Two lints were already fixed this way — both `unnecessary_sort_by`, both pre-existing on `main`. Expect a handful more of the same character: mechanical, with clippy's own suggested replacement.  **Worth settling rather than repeating.** The definition of done requires a clean local clippy before a task closes, and that gate has been answering a different question from the one CI asks. Either pin the toolchain — a `rust-toolchain.toml` naming 1.97 would make both sides agree and is a five-line change — or write down that the local gate is advisory. It should not silently be the second while the contract says the first.  **Do not lose this.** The reason all of it was worth doing is in the note above: CI's first run found a real cross-platform data bug within four minutes, and the fix is committed in aef9b39 and 261ecef. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>

- **KEEL-194** A write to one store is refused because a daemon is serving a different one `p2` · phase10, cli
- **KEEL-193** Prove the contract surfaces emit deterministically, before anything is built on them `p2`

  - Ran it. **Nine of ten surfaces are stable at N=100. The one that flaps does so for a reason that costs nothing to fix, so §5 proceeds as written.**  ``` CLI    cli-help / generate --help / close --help   100 runs, 1 hash Store  schema via PRAGMA                           100 runs, 1 hash MCP    tools/list                                  100 runs, 1 hash HTTP   /api/health /api/context /api/activity      100 runs, 1 hash Gen    generate, raw                               100 runs, 100 hashes Gen    generate, normalised                        100 runs, 1 hash ```  Everything ran against a fixed fixture store with its own daemon on port 7699, so nothing observed here is the live store changing underneath the probe.  **The flap is two wall-clock timestamps and nothing else.** Every `.keel/` mirror file opens with a banner carrying the generation time — `<!-- keel:generated decision dec_… v2 2026-08-14T11:28:33Z` — and `.keel/manifest.json` carries `generated_at`. So two generates in the same second are identical and two a second apart differ in all 67 files, which is why an early run at N=5 looked intermittent at 2 distinct hashes and the real answer at N=100 is 100. Strip the banner line and drop the manifest and it is one hash across a hundred runs spanning minutes.  **The normalisation is not something to invent — `keel generate --check` already does it.** The committed banner in `.keel/decisions/chrono-for-time-not-jiff.md` reads `2026-08-10T18:53:24Z`, four days old, and `--check` still reports that file among "83 current". So the emitter in §5.1 must normalise exactly the way `--check` does, and the requirement is now stated rather than assumed.  Two corrections to things I got wrong earlier in this session, both worth having on the record:  - **`keel fixture` is not broken.** §5.3 of Phase 10 says a run "produced none", which was true and the cause was mine twice over: `--home` is a subcommand flag rather than a global one, so `keel --home X fixture` parses as something else, and the second attempt was refused because a daemon was running. With `keel fixture --home /tmp/keel-det/store --force` it produces a 700 KB store in about a second — which also settles the corpus-size question, since four schema versions of that is under 3 MB. - **`keel generate --check` exits 1 correctly.** A moment in this session recorded it exiting 0 while reporting a stale file, which would have been a serious bug in something the pre-commit hook depends on. That reading was wrong: the command was piped into `tail`, so `$?` was `tail`'s status. Unpiped it exits 1, and `--repo` did not leak into the real repository either — `git status` stayed clean throughout.  The harness is at `scratchpad/determinism.sh` and is worth keeping rather than rewriting: it takes N as an environment variable, keeps two differing runs on disk when a surface flaps so the cause can be read instead of guessed, and the whole run at N=100 takes 60 seconds. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>

- **KEEL-192** The Stop hook nags in projects Keel has never heard of `p2`

  - Verified by hand, not by a regression test, and nobody should later assume otherwise. There is no test harness for the shell hooks at all — nothing in the workspace or in CI executes `stop.sh` or `session-start.sh`, so the only thing standing between this fix and a silent regression is that the file is short.  Six cases run against the live daemon: a Keel directory with a session that wrote nothing (blocks, correctly), the same payload with `cwd` pointing at the home directory instead (silent — this is the bug), this session's own id in the Keel directory (silent, because it had written), the `stop_hook_active` guard, the once-per-session marker, and an unreachable daemon. The pair that matters is the first two: identical apart from `cwd`, and they now diverge, so the new check discriminates rather than always exiting.  The test arrives when the hook moves into the binary as `keel hook stop`, which Phase 10 §8 now carries. That port is also what removes `python3` and `curl` as undeclared dependencies and makes the hooks work on Windows. Until then this logic lives somewhere untestable, which is the argument for the port rather than an objection to the fix.  *(Replaces a retracted note with identical content. The original quoted an absolute path carrying the machine's username.)* <sub>— claude · 2026-08-15</sub>

- **KEEL-190** Close the raw-SQL leak in doctor and the duplicated row readers in entity.rs `p2` · hardening, cleanup

  - Both findings turned out smaller than the review made them sound, in opposite directions.  The doctor one was smaller because the method already existed. I had proposed adding `EntityStore::newest_event_id()`; `latest_event_id()` has been on the trait since the fix that removed the hundred-thousand-row scan, and the daemon already calls it. So `clock_sanity` was not merely reaching across a crate boundary to write SQL — it was a raw-SQL near-duplicate of a canonical helper, which is worse than the review said and cheaper to fix.  One thing had to be preserved through the switch. `latest_event_id()` returns `Err` on an id it cannot parse, and doctor must not fail: its whole value is being safe to run on a store you are worried about, and an error here would stop every check after it. So the `Err` arm becomes a `Check::problem`. The diagnostic survives because `Error::MalformedId` prints the offending id, and the separate "not a ULID" branch stays — `EventId::parse` validates the prefix only, so `evt_nonsense` still gets past it.  The file-size one was bigger in the sense that the interesting part was not size at all. `entity.rs` held private copies of `col_err`, `get_ts` and `get_ots` that `rows.rs` — the module whose doc says it owns row mapping — already had. Two timestamp readers is a real drift risk, because `rows.rs` documents the format as load-bearing and its `parse_ts` is deliberately lenient about the DuckDB-era renderings still sitting in stores on disk. A second reader that did not learn that is how those rows become unreadable.  `get_oid` was the one that could not be merged, and nearly was by mistake. Both modules had one; `entity.rs`'s accepts `''` as absent because `v_entities` spells "no project" that way for a global term (Q-4), and `rows.rs`'s would hand `''` to `EntityId::parse` and error. Same name, sibling modules, different semantics — renamed to `get_oid_or_empty` so the next person to notice the duplication sees the reason before the resemblance.  Considered and rejected: `i()` and `os()` are also defined in both `docs.rs` and `rows.rs`. They are three-line parameter shims and an `i32`-to-`i64` cast has one possible implementation, so deduping them buys an import and no reduction in risk. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>

- **KEEL-189** A passing test run leaks temp stores too, which is not what B-58 was decided on `p3` · testing, tooling

  Measured 2026-08-13 22:2x, twice, on a warm build:
  
  ```
  stores before: 449
  test exit=0
  tests passed=915
  stores after:  468
  LEAKED BY A FULLY PASSING RUN: 19
  ```
  
  B-58 says "A test binary that runs to completion leaks nothing — `TempDir::drop` works. Only a killed process leaks." The first half is not true, and the whole cost/benefit in that decision rests on it: the leak was taken to be occasional and traceable to `cargo mutants` timeouts, which is what made a repo-local TMPDIR look like too much blast radius for too little.
  
  The accumulation is steady, not bursty. Every store in TMPDIR right now is from today, and the hourly histogram tracks test runs rather than one mutation run:
  
  ```
   16  10:00     15  11:00     19  12:00
  171  13:00     57  14:00     19  15:00
   19  20:00     76  21:00     57  22:00
  ```
  
  The 21:00 and 22:00 rows are one session doing an ordinary cleanup task and running the suite three times.
  
  ## Where to look
  
  Not a deliberate leak — there is no `into_path()`, `.keep()`, `.persist()` or `mem::forget` anywhere in the tree. That leaves child processes, and five files spawn them:
  
  - `crates/keel-core/tests/single_writer.rs`
  - `crates/keel-daemon/tests/end_to_end.rs`
  - `crates/keel-daemon/tests/wont_restart_loop.rs`
  - `crates/keel-cli/tests/verbs.rs`
  - `crates/keel-cli/src/main.rs`
  
  `single_writer.rs` is the first place to look, because it kills a child on purpose — `a_killed_holder_leaves_nothing_behind` and the `#[ignore]`d helper it spawns. A killed child never runs Drop, so whatever store it opened stays. If that accounts for most of the 19, the fix is for the parent to own the directory and clean up after the kill, which is cheap and does not touch B-58 at all.
  
  ## What this does not reopen
  
  B-58's actual conclusion — sweeper rather than redirected TMPDIR — may well still be right; a repo-local TMPDIR still changes where rustc and the linker put scratch files. The point of this task is that the decision should be made against 19-per-run rather than against "only when something is killed". Fixing the leak at its source may make the question moot.

  - My diagnosis in the task body was wrong on both counts, and the way it was wrong is the useful part.  I wrote "there is no `into_path()`, `.keep()`, `.persist()` or `mem::forget` anywhere in the tree", and concluded the leak had to come from a killed child process — pointing at `single_writer.rs` because it kills one deliberately. Both halves were wrong. I had grepped for four spellings of a deliberate leak and missed the fifth, `Box::leak`, which is the one actually in use. `single_writer.rs` leaks nothing: its child opens the *parent's* path and never makes a `TempDir` of its own.  Measuring instead of reasoning found it in one pass. Running each test binary separately and counting `TMPDIR` either side:  ``` keel-core/generate     LEAKED 19 ```  All nineteen, from one file. `generate.rs`'s `fixture()` helper did:  ```rust let dir = tempfile::tempdir().unwrap(); // Leaked so the store outlives the helper; the process is a test binary. let dir = Box::leak(Box::new(dir)); ```  Nineteen tests call it, so nineteen stores. The comment is honest about what it does and the reasoning is even defensible in isolation — a test binary does exit shortly after. What it misses is that `TempDir`'s cleanup is the *only* thing that would have removed the directory, so leaking the handle makes the process's exit irrelevant.  The lesson for the next one of these: a grep for known leak spellings proves nothing, because the list of spellings is open. Counting what lands on disk per binary is a closed measurement and it took two minutes. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>

- **KEEL-188** Sweep the tree for dead code and files that outlived their phase `p2` · hardening, cleanup

  - Clippy being clean says nothing about this. The `dead_code` lint does not fire on `pub` items in a library crate, so everything found here was invisible to `-D warnings` — the workspace was clippy-clean before and after. What found it was counting references: pull every `pub fn` name out of a crate, grep the whole tree for it, and keep the ones whose only hit is their own definition.  175 `pub fn` in keel-core, three dead. The other crates gave two more, both in keel-mcp: `open_task_statuses` and `urgent_priorities`, thin wrappers over `TaskStatus::is_open` and `TaskPriority::is_urgent` that nothing called. The two tests in `enums.rs` whose names look like they cover them test the enum methods, not the wrappers — the names matched my first grep and nearly cost me the finding.  Same method on the desktop app found `inListOrder` and `useNavigate`, each defined at the bottom of its file and never imported. Removing all seven orphaned two imports that only the compiler could have told me about, which is the argument for doing this with the build rather than by eye. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>
  - Two files went, and the interesting one is why moving `RESET-PLAN.md` into Keel could not use `keel import`.  The import was refused, correctly:  ``` Error: a daemon is running at http://127.0.0.1:7654, and it owns the single write path. ```  That is B-60 doing its job — the guard KEEL-182 put on import. So the document went in over MCP instead, which is the daemon's own write path and needed nothing stopped. Worth knowing for the next migration: with a daemon up, `keel import` is not the tool, `keel_write_doc` is. `--dry-run` still works either way, because it only reads.  `START-PHASE-8.md` had no inbound references and was a kickoff prompt for a phase that finished long ago. It had also gone stale in a way that would mislead: it lists "twelve MCP tools instead of ten" as a question needing KB's decision, and the cap has been thirteen since TQ-31. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>

- **KEEL-185** The daemonless work verbs are fixed but have no tests, so nothing would catch the regression `p2` · cli, testing

  Found while deciding whether to merge `claude/recursing-blackburn-0e4d48` before pushing.
  
  Its commit `38dece4` fixed a real bug: `keel ready` printed "nothing ready" whenever no daemon was listening, because `dispatch` returns the MCP envelope, `call_daemon` unwrapped `structuredContent` from it and the fall-back-to-the-store branch returned the envelope whole. The renderer asked for `ready`, got `None`, and reported what `None` looks like. `claim` and `close` had the same shape more quietly — they fall back to their argument when `reference` is missing, so daemonless they echoed the ULID with an empty title.
  
  **The behaviour is superseded.** Verified against a fixture store with the daemon unreachable:
  
  ```
  keel ready harbour        → HARB-3, HARB-4, HARB-5 with priorities
  keel claim HARB-4         → "HARB-4 claimed — Reconcile against Stripe webhooks"
  keel close HARB-3 …       → "HARB-3 closed as done"
  ```
  
  All three name the reference and the title. Almost certainly because the digest moved from `keel-mcp::context` into `keel_core::digest`, which removed the envelope mismatch the fix was working around — that refactor is also why the branch cannot be merged, since it is written against a module this tree no longer has.
  
  **The coverage is not superseded.** That commit added six tests — `ready_without_a_daemon_lists_the_work`, `the_fallback_hands_back_structured_content_not_the_envelope`, `a_refusal_on_the_fallback_path_is_an_error_not_an_empty_list`, `claim_without_a_daemon_names_the_task_and_its_title`, `close_without_a_daemon_reports_the_reference_it_closed`, `a_cut_list_says_so_with_the_total` — and `crates/keel-cli/src/work.rs` on this branch contains **zero test functions**.
  
  So the bug is fixed by accident of a refactor and nothing holds it fixed. That is the exact shape this project keeps finding: correct today, silently wrong after the next change, with the failure being an empty list that reads as "nothing to do".
  
  Port the six tests. They do not depend on the old module — they drive the CLI against a seeded store with the daemon unreachable, which is the same thing done by hand above.

  - Correcting this task's premise before closing it.  It says `work.rs` has zero tests and the daemonless path has no coverage. The first half is true and the second does not follow. I grepped `crates/keel-cli/src/work.rs` for `fn `, found nothing, and concluded there was no coverage anywhere — without looking for an integration test. `crates/keel-cli/tests/verbs.rs` has nine, driving the real binary against a closed port, and its own module doc says why it exists at that layer rather than as unit tests.  So the port described here was mostly unnecessary. Five of the six tests from `38dece4` already have equivalents there.  Checking properly rather than believing either version found a real and narrower gap. Reintroducing the original regression — returning the MCP envelope instead of `keel_mcp::payload` — behaves differently on the two paths:  - **read path** (`ready`): `ready_lists_work_and_says_why_it_is_first` and `a_truncated_ready_list_says_how_much_it_left_out` both fail. Covered. - **write path** (`run_write`, used by `claim` and `close`): all nine tests stayed green. Not covered.  That is exactly the quieter half of the original bug. `claim`'s existing test asserts `out.contains("claimed")`, and that string is still there when the renderer falls back to echoing the ULID — the line reads like success and names the wrong thing.  Two tests added for it, one per verb, both verified to fail with the unwrap removed. The technique is the one `38dece4` used: pass the raw ULID, so a readable `HARB-n` in the output can only have come from the response.  The lesson worth keeping is about the check I did, not the code: "no tests in this file" and "no tests for this behaviour" are different claims, and I reported the second having only established the first. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>

- **KEEL-183** Write the adoption workflow: how a session backfills an existing project without flooding it `p2` · adoption, docs

  The judged half of B-61. No new code — this is the prompt, not a feature.
  
  `keel import` handles anything that is already a document. What it cannot do is decide that an ADR is a decision, that a heading is a spec, or that a `TODO` is a task. Those are judgements, and a parser making them at scale gets them wrong at scale, which is why B-61 puts them in a session instead.
  
  What the workflow has to cover:
  
  - **Read before writing.** Survey the repository and propose a shape — how many specs, decisions, tasks, terms — and get agreement before creating anything.
  - **Consolidate rather than transcribe.** The standing instructions already say forty trivial tasks that should be eight is worse than useless. An adopter's repo will offer hundreds of candidates. The workflow should be judged on what it left out, and should say so in those words.
  - **The digest is the budget.** `keel_context` is the first thing every future session reads, it is budgeted at 3–4k tokens, and it is over budget on *this* project with eight open questions. A backfill that fills the register with speculative questions makes every future session worse. That is the specific failure to warn about, because it is invisible at the moment it happens.
  - **What not to bring.** Stale `TODO` comments. Closed work that produced no decision. Git history as events — the event log is Keel's own audit trail of writes to Keel, and backfilling it fabricates an audit trail rather than recording one.
  - **Where the mechanical half fits.** `keel import` for the files, first, so the session is working against documents that already exist rather than pasting prose into task bodies.
  
  Worth testing on a repository that is not this one. Keel's own bootstrap is 1,357 lines of hardcoded knowledge about Keel's docs, which proves nothing about a repository nobody wrote for the purpose — and the failure mode here is enthusiasm, which only shows up on someone else's corpus.

  - Ran step 1 of the workflow against `~/development/data-coworker` — 63 markdown files, 129 commits, not a Keel project and not written with Keel in mind. Stopped at the proposal, which is what the workflow tells you to do, so nothing was created.  What a survey turns up:  - `decisions/` — 9 ADRs. One file, one decision, mechanical. - `specs/` — 9 `SPEC-00N-*.md`. Mechanical. - `design/` — 3 briefs and a review. - `docs/` — about 20 files, most of them one-off benchmark and results write-ups. - `BACKLOG.md` — 124 checkbox tasks: 54 open, 3 in progress, 67 done. - Code `TODO`s: **one**, in the whole repository.  Three things worth having found, because they are what a parser would get wrong:  **The backlog is not 57 tasks.** 54 open plus 3 in flight, but the `T-1xx` items are steps — "scaffold repo", "salvage audit", "run e2e on a fresh db". Consolidated they are perhaps fifteen. Importing 57 would put a project into Keel that nobody would read past, which is the specific failure the skill leads with.  **The 67 done ones are not history worth keeping.** They are closed work that produced no decision. What is worth keeping from them is the milestone structure — `M0 · Scope lock + salvage audit`, and its siblings — which is four or five milestones rather than 67 rows.  **There are decisions buried in the backlog.** Task notes carry lines like `RESOLVED (founder 2026-07-18): query logs are a first-class scan input…` with the reasoning attached. Those are real decisions living in a checkbox file, and a parser pointed at `decisions/` would find nine and miss them. This is the single best argument in the whole task for the judged half being a session: the decisions were not where decisions go.  The `TODO`-comment rule turned out not to apply here at all — one comment, repository-wide. Worth keeping in the skill anyway since it costs a paragraph and most repositories are not this tidy, but this one did not exercise it, and I would rather say so than imply the rule was validated.  Not run past the proposal: creating rows in someone else's project to prove a workflow works is the same overreach the skill exists to prevent. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>

- **KEEL-182** keel import cannot be previewed, and a bad import cannot be taken back `p2` · cli, adoption

  The mechanical half of B-61's backfill. `keel import` is already the right tool — idempotent, records `mirror_path`, re-importing an unchanged file does nothing — but it commits on the first attempt and there is no way to look first.
  
  That matters more for an adopter than it does here. Someone pointing it at an existing repository is guessing which files are specs, which are decisions and which are neither, and the first guess is usually wrong. Hard constraint 3 is soft delete only, so a wrong guess is not undone, it is archived: the rows stay on disk, out of every view, for ever. The cost of being wrong is permanent clutter in the thing that is supposed to be the clean record.
  
  `--dry-run` should print, per file, what artifact it would land in, what the title would be, whether that artifact already exists, whether the content differs from the current revision, and the `mirror_path` it would claim. That last one is the one people get wrong and the one that is hardest to see afterwards.
  
  Two things worth getting right rather than assuming:
  
  - The dry run must not open the store for writing. It is a read, so it should not take the advisory lock (B-60) and should work while a daemon is running — which is the state an adopter's machine will be in.
  - "Already imported, unchanged" and "already imported, would add a revision" are different answers and the output has to distinguish them, or re-running the preview during a migration tells you nothing.
  
  Not in scope: inferring tasks, decisions or glossary terms from a repository. B-61 puts that in the judged layer, where a session does it and the writes carry a real `session_id`.

- **KEEL-181** The app never refetches when the live feed reconnects, so a daemon restart leaves it stale `p2` · desktop, bug

  Found because KEEL-180 was created and did not appear on the board.
  
  The task was there the whole time. `/api/ready?project=keel` returned it, the row was in the store, and the board groups by status so a task with no phase still has a column. What had not happened was the app being *told*.
  
  `subscribe` in `apps/desktop/src/lib/api.ts` attaches two listeners:
  
  ```
  source.addEventListener("change", forward);
  source.addEventListener("lagged", forward);
  ```
  
  and nothing else. `EventSource` reconnects on its own — that part is fine — but the app's only route to a refetch is an announced change arriving on a live connection. So the sequence is: connection drops, writes happen, connection comes back, and none of those writes is ever mentioned. The board keeps showing what it had before the drop until something *else* is written, at which point everything appears at once and looks like it just happened.
  
  The daemon was restarted four times on 2026-08-13 — reinstalling binaries after each migration — which is four windows where exactly this could happen, and the symptom was a task that existed everywhere except on screen.
  
  Two fixes, and the first is one line:
  
  - **Refetch on `open`.** `EventSource` fires it on every connect including reconnects, so `source.addEventListener("open", forward)` closes the gap. The first fire is redundant with the initial load, which costs one wasted read.
  - **Say when the feed is down.** `onerror` means disconnected, and right now nothing on screen distinguishes "nothing has changed" from "nothing can reach me". A quiet indicator is enough; the point is that a stale board should not look identical to a current one, which is this project's whole preoccupation.
  
  Worth checking while in there: notes are announced as a separate `note` kind because they are not events (TQ-29). A probe on 2026-08-13 saw `keel_update completed` arrive on the stream but did not see a `keel_note` write announced. That may have been output buffering in the probe rather than a real gap — it is worth one deliberate test rather than an assumption.

  - Second probe, this one unbuffered: checking whether a note write reaches the live feed, since the first attempt saw nothing and that may have been `curl` buffering rather than a gap. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>

- **KEEL-180** Take an advisory lock when opening the store for writing `p2` · storage, daemon, single-writer

  B-60 settles the shape. Two things to build, and they are independent.
  
  **The lock.** `std::fs::File::try_lock` on a file beside the store, taken when it is opened for writing. The daemon holds it for its lifetime; the CLI takes it for the length of a direct write and drops it. A second acquirer is refused immediately, with a message that says the store is already open for writing rather than reporting a generic IO error.
  
  No new dependency, but `rust-version` moves from 1.85 to 1.89, which is when the API stabilised. The toolchain here is already 1.91.
  
  Measured before deciding, because the objection in TQ-36 was that a stale lock is worse than the problem:
  
  ```
  --- while the holder is alive ---
  REFUSED — still held: "WouldBlock"
  --- after SIGKILL of the holder ---
  ACQUIRED — the lock was free
  ```
  
  The kernel drops the lock with the file descriptor, so there is no stale state to clean up and no repair command to write. That is the whole reason this is worth doing rather than a PID file.
  
  **The wording.** Hard constraint 1 in `product/CLAUDE.md` currently says no other process writes to the store. It should say that everything which writes goes through `keel-core`'s write path, which is the thing actually being protected — six of the seven steps in a Keel write have nothing to do with locking. That file is KB's and the change is one sentence, so it is worth a look before it goes in.
  
  Worth testing beyond the happy path: a second opener is refused rather than blocking forever; the CLI can still write when no daemon is running; a `--force` direct write is not silently defeated by the lock the daemon holds — that path exists for a wedged daemon and has to keep working, which probably means `--force` skips the lock and says so.
  
  The failure this prevents, from 2026-08-13: `keel-daemon --bind 127.0.0.1:7699 --embeddings` with `--home` forgotten opened the live store, applied migration 4 under a daemon that was already serving, and nothing anywhere objected. The migration guard could not catch it — it refuses a binary older than the store, and this was newer. It was noticed only because `/api/health` reported a schema number that disagreed with what the store should have been at.

  - Probe while investigating why this task was not visible on the board: writing through the daemon to see whether the live feed announces it. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>
  - The constraint wording is in, and constraint 3 needed it as well.  Constraint 1 now leads with what is actually protected — everything that writes goes through `keel-core`'s write path — and keeps exclusivity as a second paragraph describing the lock, including what it does not cover: reading takes none, and `--force` skips it.  Constraint 3 was not part of the ask and was changed anyway, because today's work is what made it false. It said nothing is ever `DELETE`d, full stop, and B-55 carves out derived indexes. The carve-out is now named, scoped to `fts_source` and `document_chunks`, and tied to the test that keeps it honest. Leaving it would have been the thing B-60 objects to: a contract claiming a guarantee it no longer provides.  Worth recording how it was written, because the obvious way is wrong. `product/CLAUDE.md` carries a `keel:generated` banner, so editing the file directly would have been reverted by the next `keel generate`. Route taken: edit the file, stop the daemon, `keel import product/CLAUDE.md --project keel` — which is the documented path for exactly this — restart, then `keel generate keel --check` to prove the store reproduces the file byte for byte. It came back `up to date (76 files)`.  The alternative was `keel_write_doc` with the full body, which is correct and costs a 17 KB round trip through a model that would be retyping 234 lines of contract. Import moves the bytes without anyone transcribing them, and `--check` is what makes that safe rather than hopeful.  Also worth knowing: the import needed the daemon stopped, and it now needs that twice over — the probe refuses a direct write while a daemon listens, and the lock would refuse it even if the probe were bypassed. The two guards agreed, which is the first time both have been exercised together. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>
