  `doctor::tests::documents_with_no_vectors_are_reported_without_failing` failed in two of four `cargo test --workspace` runs today and passed in the other two. Run on its own, or with `doctor::tests` as a filter, all nine pass every time.
  
  The assertion that goes is the last one:
  
  ```
  panicked at crates/keel-cli/src/doctor.rs:717:9:
  degraded search is not a broken store
  ```
  
  That is `report.is_healthy()`, not the embeddings check — the embeddings check is `Degraded` as expected and the test gets that far. So some *other* check in `examine` goes unhealthy, and only when the rest of the binary's tests are running beside it.
  
  The test builds its own tempdir and its own store, so the interference is coming from something with reach beyond the fixture. The leaked-store sweeper is the obvious suspect — `e15dcc7` fixed it "only worked where TMPDIR ends in a slash", so it has recently started working in more cases than it used to, and a sweeper that removes another test's store mid-run would produce exactly this. Worth checking before looking anywhere else.
  
  Whatever the cause, printing the unhealthy check rather than asserting a bare boolean would have named it in the failure output instead of leaving this to be reconstructed. That is probably the first change to make.
  
  Not caused by the archive work in KEEL-175 — it fails identically with and without it, and the check that goes is not the one that touched.

  - Tried to reproduce this and could not, which is itself the finding.  **23 consecutive clean runs** of `cargo test -p keel-cli --bin keel` — 6, then 12, then a pair run concurrently with `keel-core`'s lib tests to see whether two cargo processes sharing TMPDIR would trip it. Nothing failed. It had failed 3 times in roughly 7 runs earlier the same day.  What changed between then and now is not the code. Every one of the three failures happened inside the window when a second session was building, committing and rewriting files in this same working tree — at one point `enums.rs` was mid-edit and would not compile. A test binary running while the tree it was built from is being rewritten underneath it is a sufficient explanation for an intermittent failure that vanishes once the tree is still, and it is the only thing that meaningfully changed.  That is a hypothesis, not a diagnosis. I could not prove it, and I would rather leave this open than close it on 23 runs and a plausible story.  The one thing I did change is the assertion. It was `assert!(report.is_healthy(), "degraded search is not a broken store")` — a bare boolean, so the failure output said nothing about which of forty checks had gone. A `problems(&report)` helper now lists the offending check names and their detail in the panic message. That is why this took so long to get anywhere: three failures, and not one of them said what actually broke.  The sweeper suspicion in the task body is worth dropping. `scripts/sweep-build-artifacts.sh` is run by hand, not by the suite, and it only takes directories untouched for an hour — so it cannot take a running test's store out from under it. It was the obvious suspect and it is the wrong one.  If this returns, the panic message will name the check and this becomes a five-minute job. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>

- **KEEL-176** fsck and doctor should prove the passage index matches the store `p2` · search, storage

  The mechanism that keeps passages in step with documents is a transaction on the write path. The check that proves the mechanism is working is a separate thing, and on this project it is the more valuable of the two — every bug in this area so far has been something that silently did nothing while looking fine.
  
  `documents.body_hash` already exists. Carrying it on the passage row makes coherence a query rather than a promise, in both directions:
  
  - **Missing** — a document that is `status = 'current'`, not archived, and has no passages at the current `body_hash`. Means an edit landed and the re-embed did not, or embedding failed and degraded as designed.
  - **Stale or orphaned** — passages whose `doc_id` is no longer current, whose entity is archived, or whose `body_hash` no longer matches. Means a delete was missed.
  - **Wrong model** — passages whose `embedding_model` is not the one now configured. Not an error; it is the input to a re-embed pass, and TQ-3 is the open question about when that runs.
  
  Report the counts in `doctor` with the remedy, the way the existing embeddings check does, and the offending ids in `fsck`. A clean store has to say it is clean, or a passing check means nothing.
  
  The failure case matters as much as the happy one: write a test that edits a document without re-embedding and asserts the check catches it. A coherence check that has only ever seen a coherent store is the same kind of reassurance as the pre-commit hook that called a command that had been renamed.

- **KEEL-175** Semantic search has no archived filter, and the test that should catch it can't `p2` · search, storage, bug

  A bug with no symptoms yet, and the backfill is what gives it symptoms. Cheapest possible moment to fix it.
  
  The keyword half is right by construction: `fts_source` is maintained by triggers on `documents`, archived rows are already gone from it, and the comment at `store/search.rs:353` says exactly that — "no query in this file carries a `WHERE archived_at IS NULL` that someone could later forget."
  
  The semantic half is that forgotten filter. `search_semantic` reads `documents` directly:
  
  ```
  WHERE status = 'current' AND embedding IS NOT NULL AND length(embedding) = ?
  ```
  
  No archive predicate. `reembed_missing` has none either, so a backfill embeds archived documents and hands them to the fusion, where BM25 refuses to return them and cosine does not. The two halves would disagree about what exists.
  
  On the live store that is 13 of 135 current documents — 10 specs, 2 decisions, 1 question — and 163,127 of 520,489 characters, 31% of the text. Two of them are "Keel — Decision log" and "Keel — Status", generated rollups of rows that are already indexed one by one, so they would double-count the decision register even if they were live.
  
  **The test that looks like it covers this cannot.** `archived_rows_leave_the_index` archives a *task*. Tasks have no row in `documents` and therefore no vector, so the semantic half is never reached and the assertion holds no matter what the code does. The replacement has to archive a document-bearing type — a spec or a decision — with an embedder attached.
  
  Fix in three places: the archive filter on the passage query, the same filter on whatever backfills, and the test. Under the chunking decision the filter has a better home than a `WHERE` clause anyone can forget — archiving deletes the passages, the way archiving already removes rows from `fts_source`.

  - Correcting the framing this task was written with, including the sentence in its own body.  Both this task and the note on KEEL-131 say the keyword half is "right by construction" and only the semantic half is missing an archive filter. That is wrong, and it was wrong because I believed the comment above `search_keyword` instead of checking it. **Archived documents have been coming back from keyword search all along.**  The `_fts_archived` trigger is emitted inside the loop over the six tables whose text is their own row. The five prose types index through `documents` and were never in that loop, so they had the path into the index and no path out. Querying the live store directly:  ``` spec|Keel — Status spec|Keel — Decision log spec|Keel — Open questions and risks spec|Keel — the way forward spec|Keel — fundamental problems, for outside review ```  Every one archived, every one returned. So this is a live bug in the half that has been running for months, not a latent bug in the half that has never run.  There were three holes, not one:  1. No archive trigger for the five prose tables — archiving a spec removed it from nothing. 2. No guard on `documents_fts_ai`/`_au`. Nothing in `docs.rs` refuses a revision on an archived entity, so the next edit put it straight back, with no event and no error. 3. The backfill would have undone the archive. Archiving now clears the vector, which makes the row indistinguishable from one that was never embedded, so `reembed_missing` would have re-added it on every pass.  The vector is cleared on archive rather than filtered out in `search_semantic`, deliberately. `embedding IS NOT NULL` is already in that query, so there is no new predicate to forget — which is the thing the comment in that file warns about and the thing it turned out not to have done.  `documents_missing_embeddings` also had to exclude archived from **both** halves of its ratio. Without that, `doctor` would report "13 of 135 have no vector" permanently, since those thirteen can never be embedded again. A check that cannot go green is a check people stop reading. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>
  - Stopping short of closing this, because another session is editing the same working tree and the tree does not currently compile.  `ListAgents` shows a peer session, `keel-d9`, started 48 minutes ago. It has written B-57 ("A phase's state is derived; only shipped, cut and paused are declared") and B-58 since I wrote B-55 and B-56, and it is mid-refactor of `MilestoneStatus`: `Active` is gone from the enum while `entity.rs:2033` still calls for it, and `render_status.rs` and `lib.rs` carry imports of a name that no longer resolves. `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings` fails on three errors, none of them in anything I touched.  `git status` shows thirteen modified files. Four of them are files I also changed this session — `store/schema.rs`, `store/mod.rs`, `doctor.rs` and `tests/documents.rs` — so this is not two sessions working politely in different corners.  What is verified and what is not:  - Everything except the `doctor.rs` delegation passed a full `cargo test --workspace --no-fail-fast` run — 288 in `keel-core`, 51 in `keel-cli`, every other suite green — and migration 2 was applied to a copy of the live store, taking archived rows in `fts_source` from 13 to 0 with live rows untouched. - The `doctor.rs` change that makes `embedding_coverage` delegate to `Store::documents_missing_embeddings`, and the test that goes with it, were written after that run started and have never been through a green suite.  So the work is done and the evidence is incomplete, which is not the same as done.  One thing for whoever owns B-57: three milestone rows now hold a status the new enum cannot parse — `Phase 4 — Integrations` and `Phase 11 — Hardening` at `planned` and `active` respectively, both predating today, and `Phase 12 — Search that reads the whole document` at `active`, which I created this morning before B-57 existed. Presumably the migration covers them; noting it in case it does not. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>
  - This is now live on the real store, not just proven on copies.  Migrations 2 and 3 applied to `~/.keel/keel.sqlite`, which went from schema 1 to schema 3. Afterwards: unparseable milestone statuses 0, archived rows in the keyword index 0, shipped phases with no date 0, and all 376 entities still there.  The proof is the query that started this. This morning `"maintained" AND "claude"` returned nine specs, five of them archived — `Keel — Status`, `Keel — Decision log`, `Keel — Open questions and risks`, `Keel — the way forward`, `Keel — fundamental problems`. The same query against the running daemon now returns ten hits and not one of them is archived, while `Keel — Phase 10` is still there because it is live. A control query for `embedding` returns ten live rows, so this is the archived documents leaving rather than search having stopped working.  Worth recording how nearly that check fooled me: the first two attempts printed `0 hit(s)` for both the test *and* the control, which reads as a clean pass on the test and would have been reported as one if I had not run the control. The API wraps its payload in `data` and I was parsing the top level, so every query returned nothing. A search fix verified with a query parser that always returns empty is the same joke as the archive trigger written for six tables out of eleven.  Sequence, for anyone repeating it: stop the daemon, `keel migrate`, `./plugin/install.sh`, restart. The reinstall is not optional — the installed binary predated the migrations and the store refuses a binary older than itself, so the daemon would not have come back up without it. - **KEEL-174** Long documents are embedded on their first 1,700 characters and nothing says so `p2` · search, storage

  Found while costing KEEL-131, before running the backfill.
  
  `Document::searchable_text_of` is `title + "\n\n" + body`, and that string goes to `bge-small-en-v1.5` in one piece. The tokenizer config sets `model_max_length: 512` and fastembed takes `max_length.min(model_max_length)`, so everything past roughly 1,700 characters of English prose is dropped before the model sees it. There is no chunking anywhere in the write path or the re-embed path.
  
  Measured on the live store, current revisions only:
  
  - 55 of 135 documents (41%) are over the window — 19 of 21 specs, 17 of 57 decisions, 19 of 56 questions.
  - Technical Specification: 67,594 chars, ~2.5% embedded.
  - Status: 56,299 chars, ~3.0%.
  - Phase 8: 34,275 chars, ~5.0%.
  - Build journal: 32,131 chars, ~5.3%.
  
  Decisions and questions mostly fit — they average about 1,250 characters. Specs do not, and specs are the documents most worth finding by meaning.
  
  The failure mode is the familiar one: search returns results, they look reasonable, and nothing anywhere says the semantic half only read the opening of half the corpus.
  
  Two ways out, and they are not exclusive. The real fix is chunking: split on headings into passages under the window, embed each, and keep the best-scoring passage per document at query time. That is a schema change — one document currently owns one `embedding` column, and chunking means a row per passage — so it needs a decision first. The cheap one is a `doctor` check counting documents over the window, which costs nothing and makes the gap visible while the real fix waits.
  
  Related: TQ-3 (re-embedding when the model changes) touches the same migration path, and `embedding_model` / `embedding_version` are already on the row.

- **KEEL-170** A task with a summary but no body shows "No description." in the app `p2` · bug, desktop

  `apps/desktop/src/screens/Task.tsx:232` is `task.body ? <Markdown/> : <Empty message="No description." />`. The string `task.summary` does not appear anywhere in that file — zero occurrences.
  
  Both fields are legal and both are used, which is why this went unnoticed:
  
  - `body` is optional, and its own tool description calls it "the short detail field — anything long-form belongs in a spec".
  - `summary` is **required** since 8G, and is described as what a colleague reads cold.
  
  So a session that fills only the required field produces rows that are complete by the store's rules and blank on the screen. Measured across the store: 145 tasks predating 8G have a body and no summary, and 31 Phase 11 tasks have a summary and no body. Both render as half a page — the older ones show a description and no summary anywhere, the newer ones show "No description."
  
  Worth reading the comment that sits fifteen lines above the bug, about the partial-load banner: "a page that quietly does less while looking complete is the failure this project treats as the serious one." That is this, in the same component.
  
  Suggested shape: render `body` when present, fall back to `summary`, and label which one it is showing rather than presenting them as the same thing. The list and board views already show `summary`, which is why these tasks look normal until you open one.

  - A cost this tier introduced, found later by mutation testing and worth knowing before adding more end-to-end tests: the daemon-start wait is 30 seconds and the stream reads are 5–10. Those are generous so the tests do not flake, and the price is that a *broken* daemon takes the full timeout to fail rather than failing fast. Under `cargo mutants` that turned 24 of 113 mutants into timeouts instead of verdicts — nearly a fifth of a two-hour run spent learning nothing — and it means a real CI failure would surface in minutes rather than seconds. Recorded properly on KEEL-173.  Separately, and the reason two health tests failed for a day: the *first* HTTP request a test process makes costs 12.7 seconds on macOS, against 0.9 milliseconds for the second. That is reqwest building its client and reading the system trust store. Any test that wraps its first request in a timeout is measuring that rather than the daemon. `health.rs` now warms the client in its fixture and the numbers are in its module doc. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-12</sub>

- **KEEL-145** Build the fault-injection testkit for the resilience regression tests `p1`

  - The authorizer fires at *prepare* time, not execute. Keel's write path uses `Connection::execute`, which prepares every call, so "the nth insert" and "the nth prepare of an insert" are the same number — but a future caller that prepares once and steps many times would see the fault once and look like it never fired. That is what `FaultCounter::assert_fired` is for. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-12</sub>

- **KEEL-144** Fix the oldest-first event reads that already truncate history today `p1`

  - `EventScope` is an enum rather than two `Option` parameters because "no project and no entity" and "every project" would otherwise be the same call with two meanings.  `changes::by_session` was the subtle one. It merges notes into the events afterwards, so asking for exactly `limit` events would be too few — a note can displace an event before the cut. It asks for ten times the limit, floored at 1,000, which cannot plausibly be displaced, and `truncated` still reports honestly.  `recent_activity` in the digest keeps using the forward feed when a `since` is supplied: that is a bounded window, so reading it forwards cannot lose the newest rows. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-12</sub>

- **KEEL-143** Close the two-writer footgun: lock-free health, honest fallback, probe before direct write `p1`

  - The probe is a TCP connect rather than a GET /api/health, and that is the load-bearing choice. ureq reports connection-refused and a read timeout as the same `Transport` error kind, so distinguishing them means digging into the io error's source — whereas `TcpStream::connect_timeout` answers the actual question ("is anything holding this port") directly and cannot be confused by a slow handler.  "Routed through the same write funnel" is implemented as one guarded open, not as routing the commands through the daemon's MCP API. `note`, `archive` and `task add` still write via keel-core when no daemon is running, which is legitimate and is the case the CLI exists for; what changed is that they now refuse when a daemon is up. Sending them over HTTP instead would be a larger change and would make the CLI useless without a daemon.  `--force` is global on the Cli struct rather than per-subcommand, because adding it to five variants that already have three flags each would have been worse. It is a flag and not an environment variable so that using it shows up in a shell history. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-12</sub>
  - The health tests as first written were flaky. They held the store lock for 600ms and asserted health returned inside 400ms; that passed in isolation and failed once under a full `cargo test --workspace`, where the machine is loaded enough that 400ms of wall clock means nothing.  Rewritten in f9f75e9 to hold the lock until the test releases it, with channels for the handover and a two-second timeout as the assertion. Deterministic in both directions: without the fix, health waits on a lock nothing will free and the timeout always fires.  Worth remembering as a pattern — a wall-clock budget tight enough to be meaningful is tight enough to be flaky under load, and a test that cries wolf teaches people to re-run rather than look. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-12</sub>

- **KEEL-142** Confine entity-controlled file paths to the project, in keel-core `p1`

  - Resolving both sides is not optional and it is easy to get half right. On macOS a `tempfile::tempdir()` lives under `/var`, which is a symlink to `/private/var` — so canonicalising the target and comparing it against an unresolved root refuses every legitimate path in every test. The failure would have looked exactly like the check working.  `resolve_existing` canonicalises the deepest ancestor that exists and re-appends the rest, because the file about to be generated does not exist yet and `canonicalize` fails outright on a missing tail.  One residual risk, deliberately left: `root_path` is only checked for shape, not resolved. A caller that sets it to `/` would make generate write `product/STATUS.md` at the filesystem root. That fails on permissions for a non-root user and there is no `..` involved, so it is a much narrower hole than the one closed here — but it is not closed. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-12</sub>

- **KEEL-141** Make entity writes atomic, and emit the missing revision event `p1`

  - The Revised event takes its provenance from the `Document` (author, session_id, surface) rather than from a new parameter on `write_revision`. Those three were already decided at the boundary and recorded on the revision, so taking them from anywhere else would let a revision and its event disagree about who wrote it — and it kept the trait signature unchanged, which mattered because `DocumentStore::write_revision` has four callers.  Worth knowing for whoever reads the changelog next: every existing revision in the store predates this, so the changelog is only complete from here forward. Backfilling would mean inventing events with timestamps taken from `documents.created_at`, which is possible but is history nobody observed. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-12</sub>

- **KEEL-139** Run the deep engineering review and expert panel; land the prioritized hardening backlog `p1`

  - Review round 1 complete — all six dimension reviewers reported; full reports saved in scratchpad/review-{architecture,codequality,security,performance,resilience,test-qa}.md. Headline convergence: the entity write path (create/update/archive/link/unlink in keel-core/src/store/entity.rs) runs multiple SQL statements with NO transaction, while write_revision (docs.rs:311-350) is properly transactional. Flagged HIGH independently by architecture (A1), code-quality (C1) and resilience (R1); a crash between the row INSERT and the event append leaves a row with no event, and the idempotency retry returns created:false before ever writing the event, so the event is permanently lost — silent history loss, and fsck has no check for it. Security found a real HIGH (S1): entity-controlled mirror_path/status_path/root_path get no path validation and are joined onto repo root at `keel generate`, so a prompt-injected write can plant attacker content at e.g. ~/.zshenv → code execution; the orphan-delete path was already hardened (mirror.rs:394) but the adopted-write path (generate.rs:213) was not. Also HIGH from resilience: single-writer rule has zero enforcement (R2 CLI falls back to direct store write on timeout; R3 no lock file/health probe). Other notable: document revisions append no event (Action::Revised is dead code, A2); proptest is declared-but-unused (T1/C3); P1 "read oldest-first, take tail" is already wrong today at 804 events for the 409 StaleVersion payload; no PRAGMA integrity_check anywhere (R6); mutex-under-load starvation (R7); 0 of 234 docs embedded with no backfill path (P6/R11). - **KEEL-134** The live store is still DuckDB — Phase 9 shipped in the tree but never on this machine `p1` · phase9, ops

  Found while working KEEL-132, by checking where a write actually landed.
  
  `~/.keel` holds `keel.duckdb` (40 MB), `keel.duckdb.wal` and `lance/`. There is no `keel.sqlite`. The running daemon is `~/.local/bin/keel-daemon`, installed 2026-08-11 21:04 — before `37a2341`, which is the commit where everything moved to SQLite. `~/.local/bin/keel` is the matching CLI, and its help still reads:
  
  ```
  backup         Back up both engines to Parquet
  ```
  
  So the tree is SQLite-only — `Cargo.lock` has no `duckdb` entry at all — while the store this project runs its own life on is the one Phase 9 replaced. `keel.duckdb.wal` had been written to seconds before this was noticed, by this session's own `keel_claim`.
  
  Nothing is broken and nothing is lost. What it means is narrower and worth stating exactly: **Phase 9's exit criteria are met by the code and not by the machine.** "`keel migrate` on the real store, verified by row count per table and hash per document" was run and verified, but against a copy — the live store was never cut over. Anyone reading STATUS.md would reasonably think otherwise.
  
  Two consequences for whoever picks this up:
  
  - **Do not run a CLI built from the current tree against `~/.keel` before migrating.** It will look for `keel.sqlite`, not find one, and create an empty store. `keel generate` would then rewrite every file in the repo from nothing. Use the installed binary until the cutover is done.
  - The measurements that justified Phase 9 — the search stall in particular — are still being paid on this machine, so anything measured here before the cutover is measuring the old engine.
  
  The sequence is `keel backup` on the old binary, stop the daemon, `keel migrate`, install the new binaries, start, and check that a write reaches `keel.sqlite`.

  - Cutover done. The sequence in this row's body was followed exactly, with one addition: an inventory taken from the backup's Parquet files before anything was written, so the migration could be checked against something that does not go through the DuckDB bindings.  - `keel backup` on the old binary — 1431 rows to `~/.keel/backups/2026-08-12T00-06-47Z`. - Daemon stopped. It checkpointed its 4 MB WAL and exited clean, so the file migrated from was quiescent. - Raw copy of `keel.duckdb` and `lance/` taken with a SHA-256, as insurance for the migration window. - `keel migrate` from a throwaway build at ab66018: **1599 rows across 18 tables, 234 document hashes and 1 blob hash — identical**. - Verified a third time by counting every table with `sqlite3` and diffing against the Parquet inventory. All eighteen agree. `pragma integrity_check` — ok. - New binaries built from this worktree and installed.  The store is 40 MB of DuckDB plus a Lance directory down to a **4.6 MB** SQLite file. `keel` went from 44 MB to 6.8 MB and `keel-daemon` from 75 MB to 38 MB, which is the vendored DuckDB leaving the binary.  This note is itself the check that a write reaches `keel.sqlite`: it went through the new daemon over MCP.  One thing the inventory settled on the way past: 234 documents and **zero** embeddings, straight out of the Parquet. That is KEEL-131 confirmed from the data rather than inferred — the daemon was started without `--embeddings`, so it stays true until someone runs it. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-12</sub>
  - How to go back, if it ever matters. The installed `keel` can no longer read DuckDB, so the recovery path is not obvious enough to leave unwritten:  1. `git worktree add <dir> ab66018` — the last commit that has both engines and `keel migrate`. 2. `CARGO_PROFILE_DEV_DEBUG=0 cargo build -p keel-cli` — about five and a half minutes, 2 GB of target, and it statically links its own DuckDB so it cannot mismatch the file it reads. 3. `restore` from `~/.keel/backups/2026-08-12T00-06-47Z`, which is the pre-cutover Parquet of both engines.  Do not use the brew DuckDB for this. `duckdb` 1.5.5 is on this machine and `keel-core` at that commit has a `bundled` feature you can turn off to link a system library — but the bindings are for a different version, and `migrate --verify-only` reads *and* writes through them, so a systematic misread would verify clean. Building the bundled one is the whole point.  The brew CLI is still the right tool for one thing, and it is what made this cutover checkable: Parquet is version-independent, so `duckdb -c "select count(*) from read_parquet(...)"` over the backup gives an inventory that does not go through the Rust bindings at all. That is what the eighteen row counts were checked against.  Two things found while verifying, both now moot but worth having said:  - The **old** binary's backup manifest omitted `notes` from its counts — 1431 reported against 1615 actual, the difference being 168 notes and the 16 migration rows. `notes.parquet` was written correctly, so no data was at risk; only the number was wrong. The new backup counts all 18 tables and reports 1600, so the bug went out with the code path. - A post-cutover backup is at `~/.keel/backups/2026-08-12T00-25-15Z` and is a plain `keel.sqlite` from `VACUUM INTO`, restorable with the binary that is actually installed. The old Parquet one needs step 1 above first, which is the argument for having taken this one. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-12</sub>

- **KEEL-133** keel ready says "nothing ready" when no daemon is listening `p2` · bug, cli

  Reproduces on the pre-Phase-9 binary against the DuckDB store, so it is not a Phase 9 regression. Found while verifying KEEL-129.
  
  In `crates/keel-cli/src/work.rs`, `ready()` gets `structured` from one of two places. `call_daemon` unwraps `structuredContent` before returning it. The direct fallback — `directly(...)` plus `keel_mcp::dispatch` — returns the whole tool-result envelope `{content, isError, structuredContent}`. The prose renderer then does `structured.get("ready")`, which is `None` for the envelope, so it falls into the `items.is_empty()` branch and prints "nothing ready".

  - All three work verbs had the mismatch, not just `ready`. `claim` and `close` read `reference` off the response and fell back to the argument when it was missing, so daemonless they printed whatever you typed — `keel claim tsk_01KZ… ` answered with the ULID and an empty title, and `close` never mentioned the `duplicates`/`supersedes` edge it had just drawn. `ready` was the loud one only because its missing field is a list, and an absent list renders as "nothing ready".  `lint` is clean: it reads through `read_daemon`, has no fallback at all, and bails when nothing is listening. There is no `keel activity` command — the activity feed is read over `/api/activity` in gate.rs, which unwraps `data` itself.  The root cause was two copies of "call the daemon, else open the store": `ready` had its own and it was the one missing the unwrap. There is now one `run_tool`, and the unwrap lives inside `directly` so a future caller cannot forget it. `keel_mcp::structured` is the single implementation, shared with the daemon's `as_api`, which had a third copy of the same two lines. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - Verifying this against the real store is not currently possible and that is worth knowing before someone tries. `~/.keel` still holds `keel.duckdb`, the daemon on 7654 is the binary installed at 21:03 on 2026-08-11 — before SQLite landed — and the current tree cannot read DuckDB at all. Commit 8150c26 already says the cutover has not happened.  So the daemonless path was checked against a store bootstrapped into a temp home instead: `keel bootstrap --home <tmp>` then `keel ready keel --daemon <dead port>` prints five rows where it used to print "nothing ready", and `claim` and `close` by ULID both answer with `KEEL-36` and its title.  One side effect to know about: running the daemonless path with the default home created an empty `~/.keel/keel.sqlite`, because `Store::open` creates and migrates a store that is not there. It holds no rows, so the deleted migration would not have refused it as a populated target — but a fallback that silently creates an empty store, and then says "no project matches `keel`", is a worse answer than "there is no store at this path". <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-132** SPEC.md sections 1 to 7 still describe the two-engine store `p2` · docs, phase9

  Phase 9's removal covered the decision log — D-1 is marked superseded by D-1a with the measurements that overturned it. It did not cover the body of the document, and the body is where the design is actually described.
  
  What is now wrong rather than merely dated: the architecture diagram, the storage layout in §2 and §3, the search design in §5 which describes `lance_hybrid_search` and a BM25 index that is rebuilt, and the backup format in §11 which describes a Parquet export plus a Lance dump.
  
  This is worse than a gap. A missing section reads as missing; a detailed description of a system that no longer exists reads as current, and it is the first thing a new session or a new person is pointed at.
  
  **Mark what changed rather than silently rewriting it.** The same treatment D-1 got: the old design was correct when it was written, and the reasoning that replaced it is only legible beside what it replaced. A spec that reads as though it always said SQLite loses the most interesting thing about the project's history.
  
  Worth pairing with a read of `PHASE-9.md`, which already contains the survey, the measurements and the fourteen-candidate comparison — much of what §2 and §5 need is written, in the right words, in another document.

  - §13 was left untouched on purpose and now needs KB. Six rows in the decision table argue from something that is gone: D-1a's closing clause says "§3 onwards still describes the old shape", which this revision made false; D-2 and D-2b say "dataset" where they mean a table; D-4's rationale is the DuckDB 1.5.x/DuckPGQ version conflict; D-5's is Quack; D-6's is "storage engines are Rust-native", which is now a C amalgamation compiled into the binary. Every one of those decisions still reaches the right conclusion — that is the interesting part — so nothing was quietly reworded to match. A blockquote under the table says which rows and why, and TQ-37 asks KB what to do about it. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-131** Semantic search has never run — there are 227 documents and no embeddings `p2` · storage, daemon, search

  Found while verifying the Phase 9 migration. The store holds 227 documents and **not one has an embedding**; `~/.keel/models` is empty, so the model was never fetched.
  
  Nothing is broken in the sense of erroring. Search has always returned results. They have always come from the keyword index alone, and nothing anywhere says the other half is not running — which is exactly the silent degradation the search work was written to avoid, arriving from a different direction.
  
  This is not something the migration lost. The verification compares embedding width per document on both sides and found no difference: there was nothing there to drop.
  
  Two parts, and the second is the slow one.
  
  **Attach an embedder where the store is opened.** `SqliteStore::with_embedder` exists and `search` now consults it — the gap is that the daemon never passes one. `DuckStore` has the same shape and the same gap, so this is not a regression introduced by the move.
  
  **Backfill the 227 documents.** A pass over every current revision, embedding the body and writing the vector. Slow the first time because the model has to download, and worth a progress line rather than a silent wait. TQ-3 is the related open question — what happens when the model *changes* — and this does not settle it; it only fills what is empty.
  
  Done when a search whose answer shares no words with the query returns the right document, and `SearchSource::Semantic` appears in a real result rather than only in a test.

  - The machinery for this landed in b26805f (KEEL-151): `keel reembed --missing`, a startup warning naming the count, and a background model load so the first download does not block the listener.  **The backfill itself has not been run**, and this task should stay open until it has. Two reasons it was left: the running daemon holds the store, and `keel reembed` now refuses a direct write while a daemon is listening — correctly, since a second writer is exactly what KEEL-143 closed. And the daemon needs restarting onto the new binary anyway before it will attach an embedder at all.  The sequence, when someone is at the machine:  ``` keel doctor            # names the count: 133 current documents, none with a vector # restart the daemon onto the new binary, with embeddings enabled keel reembed --missing # first run downloads ~130MB keel doctor            # embeddings should now read ok ``` <sub>— claude · 2026-08-12</sub>

The semantic half is that forgotten filter. `search_semantic` reads `documents` directly with `WHERE status = 'current' AND embedding IS NOT NULL AND length(embedding) = ?` and no archive predicate, and `reembed_missing` has none either. So a backfill run today would embed every archived document and hand them to the fusion, where BM25 correctly refuses to return them and cosine happily does.  Measured on the live store: **13 of 135 current documents belong to archived entities — 10 specs, 2 decisions, 1 question — and they are 163,127 of 520,489 characters, 31% of the text.** Among them are "Keel — Decision log" (26,840 chars) and "Keel — Status" (56,299), which are generated rollups of rows that are already indexed individually, so embedding them would double-count the whole decision register even if they were live.   **Error variants.** `error.rs` has storage errors phrased in DuckDB's terms. An error that names an engine nobody is running is worse than a generic one.

  **The desktop app.** `Home.tsx` and two test files mention the engines in user-facing or fixture text.
  
  **`deny.toml`**, if either engine has an entry there.
  
  **The contract itself.** `product/CLAUDE.md` names the three traits as "DuckDB entity CRUD" and "Lance documents and blobs", hard constraint 1 says "no other process writes to DuckDB", and the definition of done has a note about the vendored DuckDB filling the disk. It was not the size; it was the house-style gate refusing a banned word in the existing text.  Revision 5 restores the full document with D-1 superseded, so the current revision and every generated file are correct. Nothing was lost, because revisions are append-only and revision 3 is still readable. But `keel_get` with `version: 4` will return five bytes, and a diff across that boundary looks alarming.   - **A sequencing mistake worth recording, because it was mine and it is the kind that is easy to repeat.**  I briefed this task to delete `keel migrate` along with the engine it reads, on the reasoning that its job was done — it had run and verified clean. That reasoning was wrong in one word. It had run against a **byte-identical copy** of the live store, not the live store. Copying proves the code; it does not perform the cutover.  So the live `~/.keel` is still DuckDB, still served by the previously installed daemon binary, and the only tool that can move it now lives one commit back at `37a23412b97661f5bdc91d550b986730c06a5176`. That is workable and it is written into the commit message, but it is a worse position than doing it in the other order, and there was no reason to do it in this one.  The general shape: **"verified on a copy" and "done" are not the same claim, and a removal that happens between them strands the thing that closes the gap.** The migration should have been the last thing deleted, after the store it exists to move had actually moved.  Nothing is lost and nothing is at risk — the old store is untouched and working, the branch is unmerged, and the migration is one `git checkout` away. It cost a worse cutover procedure rather than any data.  **What the cutover actually needs, for whoever does it:** stop the daemon, build from `37a2341`, run `keel migrate` against `~/.keel`, confirm the verification is clean, install the binaries built from this branch, restart. The safety copy taken at the start of this session is at `/tmp/keel-backup-before-migration` and already contains a migrated `keel.sqlite`, so the first run has a known-good result to compare against. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-129** 9C — Every surface still works, checked rather than assumed `p1` · phase9, 9c, testing

  The test suite is the migration specification and it is not the whole story. Tests run against a fixture; the real store has 122 tasks, 62 documents, seven sessions of events and one design image, and it is where the awkward rows live.
  
  What gets exercised against the migrated store, by hand, once:
  
  `keel generate keel` and then `keel generate keel --check` — the check has to be clean, which proves every renderer reads the new store identically.
  
  `keel render-status`, `keel fsck`, `keel lint`, `keel ready`, `keel claim`, `keel close`, `keel note`, `keel backup` and `keel restore`.
  
  The daemon: every route under `/api`, the MCP endpoint, and the SSE event stream, which is the one that depends on the event cursor staying monotonic.
  
  The app: every screen, with the browser console watched for errors. The Library's design thumbnail is the blob path end to end, and search is the screen most likely to be subtly wrong rather than broken — a fusion with an inverted sign returns results in a plausible-looking order.
  
  The two snapshot suites are the contract and their diffs get read rather than accepted. A snapshot that changes because the store changed is a bug: the MCP surface is not supposed to know what is underneath it.
  
  The measurement from KEEL-123 gets re-run here, same script, and the before and after go on the milestone.

  - The switchover found two things the SQLite store was missing, both silent.  **`write_revision` never embedded.** `DuckStore::write_revision` computed a vector from the store's embedder on the way in; the SQLite one wrote whatever `document.embedding` already held, which is always `None` for every caller. So with the SQLite store attached to a real embedder, no document would ever get a vector and semantic search would return nothing — while keyword results kept arriving and nothing said the other half had stopped. `documents.rs::embeddings_are_written_when_an_embedder_is_attached` caught it. The fix is the same block DuckDB had, ported.  **No older-binary guard.** DuckDB refused to open a store whose migration ledger is ahead of the binary — the check written after KEEL-95, where a daemon 84 seconds behind wrote a NULL into a column it did not know about. SQLite's `migrate()` just skipped ahead and opened. `roundtrip.rs::a_binary_older_than_the_store_refuses_to_open_it` caught it. Ported, with the same message.  Both are the case for repointing the existing tests rather than writing new ones: neither gap is something a test written against the new store would have thought to look for. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - The KEEL-123 measurement, re-run on the same terms: debug build, a copy of the real store, 20 rounds. Before is the second table in KEEL-123's note, which was taken exactly this way.  ```                      DuckDB      SQLite tasks                31.2ms      16.8ms milestones           19.8ms       1.8ms note counts          14.2ms       2.9ms ranking + blocked   508.0ms      23.6ms digest depth=full   999.0ms      46.0ms every note body      19.8ms       8.6ms search               45.9ms       2.9ms   (max 3.5ms) board load serial   573ms        45.1ms ```  The one worth pointing at is search's *max*, not its mean. DuckDB's full-text index does not track inserts, so the first search after any write rebuilt the whole thing; FTS5 is maintained by triggers, so there is nothing to rebuild and the worst sample is 3.5 ms against a 2.9 ms mean. The stall KEEL-123 went looking for does not exist any more rather than being smaller.  Payload is unchanged, as expected — the same JSON either way. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - Two things the switchover changes that are nobody's bug and both worth knowing.  **The single write path is now a convention, not a lock.** `keel note add` opens the store directly rather than going through the daemon. Under DuckDB that failed while the daemon was up — the lock refused it — so hard constraint 1 was enforced by the engine. In WAL mode a second writer just works, and I watched it work: a note written from the CLI landed in a store the daemon had open. Nothing was lost, because SQLite serialises the two writers properly. But the constraint now holds only because the code happens to be arranged that way, and the six non-locking steps of a Keel write — validation, provenance, the event, the revision, the embedding, the index — are exactly what a second writer can skip.  **Search returns the same documents in a slightly different order.** Same query, same corpus, both keyword-only: the result sets match, the ranking does not, because BM25 term statistics come from the index and DuckDB's FTS and FTS5 tokenise differently. Not a fusion sign error — the semantic half was returning nothing on both, since neither store has embeddings yet (KEEL-131). <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - The switchover landed and every surface was exercised against a migrated copy of the real store rather than against a fixture. 696 workspace tests pass, 220 desktop tests pass, and **both insta snapshot suites have zero diffs** — which is the result worth having on its own: the MCP surface is byte-identical across an engine swap, so it never knew what was underneath it. That is the Phase 0 trait boundary being collected on.  `keel generate keel --check` is clean, and 66 of the 67 generated files are byte-identical to the DuckDB-generated ones once the timestamp banner is stripped. The one difference is `product/STATUS.md`, and it is real state change rather than drift.  **Two silent regressions the repointed tests caught**, both of the same shape — code that keeps working and quietly does less:  The SQLite store never computed embeddings on write. `DuckStore::write_revision` embedded from the store's embedder; the SQLite one only stored a vector a caller supplied, and no caller supplies one. Semantic search would have been dead in the way that returns keyword results and reports nothing.  The older-binary guard was missing. DuckDB refused to open a store whose migration ledger runs ahead of the binary — the check written after KEEL-95, where a daemon 84 seconds behind wrote NULLs into a column it did not know about. SQLite opened it happily.  Neither needed a new test. Both fell out of repointing existing ones, which is the argument for repointing rather than rewriting.  **The performance criterion, re-measured on the same script and the same store copy:** board load **573 ms → 45.1 ms** serial; ranking and blocked 508 → 23.6 ms; digest 999 → 46 ms; search 45.9 → **2.9 ms with a max of 3.5 ms**. The BM25 rebuild stall is gone rather than reduced — the max is now within a hair of the mean, which is what "no rebuild" looks like from outside.  **One consequence to be aware of rather than pleased about.** In WAL mode a second process can open the store while the daemon holds it. `keel note add` opens it directly and now simply works; DuckDB's lock used to refuse. **Hard constraint 1 — the daemon owns the single write path — is now a convention rather than something the engine enforces.** Nothing was lost, and the six of seven reasons for the single write path are unchanged, but the guard rail that made violating it impossible is gone. Worth a decision on whether to enforce it some other way or to accept it.  Two smaller things found: search returns the same documents in a slightly different order, which is two BM25 implementations and two tokenisers rather than a fusion sign error — the semantic half returns nothing on *both* stores because neither has embeddings (KEEL-131). And `keel ready` prints "nothing ready" with no daemon running while `--json` shows the tasks; confirmed pre-existing against the stashed pre-Phase-9 binary, so it is not a regression from this work. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-128** 9B — Backup becomes one file, and restore stops needing two halves `p1` · phase9, 9b, storage, cli

  `VACUUM INTO`, measured at 1.2 ms against a live database. It takes a consistent snapshot without stopping the daemon, which the current two-format export cannot promise: the DuckDB export and the Lance dump are two operations, and a write landing between them produces a backup that is internally inconsistent and passes every check.
  
  The backup round-trip test already exists and already asserts equality rather than eyeballing it — back up, wipe, restore, diff. It keeps working, and it gets shorter.
  
  The half of `restore` that refuses a backup missing its Lance directory is deleted, not left in place returning false. Dead validation of a condition that can no longer occur reads as a live safety check to whoever finds it next.
  
  Worth checking while here: whether anything in `~/.keel/backups` is in the old two-part format, and what a person with old backups is told when they try to restore one. Refusing clearly beats reading it wrongly.

- **KEEL-127** 9B — keel migrate: read the old store, write a new one, prove they match `p0` · phase9, 9b, storage, cli

  It never modifies the old store. It reads and writes a new file, so a failed migration costs nothing and can be retried.
  
  KEEL-95 is the live lesson this is written against: a migration added a column, a daemon 84 seconds behind wrote a NULL into it, and every decision in another project became unreadable. B-44 came out of it — a column added by a migration is NULL for every writer that has not restarted yet. A whole-engine migration is that hazard at its largest, which is why nothing is done in place and why the daemon has to be stopped first.
  
  **Verification is the deliverable, not the copy.** Per table, a row count on both sides. Per document, a content hash on both sides. Per blob, a byte hash. It reuses the assertion the backup round-trip test already makes rather than inventing a second way to say the same thing. If anything differs, it names what and exits non-zero.
  
  Things that are easy to get silently wrong in a copy like this and each deserve their own check:
  
  Archived rows. Soft delete means nothing is ever gone, so a migration that skips `archived_at IS NOT NULL` loses the audit trail and every count still matches on the live rows.
  
  Links, both stored directions. Only `blocks` is ever stored and `depends_on` is swapped on write. A migration that re-normalises on the way in would double-swap and invert the whole graph.
  
  The event log and its ordering, since the cursor has to stay monotonic across the move.
  
  Embeddings. They can be regenerated, but a run of `fastembed` over the whole corpus is slow and pointless if the vectors copy cleanly — copy them, and verify by dimension and a sample hash.
  
  Task numbers and ranks. `KEEL-42` means the same thing forever and a migration that renumbers breaks every reference in every document.

  - The verification only means something if it recomputes. Comparing the old store's body_hash column against the new one's is vacuous: the column is copied verbatim, so it agrees with itself whatever happened to the prose beside it. verify hashes the title and body that actually landed and compares that against the hash the old store recorded, and does the same for blob bytes. The two tests that prove it edit the target's body and bytes while leaving the stored hashes alone, and both were caught. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - One legacy shape the new schema cannot hold, so the migration checks for it before writing anything: two tasks or two decisions in the same project with a NULL number. Both read back as 0, and SQLite's unique index over (project_id, number) treats NULLs as distinct but two zeroes as a collision. Left alone it would fail halfway through the copy with `UNIQUE constraint failed: tasks.project_id, tasks.number`, which names the index and not the cause. Two queries up front turn that into a sentence saying which project to fix. This is KEEL-95 and B-44 again, one size larger. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - Two things about the SQLite schema changed under this task while it was being written, and both turned a planned loss into no loss at all. The events table gained summary and meta, so migrated history keeps the sentence a person reads instead of rendering blank in the changelog and the activity feed. And the keyword index became external-content FTS5 maintained by triggers on the tables themselves, which means a row-level copy that never touches the write path still arrives with a populated index — and archived rows stay out of it, because the same triggers say so. There is now a test asserting a migrated row is findable by keyword. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - Left in_progress rather than closed, deliberately. The library half is finished and tested — keel_core::migrate::{migrate, verify} in crates/keel-core/src/migrate.rs, seventeen tests green under `cargo test -p keel-core --lib migrate`. What this task asks for and does not yet have is the `keel migrate` command wired into the CLI, and a run against the real store with the row counts and content hashes checked by the command rather than by eye. That was held back on purpose: the CLI is being edited elsewhere this session. Whoever picks it up needs a CLI subcommand that opens the old store, opens a fresh SQLite file beside it, calls migrate then verify, prints the report and exits non-zero when it is not clean. Stop the daemon first — DuckDB has one write lock. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - Run against the real data, and it is clean. Not the fixture — a byte-identical copy of the live `~/.keel`, taken first so the running daemon never had to stop and a failed run could cost nothing.  **1,514 rows across 18 tables, 227 document hashes and 1 blob hash, all matching. 1.9 seconds.** That is the exit criterion: verified by row count per table and hash per document, by the command rather than by eye.  The store goes from **40 MB to 4.3 MB** — 34 MB of DuckDB and 5.5 MB of Lance become one 4.3 MB file.  Spot-checked afterwards rather than trusting the report alone. All 137 task numbers survived and `KEEL-124` still reads "9B — The SQLite store: schema, EntityStore and GraphStore", which is the thing that must never drift because it is cited in prose nothing rewrites. All 741 events kept their summary. 48 `blocks` links, **0 `depends_on`** — the graph was copied verbatim rather than re-normalised, which would have double-swapped it and inverted every edge. 294 rows in the keyword index, populated by the triggers during a copy that never touched the write path.  **The finding worth acting on, and it is about the live system rather than the migration: there are 227 documents and not one of them has an embedding.**  `~/.keel/models` is empty, so the embedding model was never downloaded. That means the semantic half of hybrid search has never run on this store — every result Keel has ever returned came from the keyword index alone. The verification compares embedding width per document on both sides and reported no difference, so this is not something the migration dropped; there was nothing there to drop.  It matters more now than it did, because it is exactly the failure mode the search work was warned about: results keep arriving, they are merely worse, and nothing in the output says half the system is not running. Worth its own task — the fix is to attach an embedder where the daemon opens the store and backfill the 227 documents, and neither belongs in this one. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-126** 9B — Search on FTS5 and sqlite-vec, with an index that does not need rebuilding `p0` · phase9, 9b, storage

  This is the one part of the migration that is not a straight substitution, and it is also the part that fixes the stall in KEEL-123.
  
  **The keyword half.** FTS5 with triggers on the source tables, so the index updates when a row changes. That is the whole point: today `refresh_entity_index` runs `CREATE OR REPLACE TABLE fts_entities` over every searchable table and then rebuilds the BM25 index from scratch, because DuckDB's full-text index does not update when its table changes. Measured cost of that: 217 ms on the first search after a write, against a 13 ms mean.
  
  FTS5 has its own `bm25()` scoring function, which returns a negative number where lower is better. The fusion currently expects higher-is-better. Getting that sign wrong ranks the worst match first and looks like a plausible ordering, so it needs a test that asserts an obviously-best document comes first rather than one that asserts a score.
  
  **The vector half.** `sqlite-vec`, brute force over a few thousand vectors, measured at 1–3 ms. It is 0.1.9 and its author says to expect breaking changes. That is survivable here because the vectors are stored as ordinary blobs rather than inside a proprietary index — replacing the search is about fifty lines, and the embeddings do not have to be regenerated to do it. Worth writing that into the doc comment so the next person knows the escape route exists.
  
  **The fusion stays.** Reciprocal rank, because BM25 scores and vector distances are not on comparable scales. `k_inner = k_outer * 4` stays too — retrieving exactly k from the index and filtering by project afterwards is how you return three results when forty exist.
  
  `SearchSource::Keyword` currently documents itself as "the DuckDB BM25 index" and `Semantic` as "the Lance vector index". Both need rewording, and `Both` — an independent keyword and semantic match agreeing — stays the strongest signal available.

  - The vector half is `sqlite-vec`'s `vec_distance_cosine` scalar function over the `documents.embedding` column, scanned in full — no `vec0` table. A `vec0` table is a second copy of every vector and needs a trigger in the schema to stay in step; without that trigger the only way to use it is to repopulate at search time, which is the rebuild this task removes wearing a different hat. Brute force over a few thousand vectors is 1-3 ms and cannot go stale.  One trap found while building it: `vec_distance_cosine` raises an error when the two vectors differ in length, and that error fails the whole query, so a single document left behind by a model change would take out semantic search for everything. `length(embedding) = ?` in the WHERE clause skips those rows instead. There is a test for it. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - `SqliteStore` has no embedder, so the trait's `search` is keyword-only today. The struct is `{ conn, path }` and adding a field means editing `mod.rs`, which this task was scoped away from. The semantic half is reachable now through `SqliteStore::search_with(&query, Some(&embedder))`, and the fix is the three lines `DuckStore` already has: an `embedder: Option<Arc<dyn Embedder>>` field, `with_embedder`, and an `embedder()` accessor. Then `search` passes `self.embedder()` instead of `None`.  Worth doing before the daemon switches stores, because the failure is silent: search would keep returning results, just keyword ones, and nothing in the output would say the semantic half had stopped running. The doc comment on `search` says so too. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-125** 9B — DocumentStore on SQLite: revisions, blobs in the same transaction `p0` · phase9, 9b, storage

  Revisions become a table. Blobs become a table with a `BLOB` column, which is the change that removes the awkward part of the current design: an image and the artifact it belongs to are written in one transaction rather than in two stores that can disagree.
  
  Measured in the Phase 9 survey: a 5 MB blob writes in 49.6 ms and reads in 10.8 ms. That is fast enough that incremental blob I/O is not needed and should not be reached for.
  
  The revision chain is the thing with a property test on it already, and it stays: version numbers are assigned by the store, the previous revision stays readable by version, and writing content identical to the current revision is a no-op rather than a new version.
  
  Two behaviours that are easy to lose in a translation and are load-bearing:
  
  `write_revision` returns the document with whatever version the store actually assigned, not the one the caller hoped for. Callers depend on that.
  
  `diff` is a store operation, not a UI one. It satisfies REQ-2 at the API layer, so it stays in the trait rather than moving into a renderer.
  
  Search is not in this task — it is the next one, because it is the only part where SQLite is not a straight substitution.

  - Two things the next person needs. First, `store/sqlite/docs.rs` has no `impl DocumentStore for SqliteStore` — the trait needs `search`, which is KEEL-126, so the six methods are inherent methods on `SqliteStore` with the trait's exact names. Whoever lands search writes the impl block as six one-line delegations, and each one must use the fully-qualified form `SqliteStore::revision(self, …)`; `self.revision(…)` inside the impl resolves to the trait method being written and recurses until the stack goes.  Second, the timestamp format `%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S%.6fZ` is written out a second time in docs.rs because `rows::ts` and `rows::parse_ts` are private to that module and this task could only touch one file. Making them `pub(super)` and deleting the copy is a five-minute cleanup; there is a test in docs.rs holding the fixed-width and sort properties in the meantime.  Measured on this machine, file-backed store in WAL: a 5 MB blob writes in 55.2 ms and reads in 2.1 ms. The survey said 49.6 ms and 10.8 ms, so the write matches and the read is faster than expected — nothing here needs incremental blob I/O. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-124** 9B — The SQLite store: schema, EntityStore and GraphStore `p0` · phase9, 9b, storage

  `rusqlite` with the bundled amalgamation. The schema in `store/schema.rs` is 937 lines of DuckDB DDL and most of it translates directly — the work is in the places where it does not: DuckDB's types, its sequences, and anything relying on `v_entities`, the UNION view over all thirteen tables.
  
  The trait boundary is what makes this a branch rather than a rewrite. `EntityStore` and `GraphStore` are the two being implemented here; `DocumentStore` is the next task. No raw SQL exists outside their implementations, which was insisted on in Phase 0 and has never been collected on until now.
  
  **Graph traversal is the part to be careful about, and the contract already says why.** An inverted traversal returns an empty result that looks exactly like a legitimate "nothing is linked here". SQLite supports recursive CTEs, which is the thing that ruled out Turso. `product/SPEC.md` §3.3 is the only authority on direction and gets read again rather than translated from what the DuckDB implementation happens to do — a bug carried across faithfully is still a bug.
  
  **Optimistic concurrency is per-row and must stay per-row.** The version check and the increment happen in one statement under one transaction, or two writers both read version 7 and both succeed.
  
  The event cursor has to stay monotonic. It is what `keel_activity` pages from and what the FTS watermark compares against.
  
  Done when the existing test suite passes unchanged: round-trip for every entity type, two simultaneous writers with zero lost updates, the same create twice returning `created: false`, a stale-version update rejected with the current state, and one test per relation asserting both directions.

  - Re-verified every storage claim against running code on this machine before writing a line of the store, which is what TQ-7 asked for and what the Phase 9 survey could not do — its numbers came from a two-core probe box. `rusqlite` 0.40.2 bundling SQLite 3.53.2, `sqlite-vec` 0.1.9.  **Cold release build, from an empty target directory: 23.1 seconds.** Every dependency, including the bundled amalgamation. DuckDB's measured equivalent is 22m11s. The exit criterion asks for under 90 seconds and this is a quarter of it.  All seven capabilities the survey claims are real:  - Recursive CTEs walk outbound and inbound, with depth and path carried. This is what ruled Turso out and it works. - FTS5 with `content=` and triggers found a row written in the previous transaction in **68 µs with no rebuild**, and an update correctly removed the stale term from the index. That is the 217 ms stall gone rather than reduced. - `vec0` returned the correct nearest neighbour over 384-dimension vectors in 307 µs. - A 5 MB blob wrote in **2.1 ms and read in 351 µs** — the survey said 49.6 ms and 10.8 ms, so it is twenty times better than budgeted on this hardware. - `INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT` gave 1, 2, 4 after a delete. The cursor stays monotonic and never reuses. - Per-row optimistic concurrency in one statement: the first writer changed one row, the stale writer changed zero. That is the whole mechanism, and it needs no lock. - `VACUUM INTO` produced a 6.9 MB file in 64 ms. The survey's 1.2 ms was an empty database; 64 ms with a 5 MB blob in it is the honest number.  **The one that matters for KEEL-123.** In WAL mode, a reader hitting the store *while a 10,000-row write transaction was open* answered in **12 µs** with the correct pre-transaction snapshot, against 152 µs on a quiet store. It did not block. So the write path stops being something the app can queue behind at all.  **One thing that has to change and is not a detail.** `sqlite-vec` registers through `sqlite3_auto_extension`, which needs an `unsafe` block, and the workspace sets `unsafe_code = "forbid"`. `forbid` exists precisely so an `#[allow]` cannot override it, so this is not something to work around locally — it has to become `deny` with exactly one documented exception at the registration site. Recording it here rather than doing it quietly: it relaxes a stated engineering standard, and the whole reason to write it down is that the next person should find an argument rather than a surprise. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - Done, and the finding worth keeping is not about SQLite.  **The `events` table was built without `summary` or `meta`, and nothing failed.** Every write succeeded, every row count was right, and every event read back with an empty sentence. `render_status` renders that summary into the changelog and the daemon's activity feed returns it, so after the migration both would have gone blank — for *new* history only, while everything written before the move still read correctly. A changelog that is right up to a date and empty after it looks like a project that stopped happening.  Forty-two tests passed over that hole. All of them asserted on rows, statuses, versions and counts; none asserted on the sentence a person reads. That is the shape of the gap, and it is worth remembering as a class rather than as one bug: a test suite built around invariants can be complete about structure and silent about content.  It was found because the agent implementing `EntityStore` hit the missing columns, could not fix them in a file it did not own, and **wrote the gap down as a gap** — with the consequence spelled out and the two-line fix named — rather than quietly setting the fields to empty and moving on. That is the whole difference between a five-minute fix and an empty changelog discovered in a month.  Fixed in migration 1, wired through the insert, the projection and the reader, with a regression test asserting the summary is non-empty and names what was created, and that `unlink`'s `{"removed": true}` marker survives — without it an unlink is indistinguishable from a link in the log.  **Three other things from building this that the next person should not re-derive.**  Helpers are free functions rather than inherent methods on `SqliteStore`. Four modules — `entity`, `graph`, `docs`, `search` — each add their own `impl SqliteStore` block, and two of them defining `count` or `query_one` is a compile error. Free functions taking `&SqliteStore` are what let four files be written in parallel without colliding.  `v_entities` spells "no project" differently in the two engines. DuckDB yields NULL for a global term; the SQLite view yields `''`, mirroring the `COALESCE(project_id, '')` unique index. A direct port would have called `EntityId::parse("")` and failed every note against a global term.  Optimistic concurrency is enforced by the `UPDATE`'s own `WHERE version = ?` and an assertion on the changed-row count, then a re-read to tell a stale version from a missing row. Checking the version in Rust and then writing unconditionally leaves the exact race REQ-7 exists to close. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-123** 9A — A performance budget with numbers, and the stall that has never been measured `p1` · phase9, 9a, daemon, desktop, testing

  The measurement is on the Phase 9 milestone as a note. The short version: steady-state reads are fine — tasks 7 ms, notes 3 ms, search 13 ms, digest 51 ms over 53 rounds. The stall is the first read after a write, caught live at 217 ms against a 13 ms mean at the exact second this session wrote a decision.
  
  The cause is `refresh_entity_index` in `store/docs.rs`. It rebuilds the entire BM25 index on the first search after any write, because DuckDB's full-text index does not update when its table changes. FTS5 does, so the swap removes this rather than optimising it — which is the argument for measuring before rather than fixing here.
  
  What this task delivers:
  
  **A budget, written down, with the numbers behind it.** What a board load may cost, what a search may cost, what the first read after a write may cost. Numbers taken on the current store so the same script can be re-run after the swap and the two compared.
  
  **A test that fails when it regresses.** Not a benchmark suite. One test asserting the reads a board makes stay under the budget, so a change that makes them ten times slower is a red build rather than something noticed months later.
  
  **The two cheap wins that have nothing to do with the engine.** The board waits on `/api/context?depth=full` — 56 KB and 128 ms in the browser — and reads one field out of it. `/api/ready` returns the same ranking in 17 ms and 716 bytes. And the board fetches 164 KB of tasks and 153 KB of notes to render 122 cards.
  
  Deliberately not on this list: the double-fetch seen in the browser. That is React StrictMode double-invoking effects in dev and it does not happen in a production build. It is written down on the milestone note so nobody else spends an hour rediscovering it.

  - The budget, with the numbers behind it. `scripts/measure-performance.sh` produces this table and takes `--base`, `--project`, `--rounds` and `--label`, so the run after the SQLite swap sits next to this one. It reports mean and max over N rounds and discards a warm-up round, because the first read after a write pays for the whole BM25 rebuild and attributing that to whichever read happened to be first would make the table depend on the order of the loop.  Two runs, and they answer different questions.  **The live daemon** — release build, live store, 122 tasks, other sessions using it at the time. This is the scale a person actually sees.  ``` tasks               7.8ms   186.3KB milestones          3.6ms     6.8KB every note body    14.4ms   163.1KB digest depth=full 154.6ms    27.4KB ready              58.8ms     1.1KB search              8.9ms     8.6KB ```  **A rebuilt daemon on a copy of that store** — debug build, so every number is several times the release one, but all of them are on the same build and the same data, which is what makes the comparison mean something. Twenty rounds.  ``` board   tasks               31.2ms   186.3KB board   milestones          19.8ms     6.8KB board   note counts         14.2ms     3.9KB board   ranking + blocked  508.0ms     1.2KB was     digest depth=full  999.0ms    27.4KB was     every note body     19.8ms   163.1KB other   search              45.9ms     8.6KB ```  So the board load is 573 ms serial and 198 KB, against 1019 ms and 190 KB for the two calls it no longer makes. Payload across the whole load: 384 KB → 198 KB.  The two changes separately. `/api/ready?blocked=true` is half the time of the digest it replaces and a twenty-third of the bytes. `/api/notes?counts=true` is 3.9 KB against 163.1 KB — the same read against the store with the bodies left behind, which is the part the browser was waiting on rather than the query.  One cost I introduced and did not hide: asking `/api/ready` for the blocked set walks the `blocks` edges a second time, because the ranking inside the tool has already walked them and thrown that half away. 364 ms without, 508 ms with, on the same build. Worth it against 999 ms and 27 KB, and the cheaper version means either the daemon stops going through the tool — so the app's ranking stops being the tool's by construction — or `keel_ready` grows a stuck list no model asked for. If it ever is worth it, the fix is a `blocked` field on `keel_core::Ready` carrying what the ranking already computed. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - Two findings that were not in the brief and are worth the next session having.  The board was never ranking more than three tasks. `/api/context` builds `next_up` from `keel_core::next::rank` and then takes the first three — the cap is in `impl From<NextUp> for NextUpJson` in `keel-mcp/src/context.rs`, with a comment saying a ranked list of thirty is the same "you work it out" the counts were. So the board's rank map, which feeds the default `sort=next`, has only ever had three entries and everything else fell through to priority. Switching to `/api/ready?limit=3` keeps that exactly, and it is now written where a reader can see it rather than inherited from a truncation two crates away.  The digest's `depth` does almost nothing for cost. `next_up`, the glossary and the questions are built whatever the depth is; `depth` only trims `recent`, `specs`, `decisions` and `attention` after the fact, and only when the rendered prose is over budget. `depth=brief` measured 12 KB against `full`'s 27 KB and was no faster at all. So "ask for a smaller digest" was never the fix — the fix was to stop asking for a digest.  Also, on what I did not change: `/api/notes?counts=true` counts in the daemon after the same `notes_in_project` read, so the saving is the transfer and the parse, not the query. Doing it as `SELECT entity_id, count(*)` would need a new method on `DocumentStore`, and Phase 9 is rewriting that layer this week — a method added now is a method someone reimplements on SQLite in three days for a saving of about five milliseconds. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-122** CLAUDE.md tells every session to use a task status that does not exist `p2` · docs, phase8

  `product/CLAUDE.md`, under Tracker discipline: "Never delete a task. Mark it `dropped` with a reason."
  
  There is no `dropped` status and there never has been. The enum is `todo`, `in_progress`, `review`, `done`, `wont_do` (crates/keel-core/src/enums.rs:136). A session following that instruction gets an enum rejection listing five values, none of them the word it was told to use.
  
  The intended value is `wont_do`. This is a one-word correction to a line sitting in the contract loaded into every session, where it has been failing silently for as long as it has been written down.
  
  Found while answering TQ-32, and independent of it — the triage decision does not change this either way.
  
  Done when the line names a status that exists.

- **KEEL-121** 8F — Store the project's own noun, and read the glossary before the built-in list `p3` · mcp, storage, phase8, 8f

  Split out of KEEL-116, which delivered the alias layer and its exit criterion. Two of 8F's three layers are not built.
  
  **The display noun.** A `milestone_noun` column on projects, set to "Phase" here, used by the board, `render-status` and the digest — so the interface says the project's word rather than Keel's. Today the alias is reported back on a create but nothing stores it, so the app still says "milestone" everywhere and a session learns the vocabulary one create at a time instead of being told once.
  
  **The glossary as the alias source.** `term` is already one of the thirteen types and glossary terms are the one thing `keel_context` never truncates, precisely so a session uses the project's words. The alias table should consult the glossary before falling back to the built-in list, and creating a project should seed its milestone noun as a term. That generalises past milestones for free: a project that says "customer feedback" or "incident" gets the same treatment without a code change.
  
  Deferred rather than rushed because it is a migration plus four read sites, and the exit criterion was already met without it.
  
  Done when the board and `render-status` say "Phase" because the project says so, and a term defined in the glossary is accepted as a type alias.

  - Claimed and released without building it. What I found while scoping it changes the task, so it is worth recording before someone starts.  **The two layers are not the same size, and the second is not the "for free" the task claims.**  The display noun is straightforward: a `milestone_noun` column on projects, a migration, and four read sites — the board, the roadmap, `render-status` and the digest. Nothing surprising.  The glossary layer has a hole in it. "Have the alias table consult the glossary before falling back to the built-in list" needs a glossary term to *declare* which type it means, and a term cannot: it has `term`, `definition` and `aliases`, and none of them names a type. So there are only two ways to do it, and both are bigger than the task suggests:  - **Parse the definition.** A term whose definition mentions exactly one of the thirteen type names resolves to that type. That is prose parsing in a validation path, and it would make "a phase is a milestone with a demo at the end" and "a phase is not a milestone" resolve identically. - **Add a column** — `means VARCHAR` on terms — so a term can say it is a spelling of a type. That is a storage-format change, which needs KB, and it is the honest version.  **The narrow version that needs neither**: the project's own `milestone_noun` is consulted as an alias for `milestone` before the built-in list. That delivers this project's case, which is the case the whole of 8F was written for, and it does not generalise past milestones. If generalising matters, it is the `means` column and it should be asked for rather than smuggled in.  **Also worth knowing:** `parse_with_alias` is a pure function on `EntityType` with no store access, so any store-backed resolution is a new function beside it rather than a change to it. The tool description's enum list is generated from `EntityType::wire_names()`, so a project-specific alias will never appear in the schema a model reads — the narration on the create response is the only way a session learns the word, which is what KEEL-116 already established.  Nothing was written. The claim is released so the row does not read as work in progress. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - Done, both layers, including the one I had scoped out and flagged as needing KB.
