Asking to finish it was the answer to that, so `Term::means` is a column now — B-50 carries the argument.  **Verified end to end on a scratch store, not only in tests.** A project created with `milestone_noun: "Cycle"` stored the noun and seeded the term in one call; `keel_create(type: "cycle")` came back with *"You said 'cycle' — in Keel that is a milestone, because this project's glossary says so"*. Then a term "Incident" declaring `means: task` with an alias "outage", and `keel_create(type: "outage")` created a task. That last one is the whole point: "outage" is a word Keel has never heard of and will never need to.  **Keel's own project says "Phase" now.** The tracker writes "Active phase", "## Phases" and a "Phase" column header; the board's filter menu reads "Phase" and "Any phase"; the digest's first paragraph says "active phase: Phase 8 — The working loop".  **What I got wrong when scoping this, and it is worth having on the record.** I wrote that the narrow version — the project's noun consulted directly — would deliver this project's case and not generalise, and that generalising meant the `means` column. Both true. What I did not see is that the two are not alternatives: the noun is what the *interface* says and `means` is what the *input* accepts, and a project can legitimately have one without the other. So both are consulted, in that order, and the doc comment says why rather than leaving it looking like belt and braces.  **The ceiling is the thing to watch here.** This is the first feature where a *stored row* introduces vocabulary — written by whoever uses Keel rather than by whoever reviews a change. `means` is an `EntityType`, so a fourteenth type is unrepresentable, and a test walks every word the glossary knows to assert it.  **Two refusals worth having:** - A term named after a canonical type cannot redirect it. `keel_create(type: "task")` means a task in every project forever, and the canonical name is checked first. - A `milestone_noun` that is another type's name is refused where it is set. Allowing it would make every `keel_create` naming that type ambiguous, and the resolution order hides the problem rather than surfacing it — the canonical name wins, so the noun would silently do nothing.  **One ordering change in `keel_create` worth knowing about:** the project is now resolved before the type. That looks wrong and is necessary — a project's glossary is what decides whether "outage" means anything, so the type cannot be resolved without knowing who is asking.  **Seeding happens on update as well as create.** Only on create would have meant an existing project could set the noun, watch the interface change, and never see the word in the digest — which is the one place a session reads the vocabulary before it needs it.  18 tests across `vocabulary.rs` and its integration file; four snapshots reviewed and all four diffs were the new nullable fields and nothing else. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-120** Plain English on every prose field, not just milestone summaries `p1` · mcp, storage, phase8

  B-46, the plain-English house style, extends the milestone rule to decision bodies, question bodies, specs, feedback, notes, task bodies and titles.
  
  Three layers, because only one of them is a validator:
  
  1. Every prose-bearing tool description says what good looks like, with a worked good/bad pair. This is the layer that changes what gets written.
  2. Rejection for what is objectively wrong: empty, a body that only restates its title, a bare TQ-15 or KEEL-96 with no gloss, and a short list of phrases with no legitimate use in a tracker.
  3. Warning for the softer tells, returned with a successful write rather than blocking it.
  
  Fenced code, inline code and block quotes are stripped first. Quoting an error message or what someone actually said is carrying someone else's words, and refusing those would stop the store recording the world as it is.
  
  One `style` module in keel-core so the CLI, MCP and import cannot diverge.
  
  Done when a decision body that restates its title is refused, a note quoting an error message is not, and the tool descriptions carry the register.

  - Done. `keel-core/src/style.rs` is the one place that decides what prose may say, and it is wired into the authoring paths: `keel_create` bodies, `keel_write_doc`, and `Note::validate` — the last of which covers CLI and MCP notes together.  **The exemption is what makes the rule usable.** Fenced code, inline code and block quotes are stripped before any check. Without that, a note carrying an error message becomes unwritable and the store loses the ability to record what actually happened. Three tests hold it, including a decision body quoting a driver's own wording at it.  **Refuse versus warn, and why the line sits where it does.** Thirteen phrases are refused because they have no legitimate use in a tracker. Seven softer tells warn instead and ride back with the write that landed, in the summary and in `style_warnings`. The reasoning is that refusing on a signal teaches the wrong lesson: a model refused for something it does not accept swaps the word and keeps the shape, so the prose ends up both bad and compliant while the check reports success.  **Restatement reuses containment rather than similarity** — the KEEL-65 rule pointed at a different pair of strings. One token set must be a subset of the other, so the difference can only be added words. "Fix the board filter so it survives a reload" passes against the title "Fix the board filter"; a naive overlap score would have refused it.  **`keel import` is deliberately exempt.** It writes revisions too, but it is a person migrating text they did not author — the same carve-out the mirror rule already makes. So the check sits at the two MCP call sites rather than inside `write_revision`. The cost is that the CLI and MCP are not literally identical here; the alternative was that importing any legacy file with "robust" in it fails, which would have made the three phase specs unimportable.  **Both snapshots caught it, which is the point of having them.** `tools_list` for the three descriptions, `keel_create` for the new `style_warnings` field. Reviewed both diffs before accepting: only the intended changes.  Worth saying plainly, and it is in B-46 too: this catches structure. It cannot tell a limp sentence from a sharp one, and the layer that actually changes what gets written is the tool descriptions, not this module.  448 tests, clippy clean, fmt clean. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-119** A killed test run leaks its temp stores into TMPDIR `p3` · testing, tooling

  Found while clearing a full disk on 2026-08-11: 1,649 directories under `$TMPDIR` matching the `tempfile` pattern, each holding a 4.8 MB `keel.duckdb` plus a `lance` directory, totalling 7.7 GB. Dated from 2026-08-10 onward.
  
  `tempfile::TempDir` deletes on Drop, so this is every run where Drop did not happen — a killed cargo process, a timeout, an aborted test binary. Several today were mine. The store is large enough per instance that the leak is measured in gigabytes rather than megabytes, and nothing sweeps it.
  
  The disk reached 100% and it did not present as a disk problem: the linker failed with `errno=28` and it surfaced as 13 failing daemon tests. That is the expensive part — an out-of-space condition looked like a code regression.
  
  Options worth weighing: have the test helper register a sweep of stale `keel.duckdb` temp dirs on start; use a single shared temp root under `target/` that `cargo clean` removes; or shrink the fixture so a leaked store costs kilobytes.
  
  Done when a killed test run does not leave a multi-megabyte store behind, or something removes stale ones automatically.
  
  Not urgent, but it will recur — it took a session from "tests fail" to "disk is full" once already.

  - Not fixed, and swept by hand again — 315 leaked stores found, 255 of them older than an hour, 1.38 GB freed. Second sweep in two days, which is the argument for doing something rather than tolerating it.  **Preparatory work, so the next session does not re-derive it.** Three approaches, and the two obvious ones both have a catch.  **`[env] TMPDIR` in `.cargo/config.toml`, pointed at `target/tmp`.** One line, applies to every test binary with no code change, and `cargo clean` removes the lot. The catch: `tempfile` requires the directory to exist already, and `[env]` cannot create it. Nothing in the workspace runs before the test binaries — there is no build script — so there is no hook that could.  **A shared `test_store()` helper.** The natural home is keel-core, and it cannot go there: finding `target/` means reading `CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR`, and the standing rule is that keel-core never reads an environment variable. That rule is what keeps the CLI, the daemon and any future surface cheap, and it is not worth bending for a test helper. A dev-only crate would work, and adds a workspace member for one function.  **Shrink the fixture** so a leaked store costs kilobytes. Does not stop the leak, only makes it not matter. Twenty-five test files call `tempfile::tempdir()` and every one opens a DuckDB store; the 4.8 MB is DuckDB's own initial file rather than test data, so shrinking probably means not opening a store in the tests that do not need one.  **Why this is worth doing rather than tolerating.** When the disk filled on 2026-08-10 it did not present as a disk problem: the linker failed with `errno=28` and it surfaced as 13 failing daemon tests. That is the expensive part — an out-of-space condition that reads as a code regression.  The sweep itself is four lines: every `$TMPDIR/.tmp*` directory containing a `keel.duckdb`, older than an hour so nothing a running binary owns is touched. `scripts/sweep-test-stores.sh` is the cheapest thing that would have saved both of those hours. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - The disk filled again mid-session and this time it was measured rather than swept. The leaked test stores are the small half of the problem. KB's question — how it can refill after clearing 15 GB with nothing running but Claude — has a specific answer.  **Cargo never garbage-collects.** Every fingerprint change produces a new artifact under a new hash and leaves the previous one on disk forever. Normally a rounding error. Not here, because every test binary statically links DuckDB: `libduckdb.a` is 237 MB, each linked test binary is 100–165 MB, and there are about thirty of them. One full rebuild after a fingerprint change is roughly 8.5 GB of test binaries, and the previous 8.5 GB is still there.  What was actually on disk, counted:  - `target/` at 17 GB. `debug/deps` 12 GB, of which **8.5 GB was 88 linked test binaries**; `debug/incremental` 4 GB. - **Nine generations of `keel_daemon`, five of `keel`, five of `concurrency`, four of `keel_core`.** Three copies of `use_cases` at 165 MB each. - **Four `libduckdb-sys` build directories**, each with its own 237 MB `libduckdb.a`, plus two 238 MB `liblibduckdb_sys` rlibs. - 60,410 loose `.rcgu.o` split-debuginfo files — enormous in file count, only 0.2 GB, so not the problem despite looking like it. - 135 leaked test stores in `TMPDIR`, 813 MB. The KEEL-119 half.  **What triggered this particular round: I did.** Adding `rusqlite` and `sqlite-vec` and changing `unsafe_code` from `forbid` to `deny` are both fingerprint changes at the root of the graph, so every crate rebuilt under new hashes and the entire previous generation stayed. That is the mechanism by which 15 GB comes back after being cleared — not a leak so much as an archive nobody empties.  **How it presented, which is the expensive part.** `rustc-LLVM ERROR: IO failure on output stream: No space left on device`, and before that a bare `linking with cc failed: exit status: 1` across five targets. It reads as a compiler bug or a code regression. It is a full disk. This is the second time that has cost a session — the first is in this task's original body.  Swept to 7.9 GB with all 490 tests still passing, and the sweep is now `scripts/sweep-build-artifacts.sh` rather than a thing done by hand twice. It keeps the newest generation of every artifact and drops the rest, which is safe because Cargo rebuilds whatever it finds missing. `DRY_RUN=1` shows what it would take.  **Phase 9 is the actual fix and this is now the strongest argument for it.** SQLite's amalgamation is about 1.5 MB of object code against DuckDB's 237 MB. The same thirty test binaries cost megabytes rather than gigabytes, stale generations stop mattering, and the fixture a killed test leaks stops being worth a script.  One thing to be honest about in the first sweep: the age filter silently failed — `find -newermt` is not supported by the `bfs` on this machine and errored to stderr, so the guard read as "no output, therefore old" and all 135 stores were removed rather than only those over an hour. No test was running, so nothing was taken from under a live process. The script now uses `-mmin +60`, which `bfs` does support. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - Measured it rather than assuming, because the premise had changed under the task. A test binary that **finishes** leaks nothing — ran `documents` to completion, 23 passed, zero temp dirs left behind. `TempDir::drop` does its job. The leak is entirely from processes that get **killed**: I stopped one `cargo test --workspace` partway through and it left 27 stores, 10.8 MB, in one keystroke.  That explains the shape of the accumulation. The 2,318 stores sitting in TMPDIR today are not two days of steady drip — they are one `cargo mutants` run on 2026-08-12. 24 of its 113 mutants timed out, and each timeout kills a test binary that has already opened ninety-odd stores. 24 × ~90 is the number on disk.  Phase 9 also shrank the blast radius without anyone aiming at it: a leaked store was 4.8 MB of DuckDB plus a `lance` directory and is now a 388 KB `keel.sqlite`. Thirteen times smaller. 2,318 of them come to 1.1 GB, where the same count of DuckDB stores would have been 11 GB.  Worth knowing for KEEL-173: the mutant timeouts are the thing that multiplies this, so fixing the 30-second daemon-start wait takes the leak with it. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>
  - The sweeper written for this task has never worked anywhere but this laptop, and said nothing about it.  `scripts/sweep-build-artifacts.sh` §4 globbed `"$TMP".tmp*` with `TMP="${TMPDIR:-/tmp}"`. macOS sets TMPDIR with a trailing slash, so that expands to `…/T/.tmp*` and matches. Strip the slash — which is how Linux spells it, and what the `/tmp` fallback gives you — and it expands to `/tmp.tmp*`, matches nothing, and the loop body never runs. Measured both ways on the same 2,382 directories: with the slash, 2,382; without, **0**.  It reported nothing either, because the summary line was guarded by `[ "$leaked" -gt 0 ]`. A sweeper that swept zero looked exactly like a sweeper that found nothing to sweep. That is the same failure the graph-direction rule is about — an empty result that is indistinguishable from a legitimate one.  Fixed by stripping the trailing slash and using an explicit `/` in the glob, so it no longer depends on how TMPDIR is spelled, and by printing the count unconditionally. It also now matches `keel.duckdb` again, since 47 pre-Phase-9 stores are still on disk at 4.8 MB each and the old sweeper could never have reached them. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-13</sub>

- **KEEL-118** 8E — Copy-ready prompts on the task page `p3` · desktop, phase8, 8e

  The app cannot write, so every task page carries a few prompts you can copy and paste into Claude Code: "close KEEL-42 as done with the commit", "what is blocking KEEL-42", "split KEEL-42 into sub-tasks". One click copies.
  
  This is what makes read-only feel deliberate rather than inert, and it is nearly free now the detail view exists.
  
  Done when each task page offers at least three prompts with its own identifier filled in, and clicking one copies it.

  - Done. Four prompts on every task page, each already addressed to that task: close it with the commit, what is blocking it, split it into sub-tasks, add a note.  Copy rather than deep-link, deliberately. There is no URL that puts text into a Claude Code session, and a button that behaved as though there were would be worse than one honest about the clipboard.  A denied clipboard is swallowed rather than surfaced. This is a convenience on a read-only page and the text is on screen either way; an error state would be louder than the thing it is reporting.  One test-writing note: the prompt is a bare text node beside the "copy" affordance, so `getByText` cannot find it as an element. The assertions match the button's accessible name instead, and the clipboard test asserts the shape `what is blocking <ref>` rather than a hardcoded identifier — a prompt addressed to no task is the failure worth catching, not a particular number.  191 desktop tests, typecheck clean. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-117** 8E — Rate limiting on /mcp `p2` · daemon, mcp, phase8, 8e

  There is none. No `governor` or equivalent anywhere in the workspace, confirmed. Cheap insurance against an agent stuck in a loop, and the MCP specification lists it under what servers must do.
  
  Keep it small. This is one user and one daemon, so a simple per-connection limit is enough and a measurement would have to justify anything more.
  
  Done when a client exceeding the limit gets a proper rate-limit response rather than being served, and a test drives it over the threshold.

  - Done. A token bucket on `/mcp`, checked before the Origin check and before the store is touched — a call that will not be served should cost as little as possible.  **Hand-written, no `governor`.** One process, one user, loopback: no distributed coordination, no per-tenant accounting, no clock skew. The whole problem is "how many calls in the last second", which is a counter and an instant. The standing rule is to leave the machinery out until a measurement asks for it.  **One bucket, not one per client.** Per-IP buckets on a loopback socket all key to `127.0.0.1`, so they would be one bucket in a disguise. What is being protected is the store's single write lock, which is global.  **Tokens rather than a fixed window,** because a fixed window lets a caller spend a whole allowance in the last millisecond of one window and the whole of the next in the first — twice the intended rate, at exactly the moment the limiter should be working.  **The first numbers were wrong, and a test found it.** I set burst 60 / 10 per second. `the_digest_never_truncates_questions_or_terms` creates sixty questions in a loop and started failing. That is not a test artefact — it is what planning a phase looks like, and this project's own Phase 8 went in as roughly fifty creates and links back to back. The limit was sitting on top of legitimate work.  So the numbers moved to 300 burst / 50 per second, and the reasoning is written into the constants: this is a backstop against a *pathological* loop, not a fairness mechanism. A retrying model does thousands per second; real bulk work does hundreds, slowly. At 1–10ms per write, 50/s leaves the write lock mostly free, which is what keeps the human in front of the app served while a runaway session is throttled.  **A poisoned lock fails open.** This is a guard rail, not an authorisation check: failing open costs one unmetered request, failing closed turns a panic elsewhere into a daemon that answers nothing.  The 429 carries `Retry-After` and a message telling the caller to read the error rather than resend it, since resending is what got it here.  454 workspace tests, clippy clean, fmt clean. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-116** 8F — Let a project use its own word for a milestone `p2` · mcp, storage, phase8, 8f

  This project says "phase" on every screen. `keel_create(type: "phase")` fails with an enum error listing thirteen types, none of which is the word.
  
  Three layers, and none of them adds a type.
  
  A display noun per project: the project records `milestone_noun: "Phase"`, and the interface, the renderer and the digest all use it. The board says Phase 8, `render-status` writes Phases, and `keel_context` tells the session in its first paragraph that this project calls milestones phases.
  
  Aliases on input, applied before validation. phase, epic, sprint, cycle, iteration, release and version resolve to milestone. issue, ticket, story, bug, chore and defect to task. adr, rfc and choice to decision. risk, unknown and open question to question. requirement, design doc and prd to spec.
  
  The resolution is reported, not silent: the response says `created milestone "Phase 9" — this project calls those phases`. A silent success teaches the session nothing and it guesses the same way next time. A narrated one teaches the vocabulary in one round trip.
  
  The rule that keeps this safe: an alias is a spelling, not a concept. It must never create a fourteenth type. "Sprint isn't quite a milestone" is how a schema grows a type it cannot remove.
  
  Third layer: the glossary already does most of this and nobody wired it up. `term` is one of the thirteen types, and glossary terms are the one thing `keel_context` never truncates, for exactly this reason. Seed the milestone noun as a term when a project is created, and have the alias table consult the glossary before the built-in list. That generalises past milestones for free.
  
  Done when `keel_create(type: "phase")` succeeds and says what this project calls those. Phase 8 exit criterion.

  - Exit criterion met, verified against the live daemon: `keel_create(type: "phase")` created Phase 9 as a milestone and came back with `resolved_from: "phase"`.  **The alias layer is done. The other two are split out as KEEL-121** — the stored `milestone_noun` and reading the glossary before the built-in list. Deferred rather than rushed: both are a migration plus four read sites, and the criterion was already met without them. Recording that honestly rather than marking three layers done because one of them shipped.  **`parse_with_alias` returns the alias, and that is the design.** Resolution is narrated, not silent: the response says "You said 'sprint' — in Keel that is a milestone." A silent success teaches the session nothing and it guesses the same way next time; one round trip teaches the vocabulary.  **Two tests exist to stop this becoming a fourteenth type**, which is the real risk the spec names. One asserts every alias resolves to a type already in `ALL` and that no alias shadows a canonical name; the other that no word is listed twice, since a duplicate means whichever comes first silently wins and the loser is invisible. "A sprint isn't quite a milestone" is exactly how a schema acquires a type it can never remove.  **`bug` and `chore` resolve to `task`, deliberately**, even though they are also task *kinds*. A caller writing `type: "bug"` wants a task; the kind is a separate argument they can still set. Resolving to the type is the reading that is never wrong.  The snapshot caught `resolved_from` landing in the create response — reviewed and accepted, it is that one field and nothing else.  463 workspace tests, clippy clean, fmt clean. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-115** 8B — keel_attach: let the daemon read the file, and tell the truth about the base64 cap `p2` · mcp, daemon, phase8, 8b

  Two things, both about the fact that the shipped image path describes a ceiling nobody can reach.
  
  Base64 inflates by a third, and the model has to emit every one of those characters as tool arguments. A 100 KB image is 35,000 to 45,000 tokens. A 1 MB image, which is the declared cap, is 350,000 to 450,000. A retina screenshot is 300 KB to 2 MB. So the stated ceiling is about ten times the useful one, and the description is a trap: it says 1 MB and means roughly 100 KB.
  
  Fix the description to state the real ceiling and point at the alternative. That needs no decision.
  
  Then add `keel_attach(id, path)` so the daemon reads a local file itself. Same machine, so bytes never enter the model's context. This is what makes a real screenshot possible from Claude Code.
  
  Not the URL fetch. TQ-6 already settled that, this morning, and the answer was no: it would make the daemon issue outbound requests on a model's instruction, which is more capability than this needs. The spec lists it as an open decision but it is closed. TQ-33 records that closure, so the spec's list of open decisions is the stale half of the disagreement.
  
  MCP offers no better route. Tool inputs are JSON only, there is no binary input type, and the proposal to add one is still an open pull request.
  
  Done when a file on disk can be attached without its bytes passing through a model, and the base64 description states a ceiling that is actually reachable.

  - Done, both halves, and the second one arrived as a field rather than as the tool TQ-33 named. The argument is in B-49: TQ-33 approved the capability and TQ-31 set thirteen tools as the ceiling, both KB's, and the form of a capability is the reversible half. `image_path` sits beside `image` on `keel_create`, and in `keel_update`'s `changes` for attaching to something that already exists.  **Verified against a live daemon on a scratch store**, not only in tests: a 683 KB PNG went from a path on disk into the store and came back out of `/api/blob/{id}` as `image/png`, 683,411 bytes, byte-identical. Through base64 that same file would have cost the model roughly 240,000 output tokens.  **The description was the actual bug and it needed no decision.** It advertised 1 MB, which is 350,000 to 450,000 output tokens — about ten times anything reachable. It now states 100 KB as the practical ceiling, says why the cost falls on the caller, and points at `image_path`.  **Three refusals worth having, each with a test:** - Anything URL-shaped, with the reason in the message. TQ-33 predicted this failure mode in as many words: a path argument that quietly starts accepting a URL is that decision reversed by accident. Covered for `https:`, `http:` and `file:`. - A relative path, because the daemon's working directory is its own and a relative path resolves against something the caller cannot see. - A file whose bytes are not an image, even when it is called `.png`. The extension is whatever somebody typed; the magic bytes are what the app will try to render.  **Both arguments at once is refused rather than resolved.** Silently preferring one would mean a caller who sent both got the file sometimes and the payload other times, on an ordering nothing documents.  **Attaching to an existing row does not bump the version twice.** An update whose only instruction was `image_path` has nothing to change on the row, and the store rejects an empty change set — so that case reads the row, writes the blob, and repoints in one update. Getting this wrong would have been a version bump with no image behind it.  The 10 MB ceiling on the file path is ten times the base64 one, and the asymmetry is the point: nothing here enters model context, so the constraint stops being the context window and becomes only "is this a picture or a mistake". <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-113** 8G — keel lint: report the rows that are already unreadable `p2` · cli, phase8, 8g

  Around ninety-four task rows exist without a summary and something has to look at them once. `keel lint` reports and never rewrites.
  
  Not rewriting is the point. A machine inventing a summary would produce exactly the confident, plausible, wrong prose the requirement exists to prevent. Same reasoning that stops the mirror ever reading a file back.
  
  What it reports: missing summaries, and bare identifiers with no gloss beside them. The `unresolved_id_reference` parser in fsck already recognises the TQ-n, B-n, REQ-n and KEEL-n families, so that half is reuse rather than new code.
  
  Done when `keel lint` reports zero unexpanded identifiers across the rows that already exist. That is a Phase 8 exit criterion.
  
  Depends on the summary column landing first.

  - Done, and the exit criterion is met: `keel lint keel --check unexpanded_identifier` reports zero. It reported nine when the check first ran, and those nine bodies were glossed by hand — the sentences were things like "Waiting on TQ-35." and "Decision B-45.", which name a thing and say nothing about it.  **Three rules, not one.** Missing summaries (122 rows), unexpanded identifiers (0 now), and — added because it is the same shape of problem — closes with no reason, which is the 109 rows that predate B-47. All three are enforced on the way in and none can be enforced backwards, which is exactly what a lint is for.  **The identifier check is a word count, and the doc comment says so.** An identifier in a sentence with fewer than six other content words is nearly always leaned on rather than explained. There is no exact test for "did this sentence tell me what TQ-35 is", and pretending otherwise would be worse than admitting the heuristic. It is safe *because* this only reports: a false positive costs a glance, not a paragraph. Backticked identifiers are exempt, so writing about the check does not fail it.  **A bug caught by writing the test after the code.** `by_check` derived its per-rule counts from `self.findings`, which is the *truncated* list — so `--limit 12` printed "12 task_without_summary" under a total of 240. The counts are now computed before the cut and stored. That number is what a person reads to decide what to work on, so it has to describe the project rather than the page.  **Not an MCP tool, deliberately.** Thirteen is the ceiling, and a model handed ninety rows to improve would improve them by inventing prose — the precise failure the summary rule exists to prevent. It is a CLI command reading `/api/lint` through the daemon, for the reason `fsck` does: a report you have to stop the daemon to run is one nobody runs.  **Exits zero.** These rows predate the rules that would have refused them, so a non-zero exit would fail every build until someone had worked through 122 summaries by hand.  **One thing this session's own work exposed.** Fixing the nine glosses turned 240 findings into 231. The summaries are the long tail and they are not this task's job — a machine writing them is the thing being avoided.  Also fixed while here: `a_client_in_a_loop_is_rate_limited` had started failing, and it was the test rather than the limiter. It hammered `tools/list` 1000 times sequentially and relied on outrunning a 50-per-second refill, which needs every call under 20ms — and the response had just grown from ten tool definitions to thirteen. It now fires `ping` in concurrent batches, so the assertion no longer depends on how big some other endpoint's response happens to be. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-112** 8G — A task cannot be created without a summary `p1` · storage, mcp, phase8, 8g

  One or two plain sentences a colleague could read cold, six weeks from now, without having been in the conversation. What is wrong or wanted, who or what it affects, and what done looks like.
  
  Checked: `Task` (types.rs:354) has `title` and `body` and no `summary`. `Milestone` has one, so the name and its serialisation already have a precedent.
  
  Three parts:
  - A new nullable column on tasks. Nullable because roughly ninety-four rows already exist without one and cannot be made invalid retroactively.
  - `summary` required on `keel_create` when the type is task. This is the mechanism: a model physically cannot complete the call without confronting it, on every surface, whether or not any skill loaded. The description carries the contract and one worked good/bad pair.
  - Synchronous rejection in the storage layer, so the CLI and MCP cannot diverge and the row never exists in a bad state.
  
  The evidence that a rejection works: two gate sessions sent `priority: "high"` against an enum of p0 to p3 and both retried successfully in the same turn. A rejection costs one round trip, not a human.
  
  Also a rendering rule that keeps the requirement felt: lists show the summary, never the body. A task with no summary shows a visible gap rather than falling back to the first line of the body. A silent fallback is how a requirement quietly stops being one.
  
  Waiting on TQ-34 for how much validation sits behind the required field.
  
  Done when no task can be created without a summary, proved by a test asserting both the CLI and MCP paths refuse.

  - Done. A task cannot be created without a summary, on either surface. Migration 14 adds the column, `Task::validate_summary` refuses empty and title-restating, and the tool description carries the contract with a worked good/bad pair.  **Two checks and only two**, per KB's call in TQ-34: empty, and a summary that only reorders its own title. The restatement check reuses the containment rule from KEEL-65 — one token set must be a subset of the other, so added words pass and "Fix the board filter so it survives a reload" is accepted against the title "Fix the board filter".  **Validated on create and deliberately not on update.** Ninety-four rows predate the rule. Running it on every write would mean none of them could ever be touched again — moving one to `done` would be refused for a summary nobody was being asked to write. Freezing a third of the tracker is worse than the hole this leaves, which is that an update can still blank a summary that was required at creation. `keel lint` is what reports whatever falls through. A test asserts a summary-less task stays closable.  **I did this the slow way first, and the lesson is worth more than the change.** I put the check in the storage layer alone and left `Task::new` taking two arguments. So the missing summaries surfaced one failing test binary at a time — and `cargo test` stops at the first failure, so each round was a 3-4 minute build to discover another three files. Forty-nine call sites across thirteen files arrived in about six rounds.  Making `summary` a positional argument on `Task::new` — which is what `Milestone::new` already did for exactly this reason, decided in B-45 two hours earlier — turns that into one build error listing every site at once. The precedent was there and I did not apply it until the third round. **If a field is required, the constructor is where that belongs; a storage-layer check alone tells you where you were wrong one place at a time.**  **The bootstrap and the fixture use their existing body as the summary** rather than getting a second string beside it. Those rows carry real prose written when the work was planned, and two fields would be two answers to one question. Both still go through the create path, so a bad body is refused rather than smuggled past the rule.  **One test needed the bad state on purpose** and my bulk edit broke it — "refuses a task with no summary" got given a summary. It now clears the field explicitly, which is honest: with a positional argument, a task with no summary is a state you have to construct deliberately, and exactly one place in the codebase wants it.  470 workspace tests, clippy clean, fmt clean. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-110** 8A — Closing a task needs a reason, and done needs evidence `p1` · cli, mcp, storage, phase8, 8a

  Keel's definition of done is a seven-item checklist written as prose in product/CLAUDE.md that an agent is asked to honour. That is a convention. Making the evidence an argument of the transition, enforced in the storage layer so the CLI and MCP cannot diverge, makes it an invariant.
  
  Five reasons:
  - `done` — finished. Message and at least one piece of evidence required.
  - `wont_do` — deliberately not doing it. Message required.
  - `duplicate` — of another task. Writes a `duplicates` link.
  - `superseded` — by another task. Writes a `supersedes` link.
  - `no_change` — looked at it, nothing needed doing. Message required.
  
  Both relations already exist in `Relation` (link.rs), so the automatic links need no schema change.
  
  Evidence is typed and repeatable: `commit:<sha>`, `pr:<url>`, `test:<command>`, `doc:<entity-id>`, `url:<url>`, `image:<blob-id>`.
  
  Open question: `duplicate`, `superseded` and `no_change` are reasons, but only `done` and `wont_do` are terminal statuses. Where the reason is stored needs settling — a column on the task, or all three mapping onto `wont_do` with the reason recorded separately. Worth deciding when this is picked up.
  
  Waiting on TQ-31 for whether this is a tool or stays a CLI wrapper over `keel_update` with the enforcement in the storage layer.
  
  Done when a task cannot reach `done` without a reason, a message and evidence, with a test proving both the CLI and MCP paths refuse. That is a Phase 8 exit criterion.

  - Done, and the exit criterion holds on both surfaces: a task cannot reach `done` without a reason, a message and evidence, proved by a keel-core test and by a daemon test driving real HTTP.  **The open question in this task's body is settled: the reason is a column.** `close_reason` on the task carries all five values, and the status enum stays at five — `done` maps to Done, the other four to WontDo. Mapping all of `duplicate`, `superseded` and `no_change` onto `wont_do` with the reason recorded nowhere was the alternative, and it loses the only part that distinguishes them.  **The enforcement is in `DuckStore::update`, not in `work::close`.** That is what makes it an invariant rather than a second convention: a caller who reaches for `keel_update(status: done)` to get round the tool is refused by the same check. A daemon test drives exactly that path.  **Checked on the transition only, and this is the honest limit.** 107 tasks closed before any of this existed and carry none of it. Running the check on every write would freeze all of them — moving one's priority would be refused for a message nobody was being asked to write. Backfilling would mean inventing reasons for work nobody remembers, and a store that cannot tell an invented reason from a stated one is worse than one with visible holes. Same shape as the summary rule (TQ-34), same escape valve: `keel lint` reports what falls through.  **One hole left open deliberately.** `keel_create` with `status: done` still bypasses the rule, because `bootstrap` and `import` create already-closed rows and enforcing on create would break both. Creating a task already finished is a migration shape, not doing work — but it is a hole, and it belongs on `keel lint`'s list (KEEL-113).  **The message is a column, not a note.** A note cannot be checked: the storage layer has no way to know one was written in some other call. Notes stay the place for everything learned along the way, and `keel_close`'s response says so rather than assuming it.  **Evidence prefixes are validated, not just documented.** A bare sha in the list would make "what shipped this week, with the commits" silently wrong, which is the whole reason for typing it.  This close was itself made with `keel close KEEL-108 --reason done --evidence commit:…`, which is the first use in anger. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-109** 8A — keel claim: taking a task, atomically `p1` · cli, mcp, storage, phase8, 8a

  "Move a task to in_progress before starting it" is an instruction in the contract that sessions have to remember. Across sixty-six tasks there were once zero transitions into that state, which is what the SessionStart hook now nags about.
  
  `keel claim KEEL-42` replaces the instruction with a mechanism. One call sets the status, records the claiming session and the time. Claiming something already claimed fails, unless the claim is stale (reuse the three-day threshold `fsck` already warns on) or `--force` is passed. Closing releases it.
  
  Waiting on TQ-31 for whether this is an MCP tool as well as a CLI command.
  
  Done when a second claim on a live claim is refused with the claiming session named, a stale claim succeeds, and a test covers both.

  - Done. `keel claim KEEL-42` and `keel_claim` both refuse a task another session holds and name the holder in the refusal.  **There is no lock, and that is the design.** Claiming goes through the ordinary optimistic-concurrency update with the version it read, so two sessions racing both read version 7, the first writes 8 and the second is rejected with a stale-version error carrying the current state. Adding a lock would have been a second answer to a question the store already answers, and it would have needed its own release path.  **A claim with no session is the one write Keel refuses outright.** Everywhere else an anonymous write is merely less traceable. Here the session *is* the content: a claim naming nobody hides the task from `ready --unclaimed` while telling no one who to ask about it. The CLI takes `--session` or `KEEL_SESSION` and says why when neither is set.  **Three days, reusing fsck's existing threshold** rather than choosing a second number. Two thresholds for "this session is probably gone" would eventually disagree, and the disagreement would show as work `fsck` calls abandoned and `keel claim` refuses to take.  **Releasing happens in the store's update path, not in `close`.** Any route into a terminal status clears the claim, so a plain `keel_update(status: done)` cannot leave a claim standing — otherwise `ready --unclaimed` would keep skipping work nobody is doing.  Re-claiming your own task writes nothing and returns the same version, so a retry costs nothing and leaves no second event. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-108** 8A — keel ready: what can be worked on right now `p0` · cli, mcp, desktop, phase8, 8a

  Open, not blocked by anything live, parents excluded because their children are the real work. The ranking already exists in `next::rank` and is good: it orders by how many other tasks a task unblocks, so a p1 that releases three things beats a p0 that releases nothing.
  
  It has never had a front door. Today the only way to reach it is inside the roughly 3,500-token digest that `keel_context` returns, so asking "what should I do next" costs a full digest.
  
  Make it a CLI command, an MCP tool and a view in the app, all reading the same computation. Filters: `--unclaimed`, `--label`, `--no-label`, `--limit`, and `--milestone` so "what is next in Phase 8" is one flag.
  
  Waiting on TQ-31 for whether this is an MCP tool. It is a CLI command either way.
  
  Done when the same ranked list comes back from one command, one tool call and one click, and a test asserts the three agree.

  - Unblocked — TQ-31 settled as thirteen tools, so all three verbs get their own. Not started.  Two things the next session should know before picking this up, both learned the hard way today:  **The ranking already exists and is good.** `next::rank` in keel-core orders by how many other tasks a task unblocks. This work is a front door onto that computation, not a new computation. Three surfaces reading one function — CLI, tool, app view — and a test that asserts the three agree.  **If a required field is involved anywhere, put it in the constructor first.** KEEL-112 cost several hours because the check went into the storage layer while `Task::new` still took two arguments, so forty-nine call sites surfaced one failing test binary at a time across six rebuilds. `Milestone::new` had already established the pattern. The compiler finding every site at once is worth more than it looks.  Claiming and closing (KEEL-109, KEEL-110) touch the same storage path and are probably one sitting with this. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - Done, and the "three surfaces agree" claim is checked rather than asserted. A daemon test drives `keel_ready` over MCP and `/api/ready` over the local API and compares the references in order; the CLI reads the same endpoint, so there is one ranking with three doors. Verified against the live store too: `keel ready keel` and the app both said 11 ready in the same order, 9 under Phase 8.  **The filters live in `ReadyFilter`, in keel-core.** Putting them in the tool handler was the alternative and it would have meant the app filtering client-side against a list the daemon had already cut — so "unclaimed only" would have shown fewer than it should whenever the limit bit.  **Parent exclusion needed the whole task list, not the ranked one.** A parent whose children are all closed is still a parent, and its children may be absent from the ranking. Reading parenthood off the ranked list would have offered a parent as soon as its last child finished.  **Truncation is reported as a flag, not inferred.** `items.len() == limit` cannot tell ten-of-ten from ten-of-ninety, and hard constraint 4 is that a cut list says so.  Found while testing: **KEEL-111 has no milestone**, so it is in the unfiltered list and not in `--milestone "Phase 8"`. That is the filter being right and the row being unplaced — the gap C7 put on the board as "unplaced".  Not done, and left deliberately: `--label` and `--no-label` exist on the CLI and the tool but not as controls on the screen. The screen has unclaimed and milestone, which are the two that get asked; labels are a filter you reach for when you already know the label, which is a board question.  470 workspace tests, 200 desktop tests, clippy clean, fmt clean. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-107** C4 — Activity: make the rows go somewhere, then decide about grouping `p3` · desktop, phase8, 8c

  Activity is a reverse-chronological feed of up to 300 mutations, filterable by actor. Each row is a timestamp, an actor badge, a one-line summary and a session id, and none of them is a link. Confirmed: Activity.tsx has no anchor of any kind. Its own header says its job is "what did Claude do today", and it answers "what were the last 300 events".
  
  The spec offers a rework as "What changed", grouped by session, and a deletion, and calls it close. Waiting on TQ-35, which asks whether this screen is rebuilt as a session-grouped feed, fixed cheaply, or deleted.
  
  One thing found while checking, which the spec does not mention: TQ-29 established that notes leave no row in `events`. So a per-session change count built from events alone would miss the notes, which is the part most worth reading. Grouping by session means unioning events with notes, not just regrouping what is already fetched. That moves the cost up.
  
  The recommendation in TQ-35 is to make every row a link and add a time range first, which is cheap and removes the most-cited defect, and to decide about session grouping against a screen that works. Also move the "written outside a tracked session" treatment into the tooltip: that is a build-time concern showing as product copy, the same class KEEL-85 cleaned up.
  
  Blocked on TQ-35 until that choice between rebuilding, a cheap fix and deletion is made.

  - Done as the rebuild KB chose in TQ-35, not the cheaper fix I recommended. The screen is "What changed": seven sessions on this store, newest first, each expandable, and every one of the 56 rows inside the first session is a link.  **The union is the whole cost, and it is where the work went.** Notes leave no row in `events` (TQ-29), so a per-session account built from the feed would silently miss them — and this session's own account reads "created 3 things, 48 changes, wrote 5 notes", where the five notes are the part worth reading. `changes::by_session` unions the two streams, and the test that matters asserts a session which wrote a note shows the note. A test that only ever wrote fields would have passed against exactly the version of this that was not worth building.  **Its own endpoint, not a shape on `/api/activity`.** That URL *is* `keel_activity`, and the two answer different questions: the tool pages every mutation from a cursor for a model catching up; this answers "what did each session do" for a person who left Claude working. B-15's rule — the local API has more endpoints than the tool surface has tools.  **The limit counts changes, not sessions**, because a session can be four hundred of them and is still one row. On the live store 500 changes across 7 sessions is a cut list, and it says so.  **The screen kept its old name until it earned the new one.** C1 deliberately left it as "Activity" because "What changed" promised the grouped view it did not have. It is renamed now, along with its route, the palette entry and the shortcut.  **Found while building, and left alone: a close reads as five rows.** `close_reason`, `close_message`, `evidence` and `status` are four events from one call, because the store writes one event per field — deliberately, so "what changed" stays filterable by field. Collapsing them would need something identifying the call that produced them, and an event carries no correlation id. Worth a task if the noise bothers KB; it is not a fault in this screen.  **One thing I could not verify cleanly:** the browser console holds two stale errors from mid-edit — a `shortName` reference from before Ready.tsx reloaded, and a 404 for the deleted `Activity.tsx` from another chat's dev server watching it. Both screens render correctly after a reload and the typecheck is clean, so they are HMR artefacts rather than live faults. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

  The Board has a text filter plus status, priority and label menus and that part works. The Library sidebar has no filter at all, with around sixty documents in it. Cmd-K already does title matching across projects, documents and tasks, which is a good jump-to that most people will never find.
  
  Three changes:
  - A filter box in the Library sidebar, matching the Board's, narrowing titles within the selected type.
  - Say what the box does. "Filter…" does not tell you what it filters or how it differs from Search. "Filter these 94 tasks" and "Filter 62 documents" give the scope and the count, and the verb says it narrows what is already on screen.
  - Move the "Jump to…" button from the bottom of the sidebar to the top next to the project switcher, where it reads as the search affordance it is. Depends on the C1 rework.
  
  The rule to hold: filter narrows what is in front of you, instantly and literally. Search queries the whole store and understands meaning. The interface should never make you guess which one you are using.
  
  Done when both the board and the library have a filter box that names its scope and count.

  - Done. The board reads "Filter 120 tasks", the Library reads "Filter 46 documents", and "Jump to…" sits at the top of the rail beside the project switcher rather than in the footer.  The count is the part that does the work. "Filter…" said neither what it filtered nor how it differed from the Search screen; a number tells you the scope you are narrowing, and the verb tells you it is narrowing what is already on screen rather than querying the store. That is the distinction the two controls were making the reader guess at.  The Library's box landed with C2, since it had to know which kind was selected to count it. This task added the board's wording and the move.  Moving Jump-to was the smallest change and possibly the highest-value one: Cmd-K already indexed projects, documents and tasks, and the button announcing it was at the bottom of the rail where it read as a footer utility. It is now the thing next to the project name, which is where you look when you want to go somewhere.  187 desktop tests, typecheck clean. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-105** C5 — Search that suggests questions about this project `p2` · desktop, phase8, 8c

  The search box says "Ask a question — 'why is billing slow', 'what did customers say about onboarding'" and the empty state repeats the billing example (Search.tsx:128 and 138). Neither has anything to do with Keel. Both were copied from the MCP tool description, which is written for a generic project. KEEL-85 cleaned up this same pattern elsewhere and missed the one screen whose whole job is to invite a question.
  
  Replace them with starter chips built from the project's own content, which the digest already has: an open question quoted as-is, a recent decision phrased as a why, and a glossary term. Fall back to generic prose only when a project is genuinely empty.
  
  Two smaller things in the same screen: the eleven type facets are eleven chips of equal weight for something used occasionally, and the Menu primitive already used for scope would collapse them. And pressing `/` from the Board should land in search scoped to this project and filtered to tasks, not everything.
  
  Done when the starter queries come from the store, clicking one runs it, and an empty project still gets a sensible empty state.

  - Done. The billing example is gone from the placeholder and the empty state, replaced by three chips drawn from this project: an open question verbatim, a decision framed as a why, and a glossary term. One digest call, which the screen was not making before.  **The first version had three bugs and the browser found all three.** Worth recording, because each is the kind that unit tests with tidy fixtures would have missed:  1. **"why did we the plain-English rule covers every prose field".** The template was `why did we ${title}`, which assumes decision titles are verb phrases. In this store they are statements. Now `why did we decide that ${title}`, which is grammatical against a statement. 2. **"what is a anchor".** Article selection needs more than a vowel check, so the phrasing changed to `what does "Anchor" mean` — the form that needs no article and therefore cannot be wrong. 3. **The truncation leaked into the query.** Chips were shortened before being used, so clicking one searched for a string ending in "…". Truncation is now display-only via `chipLabel`; the query that runs is the full sentence. Verified in the address bar.  The three sources are not arbitrary. Each demonstrates a different thing semantic search is for: a question is prose you find by meaning, a decision-as-why finds reasoning whose title never contains the word "why", and a glossary term shows the store knows the project's own vocabulary.  Not done, and left deliberately: collapsing the eleven type facets into the `Menu` primitive, and scoping `/` from the board to tasks. Both are in the task body and neither is what the screen was failing at — the copy was. Worth their own pass rather than being smuggled in here.  189 desktop tests, typecheck clean. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-104** C7 — Show which phase a task belongs to, on the row `p1` · desktop, phase8, 8c

  A card shows the identifier, title, priority, kind and labels, but not what the task is part of. So the question asked most often about this project, "what is left in Phase 8", means opening each task or going to another screen.
  
  Checked against the code, and the spec overstates this. Board.tsx does read milestones: it fetches them (line 61), builds an id-to-name map (line 100), and feeds the filter bar (line 112). Grouping by milestone landed in KEEL-77 and filtering by milestone in KEEL-78, both working. The spec's claim that `milestone_id` is never read is wrong.
  
  What is actually missing is smaller than the spec suggests:
  - The chip on the row. A quiet mono chip in the right-hand gutter beside status, dashed and faded when a task has no milestone so an unassigned task looks unassigned. That gap is worth seeing: it usually means something was filed and never placed.
  - Making the chip the filter control, so clicking Phase 8 on a row narrows to that phase.
  - A done-count per group, so a phase heading reads "Phase 8 · 4 of 15".
  - Links both ways between Board and Roadmap. A milestone on the roadmap should reach its tasks; a chip should reach its milestone.
  
  The `keel ready --milestone` flag belongs with the ready work, not here.
  
  Done when every row shows what it is part of, an unassigned one shows that it is unassigned, the chip filters, and the two screens link to each other.

  - Done. Every one of the 120 cards on the board now carries its milestone, nine of them read "unplaced", and clicking "Phase 8" narrows the board to 23 with the filter in the address.  **The card had to be restructured, and that is the part worth knowing.** A card was a single `<a>`, and a chip that filters is a control — interactive content nested inside an anchor is invalid HTML. So the card is now a wrapper holding a stretched link (`after:absolute after:inset-0`) with the meta row `relative` so it sits above the overlay. The whole card stays clickable, the chip is a real button, and neither swallows the other.  **The chip shows "Phase 8", not "Phase 8 — The working loop".** Split on the dash rather than truncated to a width: a milestone with no dash keeps its whole name, where a fixed cut would turn "Q3 Launch" into "Q3 Lau…". The full name is the hover title.  **A second name map, deliberately.** `groupNames` already existed but carries task titles as well, because grouping by parent needs them. Looking a card's milestone up in that map would return a task title for any id that happened to match. `milestoneNames` is milestones only.  **No column in the list view.** `COLUMNS` drives sortable headers and `milestone` is not a `SortBy`, so adding one would have advertised an ordering that does not exist. The chip rides at the trailing edge of the task cell instead.  **A test caught the feature working.** "groups by milestone, and names it" broke on `getByText("Phase 6")` finding two elements — the column heading and the new chips beneath it. Narrowed to the heading rather than loosened, and a second test added for the chips.  The earlier finding stands: the spec was wrong that `milestone_id` is never read. Grouping (KEEL-77) and filtering (KEEL-78) already worked. What was missing was the chip, the done-count per group, and the two screens knowing about each other — the roadmap now links a milestone to its tasks.  177 desktop tests, typecheck clean, build green. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-103** C3 — One way to show a date, used on every screen `p1` · desktop, phase8, 8c

  The Roadmap says "shipped 8/9/2026" (Roadmap.tsx:93) and Metrics does the same for its latest reading (Metrics.tsx:212). Both call `toLocaleDateString()` directly instead of `when()`, the relative-time helper in ui.tsx:538.
  
  `when()` has three faults of its own:
  - It cannot say anything about the future. Milestone target dates are future dates, and it would render one as "-3d ago". The roadmap is where this bites.
  - It drops the year past seven days, so "Aug 9" gets ambiguous as soon as the project is a year old.
  - There is no way to see the exact timestamp. Only the revision menu in Documents offers one.
  
  Fix: extend `when()` to handle both directions (in 3 days, tomorrow, just now, 4m ago, yesterday, 3d ago, then an absolute date with the year when it is not this year), put the exact timestamp on hover using the Tooltip that already exists, and use it everywhere.
  
  Done when no screen formats a date itself, the roadmap reads "shipped 2h ago" and "due in 3 days", and a test covers a past date, a future date and a date in a previous year.

  - Done. The roadmap reads "shipped 2d ago" and "shipped yesterday" where it used to say "shipped 8/9/2026", and a sweep for `\d{1,2}/\d{1,2}/\d{4}` across the rendered page finds nothing.  All three faults in `when()` were real and are fixed. The future one mattered most: it subtracted with no negative branch, so a milestone target date — the single most likely future date in the app, on the screen built to show them — rendered as "-3d ago". It now reads "in 3 days" and "tomorrow".  **`now` is injectable.** Without it a boundary test either waits for the clock or asserts something vague. With it, "in 4m" and "4m ago" are both exact assertions against a fixed instant.  **A `When` component rather than only the helper**, because "the exact timestamp is always available" is a promise a bare function cannot keep — every call site would have to remember the tooltip. It also emits `<time dateTime>`, so the value stays machine-readable instead of being a string that looks like a date.  **Four call sites, not two.** The spec named Roadmap and Metrics. `Documents.tsx:157` was also formatting its own — the revision tooltip — and that one is the *exact* timestamp the task wanted available everywhere, so it now goes through `exactly()` rather than being left as the one screen with its own opinion.  One conversion worth knowing: Metrics stores `Point.at` as epoch milliseconds because the chart plots it numerically, so the call site converts back to ISO. The alternative was carrying both, which is how the two drift.  176 desktop tests, typecheck clean. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

Design images are invisible text rows.

A decision becomes a table — number, title, status, when, supersedes — because it is a register you scan or look up by number, and that is the payoff for having numbered them in Phase 7. Questions group by open and answered with the answer inline. Feedback becomes chronological cards with source and sentiment. Design becomes a thumbnail grid.
  
  Clicking a row in any of the alternative views still opens the document reader, so revision history and diff keep working everywhere and nothing is lost.
