That was the payoff for numbering decisions in Phase 7 and the flat list threw it away. **Questions group open-first** — 11 open, 39 settled — because "what is undecided" and "what did we decide" are different questions. **A design is a picture.** The thumbnail is one list call, not one per tile, because `blob_id` sits on the design entity itself.  **Which kind you are looking at is in the address** (`?kind=decision`), so a link to the decision register is a link.  **Two things I could not do cheaply, and did not fake:** - **The answer is not inlined under each question.** Bodies live in documents and the list endpoint does not carry them, so inlining would be one request per row against fifty rows. The grouping and the link are there; the inline answer wants a bulk body read. - **`supersedes` is shown as the status, not as a resolved link.** The edge is in the graph and reading it per row is a request per decision. `superseded` in the status column answers "was this overturned"; which decision replaced it is one click away.  **`loading="lazy"` had to come off the thumbnails.** With the tile plainly in the viewport, `currentSrc` stayed empty and the fetch never started, so the grid rendered empty boxes over a blob endpoint answering 200 with a valid PNG. Removing it fixed it immediately (240×120 natural size). At a handful of designs, deferring buys nothing worth a rendering quirk — worth a measurement before it comes back.  **The type badge is gone from the sidebar rows.** The switcher above already names the kind, so repeating it sixty times was the same word sixty times.  The reader is untouched and is still the destination from every index row — verified by clicking a register row through to `#/projects/keel/documents/dec_…` with the body rendered.  187 desktop tests (10 new), typecheck clean. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-101** C1 — Navigation is inside out: put the project first `p1` · desktop, phase8, 8c

  On launch the sidebar lists eight screens at the top and the project list underneath them. Five of the eight need a project, so they render at 35% opacity with a "Pick a project first" tooltip and refuse clicks — and the one control that would fix that is below them. You arrive, most of the menu is dead, you scroll past it to choose a project, then come back up. Confirmed in App.tsx: `NEEDS_PROJECT`, `opacity-35`, and the Projects block following the SCREENS block.
  
  The fix is project-first. A project switcher at the top under the wordmark showing the current project's name, opening the same list or handing off to Cmd-K which already indexes projects — one row instead of one per project. Beneath it that project's screens (Overview, Board, Roadmap, Library, Metrics), always live because a project is always selected. Below a divider the global things: All projects, Search, What changed. Remember the last project in local storage and select it on launch, so the disabled state stops existing rather than being styled.
  
  Number-key shortcuts stay, renumbered to the new order.
  
  Done when: no navigation item is disabled on a cold launch (a Phase 8 exit criterion); the last project is restored on reload; and a test asserts the disabled branch is unreachable when any project exists.

  - Done. The rail is project-first and nothing in it is disabled — verified in the browser on a cold launch with the memory cleared: eight links, zero with `aria-disabled`, zero without an `href`.  **The mechanism is that a project is always resolved, not that the disabled state is styled away.** `activeProject = route.project ?? fallbackProject`, so the project section is live even while you are on a global screen. `#/` shows Board pointing at `#/projects/keel/board`. The disabled branch and its "Pick a project first" tooltip are gone rather than hidden.  **`defaultProject` takes the live slug list rather than trusting what is stored.** A remembered project can be archived or renamed between sessions, and honouring that would open an empty screen under an address promising content. It falls through to the first project, and returns null only for a genuinely empty store — which is the one case a project screen cannot be shown, and the rail then renders the global items plus an empty state instead of five dead ones.  **A race worth naming.** The fallback is null while the project list is in flight, which is not the same as "there is no project". Without a `projectsLoaded` flag the canonicaliser would bounce a project-scoped address to Home a beat before the answer arrived — a redirect caused by latency rather than by the address being wrong. The flag makes "unknown" distinct from "none".  **`isProjectScreen` is deliberately not `NEEDS_PROJECT`,** and the difference is Roadmap. The router does not require a project for it because the screen renders across all of them, but in the rail it is a project screen and should link to the one you are in. Two different questions that happened to have the same answer for every other screen.  **Activity keeps its name.** §C1 lists it as "What changed", but that name promises the grouped-by-session view C4 would build, and the screen already carries one header claiming a job it does not do. Renaming it now would add a second. TQ-35 decides it along with whether the screen survives.  **Three existing tests asserted the old behaviour and were rewritten, not deleted.** Two only needed the renumbered keys. The third — "does nothing for a project-scoped screen with no project" — was guarding the bug rather than a property: doing nothing *was* the dead-control problem. It now asserts the fallback instead.  Not done here: moving "Jump to…" to the top next to the switcher is C6, which this unblocks.  172 desktop tests, typecheck clean, build green, no console errors. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-100** C0 — The design pass: Geist, a token layer, and a theme the user chooses `p0` · desktop, phase8, 8c

  The app uses the system font stack, which is the loudest possible signal that nobody chose anything, and the light scheme is a second copy of the palette inside a media query — which is how three status colours came to be missing from it before KEEL-73 fixed them by hand.
  
  Drop in `design/keel-tokens.css` over `apps/desktop/src/styles.css`. Checked: it is additive, not a rename — every token the app already uses keeps its name (`--color-surface`, `--color-ink-muted`, all eight text sizes), so no component needs touching to keep working. What it adds is Geist and Geist Mono self-hosted from `apps/desktop/public/fonts/` (already placed), a fourth sunken surface for the nav rail, a brass identity colour used only for the wordmark and the ready indicator, named spacing/radius/focus, and every colour pair declared once with `light-dark()` so a token cannot exist in one scheme and be absent from the other.
  
  Then a three-way theme control — system, light, dark — as `data-theme` on `<html>`, with a segmented control in the rail. Today the OS decides and there is no control anywhere.
  
  Done when: the app renders in Geist with no network request; the theme control switches all three ways and the choice survives a reload; a test asserts no `--color-*` token is declared in one scheme only; and `grep` finds no raw hex or `oklch(` outside the token block.
  
  Everything else in 8C reads against these tokens, so this goes first.

  - Done. Tokens in, Geist self-hosted, three-way theme control, all verified in a running browser rather than by reading the CSS.  **The token file was a clean drop-in, as hoped.** Every name the app already used survived, so no component needed touching. Verified in the page: `p-4` still resolves to 16px, so defining `--spacing-hair` and friends does not disturb Tailwind v4's numeric scale — the named keys sit alongside the `--spacing` multiplier rather than replacing it.  **A verification trap worth knowing.** My first check injected a `<div class="bg-surface-sunken rounded-card text-brand">` at runtime and read the computed style. Every new token came back unset, which looks exactly like "the tokens do not work". They were fine. Tailwind v4 generates utilities by scanning source files, so a class that exists only in a string created at runtime is a class it has never seen. The honest check is real usage: once the rail actually used `bg-surface-sunken` and the wordmark `text-brand`, both resolved (0.13 sunken against 0.16 body, brass at `oklch(0.55 0.12 70)`).  **The theme is applied in `main.tsx` before `createRoot`,** not in a `useEffect`. Applying it on mount means the first paint uses the default scheme and then jumps.  **Found and fixed, unrelated but blocking:** four `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` errors in `lib/api.test.ts` from commit 61b8582 meant `tsc --noEmit` failed, and `npm run build` runs that first — so the desktop production build has been red since yesterday and nothing said so. `npm test` passes independently of `tsc`, which is why it went unnoticed. Fixed with four non-null assertions matching the file's existing style. The build now produces a bundle, and the fonts land in `dist/fonts/` and are referenced by relative path, so the packaged Tauri app has them with no network.  **Two test-writing notes,** both cases of a test that would have passed against nothing: 1. `import STYLES from "../styles.css?raw"` returns the empty string under vitest, which stubs CSS imports. Every assertion about the stylesheet would have passed vacuously. Reading from disk instead. 2. The "no second palette" check first matched the bare string `prefers-color-scheme`, which appears in the token file's own header explaining what it replaced. Matching `/@media[^{]*prefers-color-scheme/` distinguishes a media query from a sentence about one.  Not done here, deliberately: the rail still shows the C1 dead-nav problem, and `bg-surface-raised` is still used by cards elsewhere. Those are C1 and C2.  162 desktop tests pass, typecheck clean, build green. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-99** A milestone cannot be created without a plain-English explainer `p0` · mcp, storage, phase8

  The roadmap shows Phase 6 and Phase 7 as bare names with no description, because `keel_create` takes a `body` for a milestone and throws it away. `build_entity` (crates/keel-mcp/src/dispatch.rs:1070) calls `Milestone::new(project, name)` and never reads `body`, while the same function routes it into `description` for a project and `body` for a task. `Milestone.summary` exists (crates/keel-core/src/types.rs:307) and nothing writes it.
  
  Phases 0 to 5 have summaries because `keel bootstrap` builds the struct directly. The two created through the tool surface, 6 and 7, are the two that are empty.
  
  An input that is accepted and discarded is worse than one that is refused: the caller never finds out.
  
  Done when:
  - `Milestone::new` takes the summary as a required argument, so the compiler finds every call site.
  - `keel_create` declares it required for milestones and refuses a missing or empty one, saying what would be valid.
  - The tool description carries the style rule with a good and a bad example: one or two sentences, plain English, no AI register. That register is KB's own standard, written down as B-45.
  - A length ceiling is enforced (the existing summaries run 8 to 15 words; a paragraph is too long).
  - Phase 6 and Phase 7 have summaries written from what they actually shipped.
  - Tests cover a refused create and a round-trip to the roadmap.
  
  KB's call on 2026-08-11, recorded as decision B-45 with the reasoning behind making the field required.

  - Done, and verified against the running daemon rather than only in tests: `keel_create` for a milestone with no explainer now comes back with `invalid milestone field summary`, naming what would be valid, and leaves no row behind.  **The signature is the enforcement.** `Milestone::new(project, name, summary)` takes it positionally, so the compiler found all seven call sites instead of review having to. Two of those — `bootstrap.rs` and `fixture.rs` — were already setting `summary` immediately after constructing, which is what made the gap invisible: the paths a person looked at did the right thing by hand, and the one nobody looked at silently did not.  **Validation is in `keel-core`, on both doors.** `validate_entity` is called on create *before* the idempotency lookup, so a bad write is refused rather than quietly matching an existing row and reporting success. It is also called on update, because enforcing only on create means the requirement holds for the first write and any later call can blank the explainer back out.  **`body` is accepted as well as `summary`.** A caller reaching for the generic prose field means the same thing for a milestone, and silently discarding it is the exact failure this task existed to remove. A daemon test asserts the `body` path lands in `summary`.  **Three things the run turned up that were not the task:**  1. **The disk was full** — 266Mi free, `target/` at 15G — and it presented as *13 failing daemon tests*. The link step was failing with `errno=28`, which surfaces as a test failure rather than as a disk error. Clearing `target/debug/incremental` (1.6G) was enough; it costs incremental-compile speed rather than a rebuild. Worth knowing that this repo has hit the same wall before — B-11 records dropping `--all-features` for filling the disk.  2. **I misread a red suite as green, and the cause was my own shell.** I ran `cargo test --workspace 2>&1 > file`, which sends stderr to the terminal and stdout to the file — the reverse of what was intended — and then checked `$?` on the trailing `grep` rather than on cargo. Both halves were wrong in the same direction: a partially-written file showed "197 passed, 0 failures" while the suite was failing. Corrected to `> file 2>&1` and an explicit `CARGO_EXIT=$?`. The general point is that a test summary read from a file is only evidence if the exit code came from the test runner.  3. **The `insta` snapshot on the tool list caught the schema change**, which is what it is for — the tool list is an API contract. Reviewed the diff before accepting: it is the `summary` property and nothing else.  The daemon test I added asserts the refusal at the MCP boundary rather than only at the store, because the MCP path is where the bug was — the store was never asked, so a store-only test would not have caught it.  436 workspace tests, clippy clean with `-D warnings`, fmt clean. Binaries rebuilt and installed; the daemon is running the enforcing build. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-98** 8C — Make the app legible `p0` · desktop, phase8, 8c

  Parent for the eight pieces of §8C. The app is what KB looks at every day and none of §8C depends on anything else in the phase, which is why it leads.
  
  Three design attachments accompany this section and are the design rather than a description of it: `design/keel-design-system.html`, `design/keel-screens.html`, `design/keel-tokens.css`, plus the two Geist woff2 files already placed in `apps/desktop/public/fonts/`.
  
  Done when the eight children are done.

- **KEEL-97** SSE never opens in the in-app browser, cause unknown `p3`

  Live refresh: the daemon now emits an SSE comment immediately on connect rather than waiting up to 15s for the first keep-alive, and the Vite dev proxy no longer buffers. Verified with curl both directly and through the proxy — `: keel` arrives at once.
  
  The browser end is NOT verified. In the in-app Browser pane an `EventSource` stays in `CONNECTING` indefinitely: `onopen` never fires, `onerror` never fires, and a real change event emitted while it watched was not received — despite curl reading the same stream through the same proxy successfully.
  
  So the buffering fix is real and the remaining fault is elsewhere: either that browser does not support SSE properly, or something in its request interception holds the response. Untested in a normal browser and in the packaged Tauri build, where there is no proxy at all.
  
  Next step is simply to open the app in Chrome or Safari and watch `readyState`. If it is 1 there, this is a quirk of the test browser and the task closes.

  - Live-refresh probe from real Chrome. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - Fixed, and verified in real Chrome. `readyState` 1, a live `change` event received from a write made while the page watched.  **The last cause was my own fix.** Chasing the proxy buffering, I added a `res.flushHeaders()` call in Vite's `proxyRes` hook. That sends the response head *before* http-proxy has copied the upstream headers onto it, so the reply reached the browser with **no `content-type` at all**. `EventSource` needs `text/event-stream` to accept a stream, so it sat in `CONNECTING` forever.  curl did not care about the missing header and printed the body happily, which is exactly why it looked fixed. The measurement that broke it open was `fetch()` in the page reporting `status: 200, content-type: null` — a header the same request showed correctly one layer down.  Three things were wrong in sequence, and only the first two were real:  1. **The daemon sent nothing until the first change or the 15-second keep-alive.** A stream with no bytes is one an intermediary can sit on. It now yields an SSE comment on connect. Genuine, and the fix stands. 2. **The dev proxy compressed the stream**, buffering a whole gzip block. `accept-encoding: identity` on that path. Genuine. 3. **`flushHeaders()` stripped the content type.** Self-inflicted, removed.  Also learned, and worth knowing: `keel_note` writes do **not** announce. The daemon only announces when the latest event id advances, and a note leaves no row in `events`. So the app does not live-refresh on a note. That is arguably right — notes are a stream of their own — but it is undocumented behaviour and I found it by a probe silently receiving nothing. Worth a look separately rather than changing blind.  The earlier claim that this was "a quirk of the test browser" was wrong: real Chrome behaved identically, which is what made it worth chasing rather than dismissing. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
  - TQ-29 verification: this note must announce. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-95** keel_create fails on decisions written during the number migration window `p2`

  Reported from another project: keel_create with type decision failed reproducibly with `read column number of decisions: Invalid column type Null`, having worked earlier the same morning.
  
  Migration 10 added decisions.number at 18:43:57 and backfilled everything that existed. At 18:45:21 - 84 seconds later - a daemon that had the column but not the struct field inserted a decision, and the row got a NULL. Reading it strictly made every decision in that project unreadable, and since the idempotency check is a read, create failed too.

  - Fixed in a5629fc. Three parts, and the middle one is the point.  **Proportion.** A NULL number now reads as 0 - which already meant "not yet assigned" everywhere else - instead of erroring. One unnumbered row should cost that row's label, not make the whole artifact type unreadable in that project. The strict read turned a single bad row into "decisions are broken", and because the idempotency lookup is itself a read, into "creates are broken" as well.  **Not leniency alone.** Reading NULL as 0 by itself would trade one failure for a worse one: two rows written back at 0 collide on the unique index. The update path now assigns a real number to anything holding 0, matching what create already did.  **Migration 13** repairs rows already written, covering tasks as well - it had the identical shape and survived only because migration 6 landed in a quieter minute.  Verified against the live store before and after: the affected project read 500 and now returns its decision; keel_create in it succeeds; Keel's own B-1..B-43 are untouched.  The generalisable lesson, recorded as B-44: **a column added by a migration is NULL for every writer that has not been restarted yet.** Any read of a newly-added column has to tolerate that window or the migration becomes an outage for whoever writes during it. This window was 84 seconds wide and still caught a real project.  Also on me: the report arrived because another project was using the store while I was mid-migration. Nothing warned either side. Worth considering whether the daemon should refuse to serve when its binary predates the store's schema version, which would have turned a silent corruption into a startup error. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-94** One decision register: fold the B-n prose table into the decision rows `p2`

  Split out of KEEL-92 because it is engineering, not editing.
  
  The two decision registers do not contain each other, which is why neither can simply be deleted:
  
  - `product/DECISIONS.md` holds a numbered table, B-1 to B-25, with the full reasoning — several hundred words per row for the ones that matter.
  - `.keel/decisions/` holds 39 artifact files, one per decision row, each a short summary. Only 11 of them carry a B-n at all.
  
  So 39 rows and 25 table entries overlap partially in both directions: rows with no table entry, table entries whose reasoning exists nowhere else, and eleven that are genuinely the same decision written twice at different lengths.
  
  What it needs:
  
  1. **A stable readable identifier for decisions.** Only `tasks` has `number` (migration 6). B-n exists solely as prose, which is why `fsck`'s `unresolved_id_reference` check cannot resolve a `B-n` citation and has to be scoped around the whole family — see KEEL-66, where that limitation is the reason the check misses the exact case that motivated it. Giving decisions a number is a schema change and therefore KB's call.
  2. **Migrating the fuller reasoning** from the 25 table rows into the artifact bodies, so the artifacts become authoritative rather than summaries of a document.
  3. **A generated `product/DECISIONS.md`**, rendered from the rows the way `render-status` renders the tracker.
  
  Point 3 raises a question worth answering once for all aggregates: `render-status` writes to `project.status_path`, but the mirror's aggregates (`.keel/questions.md`, `.keel/glossary.md`) have hardcoded paths under `.keel/`. `product/QUESTIONS.md` was archived rather than made generated for exactly this reason — the register moved to `.keel/questions.md`, which is generated and complete but sits in a bookkeeping directory rather than where the documentation lives. Either the aggregates get configurable paths, or `product/` is accepted as the wrong place for two of them.

  - Steps 1 and 2 done. Step 3 — a generated `product/DECISIONS.md` — is not, and is the whole of what remains.  **Done: numbers.** Migration 10 adds `number` to decisions and backfills in creation order, which is correct for every store but this one. Migration 11 maps Keel's own B-1..B-25 explicitly by id, in two phases because the permutation overlaps itself and the unique index would reject the intermediate state. It is a no-op on any other store. `KEEL-B12` resolves through `keel_get` alongside `KEEL-42`, via a separate parser — `KEEL-12` and `KEEL-B12` are both valid and name different artifacts, so an optional `B` would resolve typos to real rows.  The mapping was made by hand and that was not caution for its own sake: fuzzy title matching got 4 of 25 wrong, two of them with high confidence. Three table entries (B-23, B-24, B-25) had no row at all and were written as rows first; one row (Fixture links, now B-26) had no table entry. The two registers genuinely did not contain each other in either direction.  **Done: the reasoning.** All 25 bodies now hold the table's full text, merged rather than replaced — 7 `## Context` sections and 1 `## Superseded` section were carried across. The first attempt read `data.body` where the API returns `data.document.body`, so every existing body looked empty; it would have written the merge as a wholesale replacement and dropped all 8 sections without erroring. Caught by asserting on a shrink report rather than by reading the output.  **Not done: rendering.** Four decisions the next session has to make, none of them mechanical:  1. **Where the renderer writes.** `render-status` writes to `project.status_path`; the mirror's aggregates are hardcoded under `.keel/`. Cheapest is a `decisions_path` column parallel to `status_path`, but that is the second such column and the third aggregate — worth deciding once whether aggregates get configurable paths generally. 2. **The DECISIONS.md spec must be archived first**, as `product/QUESTIONS.md` was, or generation reports the path collision and writes neither. That is correct behaviour, not a bug to work around. 3. **`product/DECISIONS.md` is not only decisions.** It also carries a Format section, the P0-2 dependency-verification findings, and the MCP deltas. Those are measurements, not decisions, and they have nowhere to go — they need their own artifact rather than being deleted or forced into a decision row. 4. **Reversals should derive from `status = 'superseded'`** plus the row's `## Superseded` section, rather than being a hand-kept table. Two rows exist for it today: B-24, and the SPEC §8.1 hook reversal, which currently has no decision row at all.  Until step 3 lands, `product/DECISIONS.md` is still hand-maintained prose and the B-n table in it now duplicates the rows rather than exceeding them — so the file is stale-able but no longer the only copy of anything.  **Not done, and worth its own decision:** `fsck` still scopes the `B-n` family out of `unresolved_id_reference`. The scoping rule is "only check a family where the project titles artifacts that way", and it now *could* check B-n against decision numbers. That is the payoff this task was justified by, and it is a small change to `fsck.rs` that I have not made. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>
  - Step 3 done. One decision register, generated.  `product/DECISIONS.md` is now rendered from the 42 decision rows by `render_decisions`, written to the project's new `decisions_path` (migration 12) with the same collision rule as the tracker: a document that has adopted the path wins and the conflict is reported, because prose cannot be regenerated and a rendered view can. The prose spec that used to own the file is archived.  KB chose a second nullable column over a general aggregate-path map: three aggregates exist, two are content under `.keel/`, and a stored map is more machinery than three instances justify. Revisit at a fourth.  The layout is index, then Reversals, then the decisions. Reversals go above rather than at the end — a reversal met after acting on the original is met too late. It derives from `status = 'superseded'` plus the row's `## Superseded` section rather than a hand-kept table, and a row marked superseded with no such section renders as a data problem rather than a heading over silence. B-24 is the first entry.  The non-decision content moved to `product/DEPENDENCIES.md`: the P0-2 verification table and the MCP deltas, headed as a snapshot taken on a date rather than a description of now, with a closing section naming the three things about it that are already false. They are the evidence behind B-2, B-3, B-4, B-12 and B-17, so deleting them would have left five decisions citing a verification nobody could read.  **fsck now checks the B-n family.** `unresolved_id_reference` resolves `B-n` against the `decisions.number` column instead of skipping the family, and every citation in this repository resolves — zero findings. That was the payoff this task was justified by, and it closes the gap named in KEEL-66.  **Two things found on the way, neither fixed:**  1. **The immutability guard is on the wrong door.** `keel_update` refuses a title change on an `accepted` decision — correctly, per SPEC — but `keel_write_doc` rewrote all 25 decision bodies without objection. The title is guarded and the content is not, which is backwards: the reasoning is the decision. Raised as TQ-27. 2. **Seven decision titles are truncated in storage** (B-5, B-7, B-8, B-11, B-18, B-19, B-22), cut at about 80 characters by whatever imported them. B-8 reads "Surface carries five values, not four: chat \". This was invisible while the prose table carried the real titles and is now the heading in the index. The full text survives in each body's `## Decision` line, so nothing is lost — but the headings are wrong, and fixing them is blocked by the immutability rule above, which is the honest reason to settle that first rather than route around it. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-93** fsck died on its own staleness check, and the test suite was red `p0` · correctness, cli

  Found while running the suite before starting the detail view. `keel fsck` failed outright — not one check reporting a finding, the whole run dying — because `stale_in_progress` binds `updated_at < now() - INTERVAL 3 DAY`, and that is a binder error against the embedded DuckDB. It landed with the staleness guard in ead61c9 and the workspace has been red ever since; the test written for it used the same expression, so it never passed either.

  - Fixed by computing the cutoff in Rust and binding it, rather than by correcting the SQL. `now()` is TIMESTAMPTZ where `updated_at` is TIMESTAMP, so the first attempt was `current_localtimestamp()` — and that failed too, with `-(TIMESTAMP, INTERVAL)` and an *empty* candidate list, even though the identical expression binds fine in the standalone DuckDB 1.5.5 shell. Something about the embedded connection's function catalogue, and not worth chasing: a bound parameter sidesteps it entirely and states the rule more plainly. The threshold is now a named constant instead of a number inside a SQL string.  Two things worth keeping from this:  **It failed at bind time, so one bad check killed all 28.** `fsck` is the integrity audit, and it could not run at all — which surfaced as a crash rather than as a finding, so it read as "the tool is broken" rather than "your store might be". A check that cannot run is worse than a missing one.  **The test never passed.** It backdated a row with the same `now() - INTERVAL 5 DAY` and unwrapped the result, so it failed on the same binder error the check did. Both were written in one sitting and neither was run — which is the specific failure the definition of done's "tests written **and** passing" exists to catch. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-92** Document reset and the question prune `p2` · docs, phase7

  RESET-PLAN 7.7. Last, so it describes the finished state. Rewrite README.md to say what is true today — the test count is wrong and it claims the gate is unrun after seven runs. Retire PROBLEMS.md, or date it clearly as a historical snapshot; it reads as current and its central claim was refuted by the review it was written for. One decision register: the numbered prose table inside DECISIONS.md and the thirty separate artifact files must become one thing — the product's own integrity checker has to be specially configured to route around the split. Same for the two question registers. Prune the questions from 17 to about seven real ones: two are finished, seven have working answers nobody has objected to. Correct the three known errors in the SPEC and mark the reversed decision as superseded — the Reversals table built for exactly this is empty. Cut the duplication in JOURNAL.md, which re-narrates four other documents nearly section by section.

  - Done, with one half split out as KEEL-94.  **The question prune: 18 open, not 17, and now 8.** Ten had shipped answers nobody had objected to — TQ-2, TQ-4, TQ-5, TQ-8, TQ-14, TQ-15, Q-2, Q-3, Q-4, Q-7. Each got the answer written into its body before the status moved, because "answered" with no answer is the same information loss as a status with no note. Two of them named SPEC errors and were closed by fixing the SPEC, not by asserting the fix.  **One register for questions, achieved by generating it rather than by deleting a copy.** `.keel/questions.md` carried only unresolved rows, which is why `product/QUESTIONS.md` existed at all: something had to hold the settled ones, and that something was hand-maintained prose. The aggregate now emits both halves, Open first, with the reasoning attached to each. `product/QUESTIONS.md` is archived and its file removed; both CLAUDE.md files and HANDOFF.md point at the generated register. HANDOFF used to name specific question numbers and had gone stale within a day — it now names the file and says why.  **KEEL-94, split out.** The decision registers cannot be unified the same way: the B-n prose table and the 39 artifact files do not contain each other. Only 11 artifacts carry a B-n, the artifacts are summaries where the table has the full reasoning, and decisions have no `number` column — only tasks do. That last point is not cosmetic: it is exactly why fsck cannot resolve a `B-n` citation and misses the case that motivated it (KEEL-66). Needs a schema decision, so it is KB's.  **Three things I found while writing rather than while looking:**  1. `.keel/README.md` — generated on every run, into every project Keel touches — still promised that a Claude Code hook converts your edit into an attributed revision. That claim outlived the hook by a day and would have kept propagating. 2. `product/CLAUDE.md`, the contract loaded into every session, said the same thing and cited SPEC §8.1 for it. It also still told sessions to update a changelog that now derives itself, and to use `blocked` as a status that no longer exists. 3. SPEC §8.1 was 15 lines arguing why one read of the mirror was safe. Rewritten as a superseded section that says the hook never ran, why deleting beat repairing, and that the rule now has no exception. The Reversals table — empty since it was built — has its first two rows: this, and B-24.  **JOURNAL: 6,029 words to 4,617.** Four sections re-told the gate runs and now point at GATE.md. What was kept is the part GATE.md cannot carry: that I measured an instrument for five evenings while believing I was measuring an agent, and that what caught it was a rule (`observed == launched`) rather than reading more carefully.  **README: every load-bearing number in it was wrong.** 264 tests → 408 plus 146 desktop; nine tools → ten; the gate described as unrun when it had run seven times and closed; `plugin/` described as containing "the mirror hook". Added a fourth entry to "most likely to bite" — `blocked` is not a status — and the pre-commit install line, since nothing told anyone to install it.  Verified end to end: hand-edited five generated files, `keel import` round-tripped all five, `generate --check` up to date at 51 files. 408 Rust tests, 146 desktop, clippy and fmt clean. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

The six are archived in Keel with revisions intact, not deleted, and their files are gone from the repo. KEEL-66's own note says the fsck check does NOT catch the case that motivated it — no project titles artifacts D-n, because Keel's decisions live as a numbered table inside a prose document rather than as rows, and a lexical check cannot distinguish "cites a convention documented in prose" from "cites nothing". The page now says partly addressed and names the limitation. That is the two-decision-registers problem arriving as a concrete cost, and it is the argument for KEEL-92's unification. - **KEEL-90** Protocol honesty, and one name for the daemon address `p2` · mcp, daemon, phase7

  RESET-PLAN 7.5 and 7.6. Advertise both protocol revisions, since both are accepted. Return "tool not found" as a tool error rather than HTTP 404, which by the protocol's own documentation means "there is no MCP server at this address" — a typo currently looks like the server vanished. Keep the server's introduction text in one place; it exists word-for-word in two source files, so editing one makes the two ways a client can ask "who are you" disagree. And the daemon's address is spelled three different ways across the hooks, hardcoded a fourth time in the plugin config and defaulted a fifth time in the CLI: pick one environment variable and make everything read it.

- **KEEL-89** Make the local API and the MCP tools genuinely one thing `p1` · daemon, mcp, phase7

  RESET-PLAN 7.4. The code claims they can never drift; they already have. Route every endpoint through the same dispatch path — four bypass it. Fix list-type parameters being silently dropped: asking the web API to search only specs returns everything, with no error, while the same request over MCP filters correctly. Stop coercing numeric-looking text into numbers, so searching for "404" stops failing with "query must be a string". One error shape across the API, not three. Allow POST in the CORS rules so the one POST endpoint is reachable from the desktop app. Stop scanning up to 100,000 event rows twice per tool call, under the global write lock, just to read the last event id.

  - Done. Both drift bugs reproduced exactly as the plan described, and both had one cause.  `params_to_json` guessed types from the *value*: anything that parsed as an integer became a number, "true"/"false" became booleans, everything else stayed a string. So `?types=spec` was passed as a bare string, the tool ignored it, and a search restricted to specs returned specs, decisions, questions and tasks with no error at all. And `?query=404` parsed as an integer, arrived as the number 404, and was rejected with "query must be a string" — the one search term guaranteed to be numeric being an HTTP status code, which is exactly what anyone searches a project for.  It now reads the *declared* type out of the tool's own schema. That is what makes the two surfaces the same surface rather than two that resemble each other: the schema being consulted is the one the tool advertises, so this class of drift cannot recur. Verified live — `?types=spec` returns only specs, `?query=404` returns a result set.  **One error shape**, `{code, message}`, the same one MCP returns. There were three: a bare string, `{message}`, and the full form. The desktop client reads `error.message`, so the bare-string form arrived as `undefined` and the app showed "Request failed (400)" — losing the explanation in exactly the case where the daemon had bothered to give one.  **POST is allowed by CORS.** `/api/generate` is the one endpoint the app needs in order to *do* anything rather than read, and it was unreachable from the browser for as long as the rule said GET only.  **`latest_event` is one row.** It fetched up to 100,000 events and took the last, twice per tool call, while holding the global write lock. Now `SELECT max(id) FROM events` — correct only because ULIDs are minted monotonically (B-9), which is the same property the event cursor depends on.  **Not done, and I think the plan is wrong about it:** "route every endpoint through the same dispatch path — four bypass it". `/api/entities`, `/api/notes`, `/api/document` and `/api/graph` bypass because there is no tool for them, and there is deliberately no tool for them — an accepted decision says Keel's local API has more endpoints than the MCP surface has tools, because a UI knows exactly what it wants while a model chooses worse among more options. Routing them through dispatch would mean four new tools and a worse tool surface. What they actually needed was to stop differing in the ways that mattered: parameter typing and error shape. Both now come from one place.  Found while fixing the test for this: the old assertion checked that `include_archived` became a boolean on `keel_search` — a parameter `keel_search` does not take. The value-guessing conversion made it a boolean anyway, so the test passed while describing a parameter that was being discarded one layer down. With typing driven by the schema, the test had to name a tool that really declares it. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-88** Tidy the tool surface `p1` · mcp, phase7

  RESET-PLAN 7.3. Settle on ten tools and say ten everywhere — the codebase says "nine" in five places and the skill's tool table omits keel_note entirely. Fix the description whose example uses `id` where the parameter is `ids`. Put the seven undocumented parameter names into the schema or stop accepting them. Mark the required fields on keel_note, which declares none. Mark archive and remove as destructive; they claim not to be. Make keel_update's description say when to use keel_write_doc instead — only one side of that pair explains the split today. Delete the dead code: the unread constant, the unused error code, the handling for two unadvertised protocol methods, the test-only lookup, the two phantom session markers. Add one test asserting the tool list and the dispatch code agree, so that class of drift cannot recur.

  - Done, with one correction to the plan's numbers and one finding.  - **Ten is said as ten**, in `tools.rs`, the daemon's comment, the bootstrap text — and the skill's tool table now has `keel_note`, which it omitted entirely. - **`keel_get(id: spec_id)` in the description is now `keel_get(ids: [spec_id])`.** A model copying it verbatim got an error. - **`keel_note` declares `required: ["id"]`** — it declared nothing. Not `body`, deliberately: it is required for the common case but not when listing or retracting, and demanding it would make those two uncallable. - **`destructiveHint` is per-tool** instead of hardcoded `false` for all ten. The old comment justified it as "nothing in Keel is ever deleted", which conflates *recoverable* with *additive*. The annotation a host gates on means non-additive: `keel_update` overwrites fields and can archive, `keel_link` can remove an edge. Those two are true; the rest, including `keel_write_doc` and `keel_note`, are genuinely append-only. - **`keel_update` now says what `keel_write_doc` is for.** Only one side of the pair explained the split, so a model reading the other would put a body in the wrong place.  **The undocumented parameters were four, not seven, and they could not simply be dropped.** `keel_create` accepts `name`, `term`, `slug` and `definition` with none of them in the schema. `name` and `term` were at least mentioned in the `title` prose; `slug` was invisible — and a project *cannot be created without one*. So they are declared now rather than removed.  **Dead code:** `META_CLIENT_CAPABILITIES` (nothing read it) and the `Mcp-Name` validation for `prompts/get` and `resources/read` — this server advertises neither capability and routes neither method, so validating them read as support for them. I could not find the "two phantom session markers" the plan names; if they existed they are already gone.  `strip_banner_for_test` is renamed `strip_banner_public`: it stopped being test-only when the pre-commit check started needing exactly that rule. A name that says "test only" on something the product depends on is worse than no name.  And the drift the plan wanted closed cannot recur: a test dispatches every advertised tool and asserts none answers "no tool named that", plus the reverse — and a second test pins the count at ten, because a number in prose drifts and a number in an assertion does not. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-87** Delete the file-edit hook, and replace it with a check that fails loudly `p0` · plugin, mirror, phase7

  RESET-PLAN 7.2, KB decision 3: delete, not repair. Two of the hook's three moving parts do not work and its stated safety guarantee is false. It calls `keel mirror`, a command that does not exist, so it fails silently every time — which makes the claim written into it and into the plugin README ("the database wins unconditionally afterwards, the file is regenerated") untrue, and opens the window for silent divergence the design says cannot exist. It also captures the document's heading along with the body, so every round trip adds another copy of the title. And it reads KEEL_SESSION_ID, which nothing anywhere sets, so every revision it writes is attributed to nobody. Delete plugin/hooks/mirror-edit.sh and replace it with a pre-commit check that refuses a commit when a generated file has been hand-edited, telling you to make the change in Keel instead. A mechanism that silently does not work is worse than no mechanism.

  - Done. `plugin/hooks/mirror-edit.sh` is deleted, its `PostToolUse` registration removed, and `scripts/pre-commit` replaces it — installed as this repo's git hook and verified: staging a hand-edited `product/STATUS.md` refuses the commit and names the file.  All three of the plan's claims held, and one of them turned out to have a better explanation than "a command that does not exist". `~/.local/bin/keel` is a build from 9 August with a `mirror` subcommand and no `generate`: **`keel mirror` existed when the hook was written and was renamed underneath it.** The hook then swallowed the failure with `|| true`, so nothing said so for a day. That is a more useful lesson than the original reading — the danger was not a typo, it was a shell script calling a CLI with no test holding the two together.  Two things the replacement does that the original did not:  **It distinguishes "could not run the check" from "the files are wrong."** A daemon holding the store lock, or no usable binary, exits 0 with a message rather than refusing the commit. Reporting one as the other is how a green check comes to mean nothing.  **It prefers the repository's own build over `PATH`.** Found by the first run of my own test: it picked up the stale binary, got "unrecognized subcommand 'generate'", and reported drift that was not there — the same disease as a hook that silently does nothing, in the opposite direction.  The plugin README's guarantee is gone with the hook. It said "the database wins unconditionally afterwards, the file is regenerated", and that was untrue for as long as it was written down. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-86** One authority per instruction `p0` · plugin, phase7

  RESET-PLAN 7.1. Three files each tell Claude the same three things in slightly different words, and two contradict each other: the skill tells Claude to invent its own session identifier while the session-start hook tells it to use a specific one and not invent one — both are in context at the same time, and the stop hook only recognises the hook's format, so a session that follows the skill, writes correctly, and is then told it recorded nothing. Decide who owns each: session identity → the session-start hook (delete the skill's competing instruction, make the stop hook accept whatever the hook issued); when to write → the skill (the hook points at it rather than restating it); the end-of-session check → the stop hook, in one sentence. Then make the session-start hook fire only at actual session start, not on every compaction — it re-injects a 250-word preamble every time the conversation compacts.

  - Done. Each instruction now has exactly one author, and the plugin README states the assignment in a table so a future edit has somewhere to be checked against.  - **Session identity → the session-start hook.** The skill's "mint one identifier" section is gone, replaced by "use the one the hook gave you". That is what closes the false nag: the stop hook already accepted `ses_<uuid>` and the bare uuid, and the only form it could not recognise was the one the *skill* told sessions to invent. A session could follow the skill, write correctly, and be told at the end it had recorded nothing. - **What to write → the skill.** The hook now points at it instead of restating it. - **The end-of-session check → the stop hook, in one sentence.** It was a paragraph re-teaching what to record and how to phrase it. - **The hook no longer fires on compaction.** `source == "compact"` exits immediately. It was re-injecting the preamble and digest every time context was compacted — spending a few hundred tokens restating what the conversation already knew, at exactly the moment context was scarcest.  **Where I departed from RESET-PLAN 7.1, deliberately.** The plan assigns "when to write" to the skill and says the hook should point at it. Two instructions stay in the hook anyway — "record it, don't offer to" and "claim the task before you start" — because a skill is model-invoked and thirty headless sessions with `keel` installed invoked it zero times (TQ-19). An instruction that only lives in a file nobody opens is not an instruction, and moving those two into the skill would have undone the measured improvement that produced them. The skill keeps everything that elaborates: what belongs where, the artifact vocabulary, conflict handling. One authority per instruction is preserved; the assignment differs from the plan's for one item, and the reason is on the record. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-85** Speak English in the interface `p2` · desktop, phase6

  RESET-PLAN 6.6. The internals are showing. Remove from the UI: raw ULIDs as primary labels with a tooltip telling you to click and copy; the `digest ≈ 12,400 tokens` readout and its "over budget" warning, which is an agent-context measurement on a human's dashboard; tool names in empty states ("Ask Claude to write one — keel_write_doc"); CLI instructions ("run `keel fixture` against a scratch store"); internal decision references like "D-10" in tooltips. "Unattributed" becomes "written outside a tracked session". The digest size readout moves to a diagnostics screen or disappears.

  - Done. Five pieces of the build showing through, removed:  - The `digest ≈ 12,400 tokens` readout and its "over budget" warning. Both measure what an *agent* reads, in a unit only an agent has — a token count on a human's dashboard is a number nobody can act on. - "Ask Claude to write one — keel_write_doc" → "Ask Claude to write it." - "run `keel fixture` against a scratch store" → gone; an empty store should not answer with a CLI command. - "unattributed", with a tooltip citing decision D-10, → "written outside a tracked session", with a tooltip that says what it means rather than which decision permits it. - The `Id` component's "Click to select, then copy" tooltip. An interface that has to tell you how to use a piece of text is showing you something it should not have had to.  Also: the activity feed no longer prints each event's ULID. The summary already names what changed — "created task 'X'" — so the id was a second identifier for the same thing and the only one a person could not read. And the task page's `Id` row is labelled "Internal id", which at least says why it is a wall of characters.  Verified by sweeping every screen for the offending strings: none left.  **What is left is content, not chrome, and it is real.** The dashboard's open-questions card is a wall of `TQ-8`, `TQ-24`, `Q-4` — and the glossary still defines "Digest" as "budgeted to roughly 3–4k tokens". Those are rows in the store, not interface copy, so this task could not fix them. They are exactly what KEEL-92's question prune and document reset are for, and they are the most conspicuous remaining thing on the screen a human opens. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-84** Make STATUS.md safe to render, and worth reading `p1` · cli, risk, phase6

  RESET-PLAN 6.5 and KB decision 5. `keel render-status --out` overwrites its target unconditionally — no comparison, no safety check, no backup — and resolves the project by name, slug or ID with fuzzy matching. Point it at a near-duplicate project by accident and it silently writes that project's near-empty tracker over the real one. That is a genuine data-loss path. Compare before writing, require an exact project match, and refuse to write a dramatically smaller file without saying so. Then render readable IDs and the "what's next" section into it — the best computation in the system is missing from the file a human actually reads.

  - Done. `render-status --out` can no longer quietly replace a tracker:  - **An ambiguous project reference is refused**, naming the candidates, instead of resolving to whichever matched first. Two projects sharing a *name* cannot exist — near-duplicate detection catches that on create — so the reachable collision is one project matching by slug while another matches by name, and that is what the test uses. - **It compares before writing**, and says "already up to date" rather than rewriting. - **It refuses a render less than half the size of the file it would replace**, naming both sizes and suggesting the project argument is wrong. `--force` overrides. A tracker does not lose half its content by accident. - **`## What's next` is now in the file**, at the top, before the ninety-three rows — the best computation in the system appeared everywhere except the document a human opens.  Found by my own test: the comparison had to strip the generated banner, because the banner carries a generation timestamp. Comparing bytes would have meant "already up to date" never fired and every render dirtied the tree. That is the same rule `keel generate --check` uses; using a different one here is how the two would have disagreed about whether a file had changed. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-83** Write closed_at, and backfill it from the event log `p1` · correctness, storage, phase6

  RESET-PLAN 6.5. `closed_at`, the field that records when a task finished, is never written by any live code path. Every done task in the store has no completion date, so nothing about throughput, cycle time or "what closed this week" is computable. Set it when a task reaches a terminal status, and backfill from the event log for tasks already closed.

  - Confirmed while reconciling the stale rows: `closed_at` accepts a write through keel_update and round-trips fine. So the column and the API are already there — what is missing is only the automatic set on a terminal status transition, plus the backfill. The four rows closed today have it set by hand. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>
  - Done. `closed_at` is written when a task reaches `done` or `wont_do`, cleared when it comes back out, and an explicit date in the same call wins — which is the case that matters for recording a close that happened earlier than the write.  Backfilled from the event log, which had the answer all along: the most recent status change into a terminal state. All 77 closed tasks in the live store now carry a date; before this, none did. A task closed by a path that left no event falls back to `updated_at` — later than the truth but bounded by it, and a date that is roughly right beats a null that makes every question unanswerable.  "What closed this week" is now a question the store can answer, which it could not this morning. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-82** One definition of blocked, and the same numbers everywhere `p1` · correctness, phase6

  RESET-PLAN 6.5. Two parts of the system decide what "blocked" means and they disagree: the generated STATUS.md counts a task as blocked if its status field says `blocked`; the what's-next ranking counts it blocked if something links to it with a `blocks` edge. A task can be one and not the other, and then the file and the app state different facts with nothing noticing. Pick the links as authoritative — they say *what* is blocking — derive the rest, and make fsck fail when a task says `blocked` with nothing linked to it, or is linked-blocked while claiming `todo`. Then make the app, the digest Claude reads and the generated STATUS.md count open, urgent and blocked identically.

  - Done, and KB's answer to TQ-25 made it the stronger version: `blocked` is gone as a status, not merely reconciled with the links.  One function, `next::blocked_tasks`, is now the only definition — a task is blocked when something live links to it with `blocks` — and the ranking, the digest's counts, the generated tracker and the board all call it. Before, three of those computed it themselves and two of them used the status column.  Verified against the live store after migrating: the digest reports `blocked=3`, `next_up.blocked` has exactly those 3, and every one names its blocker. The two phantoms are gone — KEEL-45 and KEEL-48 used to appear as "marked blocked, but nothing links to it with `blocks`", and that sentence no longer exists because the state it described cannot be written down. Migration 8 moved them to `todo`, which is what they always were.  The board keeps a blocked column; it is derived, sits first, and a blocked task appears there *instead of* under its status rather than in both — showing it twice would double every count on the screen.  The test that used to assert the contradiction was reported is now a test that the contradiction cannot occur. That felt like the right trade: the old one guarded a behaviour that only existed because the data model allowed a mistake. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-81** Sub-tasks — a parent link between tasks `p2` · storage, desktop, phase6

  RESET-PLAN 6.4. A parent shows its children with a progress count; a child shows its parent; rollups come from that. Currently impossible: the only edge available is `blocks`, which means "must happen first", not "is part of". A field on the task, not a new artifact type.

  - Done. `tasks.parent_id`, a column rather than an edge — and that choice is the substance of the task.  `blocks` means "must happen first"; composition is a different statement. Modelling "is part of" as a blocking edge is what made rollups impossible, and it would have corrupted the ranking, which reads every inbound `blocks` as something in the way. The detail view now shows both for the same pair: KEEL-92 has KEEL-91 under "Part of — sub-tasks, 0 of 1 done" *and* under "Connected — blocked by". Both true, different things.  Three ways to make an unrenderable tree, all refused on the way in, because the store is the only place that can see the whole chain: a task as its own parent, a cycle of any length, and a parent in another project. Depth is capped at six — deeper than that is a milestone wearing a task's clothes, and a bound is what stops a malformed chain becoming an unbounded walk while something is trying to render it. Two fsck checks audit the same three things for the state a restore can leave.  Found while testing it on real data: `parent_id` took an id but would not take `KEEL-92`, so setting a parent by the readable identifier failed with "no task with id KEEL-92 exists" — about a task that plainly does. A readable identifier is only an identifier if it works everywhere one is taken, so `keel_update` now resolves reference-bearing fields the same way it resolves `id`.  Grouping by parent is in the board and the list, with tasks whose parent is not in the filtered set still grouped under it — narrowing to open work should still say which epic each piece belongs to. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-80** Task rank — a deliberate order, not reverse-creation-order `p2` · storage, desktop, phase6

  RESET-PLAN 6.4. Add a rank to tasks so a column has a deliberate order. Since the app is read-only, rank is set by Claude ("move that above the auth work") and respected by every view; the app can still offer local sort options on top. A field, not a new artifact type.

  - Done. `tasks.rank` is a DOUBLE, backfilled from the task number so the starting order is creation order rather than arbitrary.  The part worth keeping is the surface, not the column. A model should never have to pick a float, so `keel_update` takes `rank_after` or `rank_before` naming another task — `{"rank_after": "KEEL-87"}` — and the store resolves it to the midpoint of that task and its neighbour. Verified on the live store: KEEL-82 moved after KEEL-87 and landed at 87.5.  A float rather than an integer because a move should touch one row. Renumbering touches every row below the insertion point, and on a store where the daemon owns the single write path that is a lot of events for "put this one higher". The known limit is that repeated midpoints between the *same* pair exhaust float precision after roughly fifty inserts; at a few thousand rows hand-ordered by one person that is out of reach, and `rank_between` refuses rather than inventing a value when two neighbours are already equal.  New work lands at the end, not the start: a task nobody has placed has not been prioritised, and putting it on top would be the store making that claim on someone's behalf.  The app: `sort=rank` is the deliberate order and `sort=next` is the digest's ranking. They were briefly the same word, which is exactly the kind of collision nobody reports — a sort that silently means one when you asked for the other. Two tests assert they differ. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-79** Search results that go somewhere `p1` · desktop, phase6

  RESET-PLAN 6.3. Search results are currently dead text — a hit tells you what was found and gives you no way to reach it. Every hit becomes a link to the thing it found.

  - Done. Every hit is a link: tasks to their own page, the five prose types to the document reader, milestones to the roadmap, and everything else to its project. Verified against the live store — a search for "readable identifiers" returns 17 hits, all of them reachable.  The routing needs the project *slug* and a hit carries only `project_id`, so the screen resolves it against the project list it already fetches for the scope menu. Where that cannot resolve, the link goes Home rather than nowhere: landing on the right project is a worse answer than landing on the row and a better one than a dead link, which is what every hit used to be. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-78** Filters that compose and survive, encoded in the URL `p1` · desktop, phase6

  RESET-PLAN 6.3. Today there are exactly two filters, they don't combine, and they vanish on reload. Replace with a proper filter bar: multiple labels, status, priority, kind, milestone, has-blockers, free text. Encode the filter in the URL so a filtered view *is* a shareable link — that gives saved views for free. Also: the ranked Next list is the best thing in the app and it currently disappears the moment any filter is applied. Fix that.

  - Done. `lib/filters.ts` holds the model — status, priority, kind, labels, milestone, has-blockers and free text — and every control writes it to the query rather than to component state, so a filtered view *is* a link and Back undoes a filter change the way it undoes anything else. Bookmarking one is what "saved views" turns out to mean.  Three decisions worth keeping:  **OR within a facet, AND across them.** Any other reading makes "status: todo, blocked" mean nothing.  **A default is written as the absence of a parameter.** An unfiltered board is `#/projects/keel/board`, not a URL trailing four parameters that say "as usual" — otherwise a bookmarked view and a freshly built identical one look like different pages.  **"Has blockers" asks the graph, not the status field.** `blockedIds` comes from the digest's `next_up.blocked`, which is the link-based definition the ranking already uses. The app must not grow a second opinion about what blocked means before KEEL-82 settles it.  Also fixed the regression the plan named: the ranked Next panel used to vanish the moment any filter was applied, so the best thing in the app was only on screen when you did not need it. It now stays, and a test holds it. Migration 6 backfills both: keys de-duplicated by suffix so two projects reducing to the same letters get `KEEL` and `KEEL2` rather than failing the unique index and leaving the store unmigratable, and numbers in ULID order so `KEEL-1` is the oldest task and the sequence reads as the project's history.  Nothing stores the composed string. `KEEL-42` is assembled wherever both halves are in hand, so re-keying a project does not mean rewriting every row that mentions it. The ULID stays the identity — links, events, notes and documents all still point at it.  `resolve_ref` accepts either form, so every tool that takes an id now takes a reference: get, update, write_doc, note, link, and activity's `entity`. Where it shows: the digest's "Next" list, `product/STATUS.md`, board cards, the detail view's title and properties, and the palette — where typing `KEEL-76` finds the task, ranked below every title match so an identifier can never displace a search for real words.  Two things worth knowing:  **A number is never reused, and a test holds it.** If an archived task's number were handed on, `KEEL-1` would keep resolving and quietly start meaning something else — every note and commit message that used it silently wrong. `MAX(number) + 1` counts archived rows.  **The unique index is on `upper(key)`, not `key`.** References resolve case-insensitively, so `KEEL` and `keel` are one identifier; a plain unique index would have allowed both to exist as separate projects and left the lookup picking one arbitrarily. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>

- **KEEL-66** Check that ID references in artifact bodies resolve `p1` · storage, correctness

  Fixed as an fsck check, unresolved_id_reference.
  
  It scans document bodies for B-n, D-n, Q-n, TQ-n, R-n and P0-n style citations and resolves them against every live entity in the same project via v_entities - not just documents, because an artifact created without a body has no document row and the first version missed most real targets that way, reporting 227 dangling refs in a store of ~250 artifacts.

Keel's own B-n decisions live in a numbered table inside a prose document, never as titles, so unscoped every citation of them dangles by construction - 182 findings, none a real break.
  
  Scoped, it found six genuine ones: TQ-12 and TQ-13 cited in three documents after those rows were dropped when the open-questions table was edited. Both restored as answered questions, and fsck is clean.
  
  Honest limitation: it does NOT catch the case that motivated it. The Pellet question citing 'append-only (D-9)' is not caught, because no project titles artifacts D-n - those are SPEC decisions living in prose. A purely lexical check cannot tell 'cites a convention documented in prose' from 'cites nothing'. Catching that needs the prose decision log modelled as artifacts, which is the enrich-vs-collapse question.

- **KEEL-65** Near-duplicate titles defeat the idempotency key `p1` · storage, mcp

  Fixed. create now looks for a near-identical title in the same project and type after the exact key misses, and returns the existing artifact with created:false.
  
  The rule is overlap PLUS containment, and containment is what makes it safe: one token set must be a subset of the other, so the difference can only be added words, never substituted ones. 'Validate phases to 0-360' against '...0-360 degrees' is an addition; 'Open question number 4' against 'number 5' is a substitution.
  
  Overlap alone was wrong and a test caught it: sixty questions differing by one digit scored 0.875 similarity and collapsed into two rows. High overlap says 'mostly the same words'; only containment says 'one adds nothing the other lacks'.
  
  An explicitly supplied idempotency key skips the check entirely - that is the caller asserting two same-titled things are different, and guessing over an explicit assertion is the false merge that hides work. Terms, metric observations and artifacts are excluded, terms because Q-4 requires a global and a project-scoped one of the same name to coexist and the COALESCE index already says so exactly.
  
  Seven tests, including the exact pair the two gate runs produced and the sixty-question case that found the flaw.

  The gate criterion is pure recall and it now reads 18 of 20. That is exactly the condition under which a recall-maximiser scores well while making the store worse, so precision must be measured before anything raising write frequency ships.
  
  Material exists: Runs B and C produced roughly 30 artifacts, recoverable per session from the archived transcripts and stores.
  
  Two findings from my own pass, which is NOT the independent judgement this task needs. Four sessions called create twice with an identical title - idempotency deduplicated them, so REQ-7 worked on organic traffic for the first time, but the retry suggests the success response is not clearly acknowledging the write. And s6 produced three tasks from a prompt about one validation, the first organic instance of R-6 write amplification.
  
  The panel is explicit that this must not be delegated to an LLM judge until roughly 30 sessions are hand-labelled with agreement measured by Cohen's kappa; below about 0.7, don't.

- **KEEL-63** Step 6 — deterministic Stop hook for sessions that recorded nothing `p0` · plugin, gate

  Re-argued against the residual it actually addresses, per TQ-21.\n\nThe justification in WAY-FORWARD.md was the closing-message boundary: an offer is composed after tool-calling has ended, so no amount of 'record it the moment it happens' can reach it. Run A has one offer in ten sessions. That justification is dead.\n\nThe surviving one is better. Three sessions - s2, s7, s9, now confirmed by KB as genuine misses - never noticed Keel at all while heads-down on implementation work. A Stop hook fires exactly when the session knows what became true and the work is finished. It is a forcing function at the only moment that has full information.\n\nDesign, deliberately narrow:\n- No model call. Deterministic text.\n- Guard on stop_hook_active so it can never loop.\n- Fire at most once per session.\n- Fire ONLY if the session recorded nothing. A session that already wrote must not be nagged; that is how a forcing function turns into noise and gets disabled.\n\nThe last point is the one that makes it safe: it is silent for the seven sessions that already do the right thing, and speaks only to the three that did not.

- **KEEL-61** Step 2 — repair the instrument before any further run `p0` · gate, measurement

  From WAY-FORWARD.md Step 2, plus one addition promoted by the Step 1 audit. Do not run another gate until this is done.
  
  From the panel:
  - Known-answer fixtures: ten canned transcripts with zero writes must score 0/10, ten with writes must score 10/10. Run before every real run.
  - Assert writes + silent == sessions_launched, abort on violation.
  - Parallelise, one scratch store per session. Largest power multiplier available and removes DuckDB lock contention.
  - Add one neutral continuation turn per session, e.g. "ok, what about the caching thing" - instrument repair, not treatment, since the single-turn harness is why offers had nowhere to land.
  - Assert the store was reachable for the duration of each session, so a wedged daemon is not scored as a non-write.
  - Archive transcripts, tool lists and event log under a run id. Teardown becomes a separate manual step.
  - Replace the binary score with the L0-L5 ordinal rubric; report recall and ceiling and offer count.
  
  Added by the audit, and it is the one that corrupted the headline number:
  - Inject the session id from the launcher rather than asking the model to mint one. The --sessions fix corrected the denominator but the numerator is still distinct model-minted ids, so a session writing under a colliding id remains invisible. The SessionStart hook already receives the payload and can stamp a unique id per session.
  
  Also free, and it should happen here: the L0-L5 rubric can be scored retrospectively against all 41 archived sessions with no new runs. That is a labelled corpus for Step 10's hand-judging, available before Run A rather than after.

- **KEEL-58** Daemon wedge: SIGKILL mid-write corrupted a DuckDB ART index `p0` · daemon, storage

  The daemon gets into a state where every entity read fails with "count matching rows" / "run a <type> lookup" while the data is provably fine — with the daemon stopped, the CLI reads the store normally and `keel fsck` reports clean across 27 checks.
  
  Seen twice. First after archiving a project. Second later in the same session, after which a restart did *not* immediately clear it: health answered but every list still failed, and only a second restart recovered it. That suggests a race on startup — if the previous process has not released the DuckDB lock, the new one comes up with something half-initialised (the fts_entities rebuild is the prime suspect) and then fails every query that touches it, without logging anything more specific than a 500.
  
  What makes it p0: the failure is total, not partial. An agent mid-session gets storage errors on every call with no indication of the cause, and the obvious remedy — restart — is not reliably enough on its own. Soft delete is supposed to be a routine operation, and D-9 makes it the only kind of deletion this store has.
  
  crates/keel-core/tests/archive.rs covers the store layer and passes, which is what localised this to the daemon rather than the data. Needs: the real DuckDB error surfaced instead of a generic context string, a startup check that the FTS index is usable, and a shutdown that waits for the lock to be released.

- **KEEL-57** Route the remaining read commands through the daemon API `p1` · daemon, cli

  From TQ-15, which found that nothing can read the store while the daemon holds the write lock. `keel generate` goes through `POST /api/generate` and works with the daemon up. `keel fsck`, `keel status` and `keel render-status` still open the store directly and fail with a conflicting-lock error whenever the daemon is running — which is always. `fsck` is the one that matters: an integrity check you cannot run without stopping the thing you want to check is not much of a check. Each needs an endpoint, or one generic read-through. Also correct SPEC D-5, which promises a read-only connection that DuckDB does not offer.

  - Another cost of the same gap, hit today: there is no cheap way to append to a long generated document. `keel_write_doc` takes the full body — correctly, since a revision is a snapshot — so adding one section to the 35 KB JOURNAL.md means retransmitting all of it through the tool. The alternative, hand-editing the file and running `keel import`, needs the daemon stopped, which is the thing this task fixes. The practical effect is that the session narrative gets written as notes on rows instead of into the journal, which is the opposite of what the standing rules ask for. An append endpoint, or `import` working against a running daemon, closes it. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-10</sub>
  - Done. `fsck`, `status` and `render-status` now read through `GET /api/fsck`, `/api/status` and `/api/render-status`, falling back to opening the store directly when no daemon answers — the same shape `generate` has had since B-21.  Verified both ways, and the outputs are identical: with the daemon up (which used to be a conflicting-lock error) and with it stopped.  Two details worth keeping:  **"No daemon answered" and "the daemon said no" are different, and are treated differently.** A connection failure falls back, because nothing holds the lock and opening directly is then correct. An HTTP error is raised with the daemon's own message. Falling back on a real error would have produced a confusing lock error a moment later and hidden the actual cause — which is the failure mode this whole task exists to remove, just relocated.  **The project reference is percent-encoded by hand** rather than adding a crate. A slug or key is safe, but a project *name* can hold a space or an ampersand, and an unencoded one truncates the query and renders a different project's tracker. Silent and wrong, which is the class this codebase treats as the dangerous one.  The tests pin `NO_DAEMON` to a port nothing listens on rather than leaving the default. A test that passes only when the developer's daemon happens to be stopped is one that fails in CI for reasons nobody can see.  SPEC D-5's wording was corrected earlier today under KEEL-92, so the other half of this task was already closed.  **Not routed, deliberately:** `backup`, `restore`, `fixture`, `import` and `bootstrap` still open the store directly. They are writes or whole-store operations, and the hard constraint is that the daemon owns the single write path — giving them endpoints would mean write endpoints on the daemon, which is exactly what the read-only app decision forbids. They are also all commands a person runs deliberately, with the daemon stopped, which is the documented flow for `import`. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-55** Route reads through the daemon: POST /api/generate `p1` · daemon, cli

  Found by using it: nothing could read the store while the daemon held DuckDB's write lock. Tried the obvious fix first — a read-only connection — and it fails identically, because DuckDB blocks readers too while any writer holds the file. So D-5's "connect read-only" is not a thing that exists, and generation moved into the daemon, which owns the store. The CLI is a client, falling back to a direct open only when no daemon answers, which is safe precisely because nothing else holds the lock then. The spec correction is flagged as TQ-15, because the read-only connection it promises does not exist.

  - **Found by using it:** nothing could read the store while the daemon held the write lock. Tried the obvious fix first — a read-only connection — and it fails identically, because DuckDB blocks readers too. - **KEEL-47** Metric observations charted against target `p3` · desktop, metrics

  The data model is done; this is the rendering.

  - Done. Screen 8, `/projects/:project/metrics`.  The data model has existed since Phase 0 and nothing ever showed it — the same dead weight the event log was before the task page: recorded faithfully, readable by nobody.  Each metric gets its latest reading, its target, and a line chart of every observation with the target drawn as a dashed line.  **Direction is the part worth having built carefully.** A metric whose target is to go *down* — agent orientation cost, error rate — reads exactly backwards if you assume higher is better. `Agent orientation cost` at 2075 against a target of 4000 renders green, and would render red under the naive reading. Every card says "higher is better" or "lower is better" out loud, because a lone number plus a target is still ambiguous without it.  Two smaller things the chart gets right:  - **The y-axis always includes the target**, even when every reading sits far from it. Scaling to the data alone can put the target off-screen, which hides the one comparison the chart exists to make. - **A flat series gets an artificial range**, or the line renders flush against an edge and reads as a boundary rather than a value.  Hand-drawn SVG, no charting dependency — B-14's reasoning, applied again: this needs a polyline, a dashed line and a few labels.  Four tests, and the one that matters asserts a falling metric does *not* render green. The bug it guards against is a dashboard saying a project is on track when it is not, which is worse than no dashboard.  Found while verifying: `Tests passing` still read 270 against a target of 270 because the last observation was from the bootstrap. Recorded a current one — 425 — which is the loop working as intended rather than a fix. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>
