### wont_do (6)

- **KEEL-191** Nothing watches the write-ahead log, and doctor alone would not fix that `p3` · observability, storage

  `wal_pages()` in `store/mod.rs` is documented as "the number that says whether checkpointing is keeping up... a figure that only ever climbs means something is holding a read snapshot open". Nothing in production calls it. The only callers are `tests/wal_growth.rs`.
  
  ## Why this is worth a row
  
  SQLite cannot checkpoint past the oldest open read snapshot. The daemon runs for days and holds a server-sent-events connection open the whole time, so a reader that never releases its snapshot pins the log and every write after that appends to it forever.
  
  Nothing errors. Every read still answers, correctly, out of the log. The only symptom is a `-wal` file quietly larger than the database beside it. `PRAGMA wal_autocheckpoint = 1000` handles the ordinary case and the workspace denies `await_holding_lock` to stop the most likely cause, but neither tells you when the assumption has broken.
  
  ## The part that makes this more than a one-liner
  
  The obvious home is `keel doctor`, and doctor is the wrong home *on its own*.
  
  Doctor is never invoked automatically. Not by CI, which runs fmt, clippy, test, insta, deny, coverage, mutants and fuzz. Not by the plugin's SessionStart or Stop hooks. Not by `scripts/pre-commit`. Every mention of `keel doctor` in the tree is advice inside an error message — the advisory lock's refusal, the daemon's exit-without-restart — telling a person to run it once something has already gone visibly wrong.
  
  So doctor is reactive, and this failure has no symptom to react to. A check that only runs when someone already suspects a problem cannot catch the one failure defined by nobody suspecting anything.
  
  ## What to do
  
  Both halves, and the second is the one that matters:
  
  1. **A check in doctor.** Cheap, and doctor is where a person looks once they are worried. `wal_pages()` against the 1,000-page threshold, `Level::Problem` well above it — a log at several times the threshold is not a busy moment, it is a pinned snapshot. Note that `wal_pages()` runs a `PASSIVE` checkpoint to measure, so asking is also a nudge.
  
  2. **Something that runs unprompted.** The daemon is the natural place: it is the long-running process, it is the one whose SSE connection causes the problem, and it already warns at startup about store-location hazards. A periodic `wal_pages()` with a `tracing::warn!` past a threshold would mean the condition announces itself instead of waiting to be asked about.
  
  Worth deciding whether the daemon check is periodic or only at shutdown alongside the existing `checkpoint()`. Periodic is more useful and is a background timer, which the scale-discipline rule says needs an argument — this is that argument, but KB should agree to it.

  - Measured, and the result overturns the recommendation in the body above. Do not use a periodic `wal_pages()`.  `wal_pages()` is not a read. It runs `PRAGMA wal_checkpoint(PASSIVE)`, which does real I/O. On a 606-page / 2.4 MB log, close to what the live store carries:  ``` PASSIVE checkpoint: 7 ms   (0|606|606 — all 606 pages moved) stat() of the -wal: 1.32 µs ```  Roughly 5,300× apart. Three reasons a periodic `wal_pages()` is wrong, in increasing order of weight:  1. 7 ms of work on the write path, not a counter read. 2. It would quietly replace the checkpoint policy. `wal_autocheckpoint = 1000` is set deliberately; calling PASSIVE every ten writes means the log never reaches 1,000 pages, so the monitor changes what it monitors. That is the mirror-hook mistake in another costume. 3. It would not help anyway. When a snapshot is pinned, checkpoints are *already* running and failing to advance. More attempts fail more often. What is missing is noticing, not attempting.  ## The catch that makes this two-stage rather than one  A PASSIVE checkpoint does not shrink the file. Measured:  ``` before checkpoint: 2496752 bytes PASSIVE -> 0|606|606 after  PASSIVE:    2496752 bytes    <- unchanged after  TRUNCATE:   0 bytes ```  So `stat` reports the **high-water mark**, not what is currently unbackfilled, and `wal_pages()` reports 0 in that same state. The cheap call is the one that lies. A first draft of this said "stat with hysteresis, warn once and reset when it drops back" — that cannot work, because it never drops back, so one busy burst would latch the warning on forever.  ## What to build  Stat as a gate, pragma as the confirmation:  1. `stat` the `-wal` — 1.32 µs, and this is the path taken essentially always. Under threshold, stop. 2. Only if it is large, call `wal_pages()` — 7 ms, and it returns the truth. Also high means a real pinned snapshot; low means the file is a high-water mark from a past burst, so stay quiet.  Correct, near-free, and self-clearing because stage two produces a number that does come back down. The 7 ms is then only paid when the log is genuinely large, which is when a checkpoint attempt is wanted regardless.  Frame arithmetic, for whoever sets the threshold: a frame is 4096 + 24 bytes and the file carries a 32-byte header, so pages = (bytes - 32) / 4120. Confirmed exactly against the 606-page measurement above.  KB asked whether every 10–15 writes was a sensible cadence. It is — at 1.32 µs stage one could run on every write and not be measurable. The cadence was never the problem; the call was. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>
  - `PRAGMA journal_size_limit` is not set, and setting it makes most of this task simpler. KB asked whether transactions already handle it; they do not, and the reason is worth writing down because the two get conflated.  **Commit is not checkpoint.** Every write here is already in a transaction — `conn.transaction()` at eight sites across `entity.rs`, `docs.rs`, `composite.rs` and `mod.rs` — and a commit in WAL mode is durable the moment it lands in the `-wal`. Readers see it immediately. Nothing about this is a persistence or correctness question. Checkpointing is the separate, later step that moves those already-committed frames back into the `.db`, and the growth problem lives entirely downstream of commit. That is why transactional discipline cannot touch it.  **What `journal_size_limit` does.** Measured, with the limit set to 1 MB:  ``` 1. after big write:   2,496,752 bytes 2. after PASSIVE:     2,496,752 bytes   <- a checkpoint alone never truncates 3. after next write:  1,048,576 bytes   <- reset to the cap ```  The WAL resets and truncates on the **next write after a successful full checkpoint**, not at checkpoint time. The earlier note in this thread concluded the file never comes back down; that was checking one step too early.  **Why it matters here.** With the limit set, the file size becomes honest — it comes back down — so the two-stage gate that note recommends collapses into one plain `stat`, and the hysteresis that could not work now can.  **And it sharpens the signal rather than removing the need for one.** The limit only applies when a checkpoint completes. A pinned reader snapshot stops the checkpoint completing, so the WAL never resets and the limit never applies. So with `journal_size_limit` set, a `-wal` above the limit means precisely "checkpointing is not completing" — which is the condition this task exists to catch, stated directly, instead of inferred from a high-water mark that might just be a busy afternoon.  Suggested shape, for whoever picks this up: set `journal_size_limit` in `configure()` beside `wal_autocheckpoint`, with a comment saying the two are a pair — one decides when to fold the log back, the other decides how much file to keep afterwards. Then the check is a `stat` against that limit, and the pragma call is only needed to confirm. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>
  - KB proposed a background loop calling `SQLITE_CHECKPOINT_PASSIVE` on a small delay. Measured: it cannot repair this failure, and it is the ideal place to detect it. Both halves matter.  Two connections, the second holding an open read snapshot:  ``` baseline, no reader:    (busy=0, log=304, checkpointed=304)   all moved with reader pinned:     (busy=0, log=304, checkpointed=0)     nothing moved   attempt 2:            (busy=0, log=304, checkpointed=0)   attempt 3:            (busy=0, log=304, checkpointed=0) after reader releases:  (busy=0, log=304, checkpointed=304)   all moved ```  **Retrying does not help.** The oldest reader's snapshot is a hard wall — PASSIVE copies frames up to it and no further, and when that snapshot predates every unbackfilled frame it copies nothing. A loop does nothing more often. It is also mostly redundant while writes are flowing, since `wal_autocheckpoint = 1000` already fires PASSIVE after qualifying commits; the only real gap it fills is the idle case, where writes have stopped so autocheckpoint cannot fire.  **`busy` stays 0 the whole time.** This is the trap. The obvious column to check is `busy`, and a monitor written against it would never fire — it reads as success while zero pages move. Check `checkpointed`. A loop that ignores the return value is a mechanism that looks like checkpointing is handled while nothing is checkpointed, which is the mirror-hook shape and the reason that hook was deleted rather than repaired.  **Which makes the loop the detector.** It already computes the proof: `log` high and `checkpointed == 0` across N consecutive passes is a pinned snapshot, stated directly rather than inferred from a file size that might be a busy afternoon. That is a sharper signal than either the `stat` or the `journal_size_limit` approach in the notes above.  Recommended shape now, superseding both earlier sketches:  1. `journal_size_limit` in `configure()`, so the file bounds itself in the healthy case. 2. A periodic PASSIVE — cheap, never blocks, and folds the log back when the store is idle. 3. Warn when `log` stays high and `checkpointed` stays 0 across several passes.  Two constraints on the implementation. A background timer is exactly what the scale-discipline rule says needs KB's agreement first, so this is the argument and he should confirm it. And the loop must not hold the `Mutex<Store>` across its sleep: `await_holding_lock = "deny"` is in the workspace lints because a guard held across a suspension pins a read snapshot, which would make the watcher the cause of the thing it watches for.  Source note: the page KB linked (runebook.dev, a third-party mirror) states PASSIVE "will return SQLITE_BUSY immediately if it can't proceed" and runs "only if no other writers or readers are active". Both are wrong, and the measurement above is what disproves them. SQLite's own wording is "checkpoint as many frames as possible without waiting" and "the busy-handler callback is never invoked in this mode." <sub>— claude · 2026-08-14</sub>

- **KEEL-138** generate and the pre-commit check both ignore which worktree you are in `p2` · bug, cli, tooling

  Hit while closing KEEL-133 from a worktree. `keel generate keel` reported five files written and `git status` in the worktree was clean — the writes had landed in the main checkout, on the `phase-9-one-database` branch, including a 649-line `product/SPEC.md` belonging to a concurrent session's work. They were reverted by hand.
  
  `generate` takes `--repo`, so the write side has a workaround. The check side does not: `scripts/pre-commit` runs `"$keel_bin" generate "$project" --check` with no `--repo`, and there is nowhere to pass one. So a commit made in a worktree is refused or allowed on the strength of a different tree's files.
  
  Two candidate fixes, and they compose: default the repository root to `git rev-parse --show-toplevel` when the command is run inside the project's repository, and have the hook pass the tree it is committing. Note `--show-toplevel` is the worktree root, which is what is wanted here.
  
  Worth pairing with the observation that two sessions rendering one live store onto two branches will conflict in `product/STATUS.md` whatever happens, because each renders the store as it stood when that session ran. Regenerating once after the merge is the answer to that, not a change to `generate`.

- **KEEL-114** 8B — File an issue from the app, with a pasted screenshot `p1` · desktop, daemon, phase8, 8b

  A New issue button, reachable anywhere with C. Title, markdown body, optional labels and priority. Paste or drop images. Paste URLs, which become typed entries in `external_refs` now that it is a list. Creates a task in `triage`.
  
  What it cannot do: change a status, change priority after filing, close anything, edit an existing task, write a spec or decision or question, delete. The app captures; Claude manages.
  
  One mutating route, `POST /api/intake`, multipart, attributed `actor: human`, `surface: ui`. The second non-GET route after `/api/generate`.
  
  This is the path with no size problem. Bytes go straight to the daemon and no model is involved, so a 2 MB retina screenshot is fine here when it would cost 350,000 to 450,000 output tokens through a tool call.
  
  Blocked on TQ-30: this contradicts hard constraint 7 in product/CLAUDE.md, which says the desktop app is read-only, no write endpoints on the daemon for it, no forms in it. All three clauses. That needs KB to amend the constraint before this is built.
  
  Also depends on the triage status existing.
  
  Done when filing a bug with a pasted screenshot, from a cold start, takes under 30 seconds on KB's own stopwatch. That is a Phase 8 exit criterion.

  - Not being built. KB's call, 2026-08-11: "let's skip the ability to create a new issue at this time." TQ-30 carries the reasoning.  Moved out of Phase 8 rather than closed. This is "not now", not "won't do" — the argument in §8B is sound and the constraint was never amended, so nothing has to be reversed if it comes back.  Two consequences worth having on the record:  **Phase 8 cannot claim its 30-second stopwatch criterion.** Filing a bug with a pasted screenshot from a cold start is the thing that criterion measured, and it does not exist.  **Large images still have no route in from a chat surface.** The app path was the only one with no size problem, since bytes go straight to the daemon with no model in between. Claude Code is covered by the file-path attach settled in TQ-33; a session with no filesystem is not. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-111** 8A — A triage status, ahead of todo `p1` · storage, phase8, 8a

  One new value on the task status enum, before `todo`. Issues filed from the app land there. It means "a human threw this in, unrefined", and `keel ready` skips it. Without it, everything you file competes with planned work on equal footing, which is what stops the file-rough-and-sort-later loop from working.
  
  The spec calls this a seventh value. It is the sixth. enums.rs:136 declares five: todo, in_progress, review, done, wont_do. There is no `blocked` any more, removed in KEEL-82 and settled in TQ-25.
  
  Separately, and worth fixing at the same time: product/CLAUDE.md tells sessions "Never delete a task. Mark it `dropped` with a reason." There is no `dropped` status. A session following that instruction gets an enum rejection. The intended value is `wont_do`. That line is in the contract loaded into every session.
  
  Waiting on TQ-32, which asks whether a holding pen for unrefined work earns a sixth value on the status enum.
  
  Done when a triage task is excluded from `keel ready` and from the digest's open count by construction, the board shows a triage column, the migration is additive with no existing row changed, and the CLAUDE.md line is corrected.

  - Not being built. KB's call, 2026-08-11, following from TQ-30: with app filing declined, nothing files in a hurry, so a holding pen has nothing to hold. TQ-32 carries the reasoning.  Moved out of Phase 8 rather than closed — it comes back with app filing if that does.  **The `dropped` correction does not depend on this and should still happen.** `product/CLAUDE.md` tells every session "Never delete a task. Mark it `dropped` with a reason", and there is no such status. A session following that instruction gets an enum rejection listing five values, none of them the word. The intended value is `wont_do`. That line is in the contract loaded into every session and has been failing silently for as long as it has existed. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-48** Deployable daemon with auth `p3` · remote

  Blocked: needs hosting and auth decisions from KB.

  - Dropped by KB, 2026-08-11, after asking for it in plain English.  The daemon binds `127.0.0.1` and has no password because it has no door — the network stack refuses everyone else. "Deployable with auth" would mean running it somewhere reachable and adding a token so not just anyone who finds the address can read the store.  What it would buy: Keel from a second machine, from Claude on the web or a phone, or by another person. None of which is a thing KB has asked for.  What it costs: TLS, token rotation, backups that were not needed before, and losing the ability to run `keel fsck` against a local file. Every "one user, a few thousand rows, do not optimise" decision in this codebase — no caches, no pools, no queues — was made for a local tool.  The deciding argument: GitHub was the only component that genuinely needed a public address, and it was dropped in the same conversation. Nothing now wants to be reachable from outside.  The real trigger to revisit is wanting Keel on a second machine. If that happens it comes back with an actual requirement, and it is worth noting that syncing a file is a much smaller problem than hosting a service — `~/.keel` is already a git repository. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

- **KEEL-45** GitHub App and webhook receiver `p2` · github

  Blocked: registering the App needs KB's GitHub account and credentials.

  - Dropped by KB, 2026-08-11.  The integration was specified for a world this project does not live in. There is no git remote — `git remote -v` is empty — so there is no repository for a webhook to fire about, no pull requests to parse `Closes KEEL-42` out of, and no merges to propose a status change from. SPEC §9 describes decorating a PR flow that does not exist.  It was also the only thing in the system that wanted a public address, which is what made KEEL-48 look necessary. Dropping this dropped that too.  What would bring it back: pushing this repository somewhere, or Keel being used on a project that already has a PR flow. At that point it returns as a fresh task with a real requirement behind it, rather than a specified feature waiting for a world to arrive.  Kept: `crates/keel-github` stays as a six-line stub and SPEC §9 stays as written. Neither costs anything, and deleting the spec section would lose the design — the `resolves` link, propose-don't-auto-close, commits on the timeline — which was argued through properly and is right whenever it becomes relevant. <sub>— claude · 2026-08-11</sub>

| TQ-1 — Are requirement anchors (REQ-4) parsed from markdown by convention, or declared in… | question | — |

## Decisions

- **The repository stays private and macOS builds run on a self-hosted runner** — `accepted`
- **The installer refuses a download it cannot verify, rather than skipping the check** — `accepted`
- **One package owns both binaries, because dist builds one installer per package** — `accepted`
- **Serving a read-only page does not touch hard constraint 7, the repo stays private for now, and the package becomes keel** — `accepted`
- **Mutation testing comes out of CI until there is traction worth protecting** — `accepted`
- **Phase 10 runs after Phase 11, drops Windows, and plans for a release cadence that starts fast and slows down** — `accepted`
- **Updates apply themselves when compatible, and stop and ask across a schema change** — `accepted`
- **Keel ships as a product: Apache-2.0, 0.x, and the Claude Code plugin as the front door** — `accepted`
- **The write-ahead log stays on SQLite's defaults, unwatched** — `accepted`
- **The keel-github stub comes out of the tree; SPEC §1.1 stays as the intended layout** — `accepted`
- **Spec decisions that outlive their reasoning are annotated, not rewritten** — `accepted`
- **Explicit writes only, and adopting an existing project is a two-layer backfill** — `accepted`
- **Say what the write path actually protects, and put an advisory lock on the store** — `accepted`
- **A changed model is an ordinary re-embed, because search refuses to mix models at all** — `accepted`
- **Leaked test stores get a working sweeper, not a redirected TMPDIR** — `accepted`
- **A phase's state is derived; only shipped, cut and paused are declared** — `accepted`
- **Superseded decisions stay in search results and carry a label saying what replaced them** — `accepted`
- **Documents are embedded as passages, and the passage table is an index rather than a record** — `accepted`
- **The fixture corpus stays compiled in, ungated** — `accepted`
- **The write-path atomicity fix: &Connection primitives, transaction-of-one, one typed composite on Store** — `proposed`
- **Taking the payload out of a tool result is one named function, not two lines** — `accepted`
- **Phase 9 runs before Phase 10, and DuckDB and Lance come out of the tree entirely** — `accepted`
- **A glossary term can declare which type it is a spelling of** — `accepted`
- **Reading an image off the disk is a field, not a fourteenth tool** — `accepted`
- **A claim is optimistic concurrency, not a lock** — `accepted`
- **The close reason is a column, and closing is checked on the transition** — `accepted`
- **The plain-English rule covers every prose field, not just milestone summaries** — `accepted`
- **Every milestone carries a plain-English explainer, required at creation** — `accepted`
- **A missing readable number is read as unassigned, not as an error** — `proposed`
- **An accepted decision can be corrected; the revision chain is the guard** — `proposed`
- **"Waiting on a human decision" is the decision-needed label, not a new task kind** — `proposed`
- **A task marked blocked with no blocks edge is reported as a data problem, not ranked** — `superseded`
- **keel_context answers "what next" with named, ranked tasks** — `proposed`
- **KB confirmed: blocked is derived from the links, not a status** — `accepted`
- **KB confirmed: a task holds a list of external links** — `accepted`
- **Readable identifiers are composed, never stored** — `accepted`
- **The Tauri shell is suspended; the web build is the surface** — `accepted`
- **Graph traversal carries the neighbour's label** — `accepted`
- **The desktop app gets Vitest and Testing Library** — `accepted`
- **The light scheme overrides :root, not a second @theme block** — `accepted`
- **Eight named type sizes in two scales, not eleven anonymous ones** — `accepted`
- **The desktop app routes on the hash, and the router is hand-written** — `accepted`
- **KB confirmed: BM25 stays in DuckDB, Lance does vectors only** — `accepted`, 2026-08-10
- **KB confirmed: idempotency keys stay on all thirteen tables** — `accepted`, 2026-08-10
- **restore now re-establishes the store's git repository** — `accepted`, 2026-08-10
- **The p0 wedge was SIGKILL mid-write, not the FTS index** — `accepted`, 2026-08-10
- **Phase 2 closed on 18 of 20** — `accepted`, 2026-08-10
- **s2, s7 and s9 are genuine misses, not L0** — `accepted`, 2026-08-10
- **Outside panel: the gate measurement was invalid and the file plan is rejected** — `proposed`
- **Build scope is Phases 0–3; git stays local; session_id is skill-minted** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **projects gets a nullable status_path; a path claimed twice is reported, not resolved** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **Generation runs inside the daemon, exposed as POST /api/generate.** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **Keel is the source of truth; every product/*.md is generated.** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **The document reader renders markdown with react-markdown, mapping every element by hand** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **keel import is a bridge, not a migration: re-importable and content-addressed** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **Dev builds use line-tables-only debug info, and the clippy gate drops --all-features** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **Surface carries five values, not four: chat, cowork, code, ui, cli** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **missing_docs is a workspace lint, not just a keel-core convention** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **unwrap/expect/panic/todo/unimplemented are workspace clippy lints, promoted to errors by CI** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **No vector or FTS index on the Lance dataset initially — brute-force scan** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **Event summaries name artifacts, not ids** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **Keel's local REST API has more endpoints than the MCP surface has tools** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **The desktop app hand-writes its components** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **Fixture links are addressed by name, never by position** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **Tool responses lift version to the top of the entity** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **Serve MCP 2025-11-25 alongside 2026-07-28** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **BM25 moves from Lance to DuckDB** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **Every table gets idempotency_key, not just tasks** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **ULIDs are minted from a monotonic generator** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **Bundled DuckDB is a feature, not a requirement** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **All Lance access goes through the DuckDB extension** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
- **chrono for time, not jiff** — `accepted`, 2026-08-09
